# Changelog

All notable changes to ForgeC2 will be documented in this file.

The format is based on [Keep a Changelog](https://keepachangelog.com/en/1.1.0/).

## [Unreleased]

### Fixed
- **The audit export has a byte cap, because buffering made `?limit=` a memory request.** Round 8 buffered the exports so a `200` is always a complete document; that means the buffer *is* the bound, and the audit export accepts `?limit=` up to **200000** rows where a row carries details, error text and two hashes — one request could ask the single-writer box for hundreds of megabytes. Rows now stop being appended at **`auditExportMaxBytes` (64 MiB)** and the response reports it through the same `X-Audit-Export-Truncated` header the row limit uses, so the honesty rule stays single-valued: either the client receives everything, or it is told it did not. A half-written row is impossible because the check happens **before** encoding each row. **Evidence:** `TestAuditExportStopsAtTheByteCap` sets the cap to 200 bytes over 20 padded rows and requires `truncated: true`, **at least one** row emitted, **fewer than 20**, and — the property that matters for verifiability — **every emitted line still parses as a complete JSON row**.
- **Neither export truncates silently any more, and both filenames carry a timestamp.** A capped export is byte-for-byte indistinguishable from a complete one, which for the audit chain is worse than for most downloads: an investigator verifying a chain has no way to know part of it was left behind. Both exports now answer **`X-Audit-Export-Truncated`** / **`X-Task-Export-Truncated`** (`true` when the row count reached the limit); the audit export already reported its row count, the task export now reports truncation next to it. The audit filename also gained the same time convention the task export got last round (`forgec2-audit-chain_2026-10-28_190613.ndjson`, and `…_filtered_…` for a filtered selection), because two downloads in a day previously collided in the browser and the second replaced the first — while "filtered" stays in the name, since that is the property an investigator must not lose. **Evidence:** `TestAuditExportReportsTruncation` requires `false` for an unlimited export and `true` with `X-Audit-Export-Rows: 2` when three rows are exported with `?limit=2`; `TestTaskExportReportsTruncation` requires `true` for `?limit=1` over three tasks and a body of header + **one** row; the earlier `TestTaskExportIsSentWhole` now also pins `false` on the uncapped path. **A process note:** the first attempt at the task-export header silently did nothing (the surrounding text had moved in round 8), and the tests caught it — a string replacement that reports no error is not a change.
- **The task CSV export is now built whole before anything is written, so a failed encode is a 500 instead of a truncated 200.** The handler streamed its CSV straight into the response with a comment admitting the consequence — *"Headers are already sent at this point, so a write failure can only be logged, not turned into a 500"*. A partially written CSV that still parses is worse than an error, because the recipient cannot tell it is short; and the client never got a `Content-Length`, so it could not check either. Exporting is capped at `ExportTaskLimit` rows that are already loaded into memory, so buffering costs nothing: the CSV is encoded into a `bytes.Buffer`, a flush error becomes `handleQueryError` (5xx, with the `ETag`/`Cache-Control` headers cleared so a failed selection is not cached), and only then are the headers, the audit record and the body emitted — which also fixes the **audit record being written before the export could still fail**. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportIsSentWhole` seeds three tasks (one with a comma in its result) and requires a `Content-Length` header, `text/csv`, a body that parses as **4** records (header + 3), the expected header row, and **7 fields per row** — proving the comma was quoted rather than splitting a column.
- **Fixed the flake that hid the suite's real state for many rounds: a test was closing the process's stdout.** `TestSetupLogRotationWritesAFile` closes every sink the fanout writer holds, and `os.Stdout` implements `io.Closer` — so its cleanup closed the **test binary's stdout**. From then on the framework's final `PASS` line could never be written, `go test` reported `FAIL` with **no failing test named**, and the verbose log simply stopped after that test. That is exactly how this "intermittent" failure read for many rounds (about one run in six, finishing earlier than a green run, output ending mid-test); it only bit when the test ran last. The cleanup now skips `os.Stdout`/`os.Stderr` and closes only sinks it owns.
- **Fixed a regression from round 243 that the above had been masking: a rate-limited rerun answered 400 instead of 429.** Extracting `rerunTaskAsNew` for the bulk form collapsed the error into a rendered string, so `handleRerunTask` answered `400 {"error":"rerun failed"}` and `respondTaskError` — the function that maps a pending-task limit to **429** — was bypassed. The helper now returns the `error` and lets each caller map it: the single-task path calls `respondTaskError` again, the bulk path renders it as a skip reason. `TestRerunMapsLimitTo429` had been failing the whole time; it was invisible because the stdout bug truncated the report and because round 243's verification ran `-run 'TestHandleRerun|TestDispatchTask|TestCancel'`, which does not match that name — **a targeted `-run` pattern is not a suite**. Evidence: that test failed alone with `expected 429, got 400` and passes now; the full `./internal/server` suite exits 0.
- **Task attribution was written twice, and the second write failed silently.** Both manual dispatch and the WebRTC start set `task.CreatedBy` in memory and then persisted it with `s.db.Model(task).Update("created_by", username)` — **error discarded**. The audit record reads the in-memory value, so the audit trail stayed correct while the **row** could silently say "creator unknown" — the one field an investigation asks for. The pattern is now a single `persistTaskCreator` (one implementation instead of two copies, the shape round 234 went after) that **logs** a failed write with task id, creator and cause, and treats an empty creator as a no-op rather than an `UPDATE … ""`. **Evidence:** the new test writes and re-reads the row, asserts an empty creator does not overwrite it, then **closes the connection pool** to force the failure path and requires the `Failed to persist task creator` log line — reverting to `_ = … Update(...)` makes it fail with `a failed attribution write was silent`.
- **The server error reference never reached the operator.** Rounds 238/239 made every 5xx carry an `X-Error-Ref` id and a log line tagged with it, but `api.ts` built `ApiError` from the body's `error` field alone — so the id existed in a response header nobody read, and the toast still said "Query failed" with nothing to quote. The client now carries it: `withErrorRef(message, ref)` appends `(ref abc123)` (and no-ops without one), and `ApiError` gained an optional `ref` field so callers can use the id programmatically (support copy, "copy details"). **Evidence:** four new tests cover the suffix and the field, including the 4xx case; the existing **25** `api.test.ts` cases still pass, the full frontend suite is **188 files / 1004 tests** green, and all **29 gates** plus `tsc`/`eslint` are clean. The chain is now closed end to end: a handler logs the cause under a reference, `respondError` guarantees the header, `api.ts` surfaces it in the message, and the operator can quote it.
- **Only the query helper produced an error reference; every other 5xx was unquotable.** Round 238 gave `handleQueryError`'s 74 call sites a 6-character reference in `X-Error-Ref`, but any other 500 — a sanitized plugin failure, a listener that could not bind, a WebRTC setup error — answered a message with **nothing an operator could quote** and no guaranteed log line tying the response to a route. The central `respondError` now does it for **every** 5xx: it reuses an existing reference (so the id the cause was logged under is the id the operator sees), otherwise draws one, and writes one `request failed` line with the **route, status and sanitized message**. A **4xx deliberately gets neither** — it is a client mistake, and referencing every 400 would make the id meaningless. **Evidence:** the new test asserts a 500 gains a 6-hex reference and exactly one log line containing it plus the route and the message; a 400 gains no reference and logs nothing; and a reference set by `handleQueryError` is **reused, not replaced**. The full server suite passes (**136 s**) and the envelope ratchets are unchanged.
- **"Query failed" named neither the endpoint nor the log line.** `handleQueryError` backs **74 call sites**: it logged the full error and answered the literal `"Query failed"`, so an operator's report of "I got Query failed" pointed at one of 74 places and one of thousands of log lines. Each failure now carries a **6-character reference** in an `X-Error-Ref` header, and the **same reference plus the route** appears in the log — so a report can be matched to one line. The reference goes in a **header rather than the message** on purpose: the body stays a literal (so `check:error-sanitization` and the envelope ratchets are unaffected) and the UI can ignore it. References come from `crypto/rand` with a timestamp fallback, so a request never fails because an id could not be drawn. **Evidence:** the new test asserts the header is 6 hex characters, the body still says exactly `Query failed` and does **not** contain the raw error (`tenant_id`), the log line carries the matching reference, the route and the cause, and two failures never share a reference.
- **The automation webhook ran in a bare goroutine, so a panic there took the whole server down.** `ActionWebhook` dispatched with `go s.executeWebhook(params, evt)` and no `defer recover()` — while the notification dispatcher **deliberately** guards its delivery goroutine ("Notification route panicked"). The webhook path does JSON marshalling, header construction from operator-supplied params and client setup, in a goroutine nobody can catch. It now recovers and logs the panic with a stack, matching the sibling. **Also recorded, unfixed:** one run of the `internal/server` suite failed in ~6 attempts today and I could not reproduce it — **nine consecutive runs are green** afterwards (121–127 s each) and the failing run was shorter, suggesting an early abort rather than a slow test. The failure's output was lost to my own output filter, so a background loop now writes **full** output to `%TEMP%\server-flake-capture.txt` and stops at the first failure; the next occurrence will be diagnosable instead of guessed at.
- **The full suite caught a test this series had made flaky: `route-imports.test.ts` timed out under `npm run test`.** Round 213 added a test that `await`s the lazy import for **every** page module to prove each id resolves to a promise with a default export. In isolation that takes 1–2 s; under the full parallel suite the dynamic imports pull the large vendor chunks and it exceeded the **5 s default timeout** — so the guard was green when run alone and red when run the way CI runs it, which is the worst kind of test. It now has a **30 s** budget and a comment saying why. **Evidence:** the full frontend suite went from `1 failed | 186 passed (187)` to **`187 files / 1000 tests` green**, and the same round certifies the backend: the complete CI package list (config, crypto, db, malleable, obfuscation, payload, payload/agent, plugin, scripting, server, middleware, pkg, cmd) passes, `server` in **165 s**; all **28 gates** pass; `tsc` and `eslint` are clean.
- **`aiSSRFClient` had the same zero-timeout hole as `ssrfSafeClient`, one round after the other was closed.** It copies `base.Timeout` and falls back to `http.DefaultClient`, whose `Timeout` is zero — so the nil/zero case produced an AI endpoint client that waits **forever** on a hung provider. The team knew the hazard: `handlers_identity.go` carries the comment "http.DefaultClient has no timeout, so a hung IdP would pin the handler" — but this copy never got the guard. A zero timeout now becomes `HTTPClientLongTimeout` (5 min) rather than the short bound used for webhooks, because AI calls are legitimately slow but still need an end; explicit timeouts are preserved. **Evidence:** the new test asserts `nil → 5 min`, `zero → 5 min`, `45 s → 45 s`, and with the fallback disabled it fails with `Timeout = 0s, want 5m0s`. This is the second copy of the same construct — a grep for `base = http.DefaultClient` now finds only these two, both guarded.
- **`ssrfSafeClient` could hand out a client that waits forever.** It copies `base.Timeout` verbatim, and its nil fallback is `http.DefaultClient`, whose `Timeout` is **zero — no timeout at all**. Production `New()` does set one (30 s short, 5 min long), so this was the only route to an unbounded request: the nil/zero case, which is exactly what a hand-built server or a future call site without a client would hit, and a blackholed webhook or stager host looks like a successfully opened socket that never answers. A zero timeout now becomes `HTTPClientShortTimeout`; explicit timeouts (5 min downloads, 90 s feature-specific ones) are **preserved**, because the fallback only fires when the value is unset. **Evidence:** the new test asserts `nil → 30 s`, `zero → 30 s`, `5 min → 5 min`, `90 s → 90 s`, and with the fallback disabled it fails with `Timeout = 0s, want 30s` for the first two cases; a second test keeps the SSRF property honest by requiring a redirect to `169.254.169.254` to be refused.
- **The Discord external-C2 sender slept for as long as the server told it to, and a shutdown could not stop it.** A 429 carries `retry_after`, which was fed straight into `time.Sleep`: `retry_after: 1e9` would have pinned the sender goroutine for **277 777 hours** (ℹ the test prints exactly that), a missing or `NaN` value spun immediately, and because it was a plain sleep the `stopCh` was ignored for the whole duration. The delay now goes through `discordRetryDelay`, which maps a missing/negative/`NaN`/`Inf` value to **1 s** and clamps anything larger than **5 minutes**, and the wait itself is a `select` on `stopCh` — so a rate-limited sender still shuts down. **Evidence:** the table test covers missing, negative, `NaN`, `+Inf`, 1.5 s, 60 s and `1e9`; removing the clamp makes it fail with `absurd: discordRetryDelay(1e+09) = 277777h46m40s, want 5m0s`.
- **Notification delivery failures were discarded, and the "cause only" log was still writing the token.** `DispatchNotification` persists the row and then ran `_ = s.sendNotificationRoute(route, n)` inside a goroutine — so a route that had started failing (expired Telegram token, a webhook that moved, a bad chat id) left the operator with a notification that **exists in the console and never reached the channel**, and nothing anywhere said so. It is the same discarded-result shape as the automation script and webhook rounds, in the one delivery path an operator watches. The error is now logged with the **route name, channel and notification title**. The text goes through a new `deliveryErrorText`, which strips `*url.Error` down to its cause, because `*url.Error` embeds the request URL and a notification target carries its credential **in that URL** (`https://api.telegram.org/bot<token>/sendMessage`); the automation webhook path already carried a comment promising "log cause only" while passing the wrapper, so this also closes a **secret-in-logs** hole the comment claimed was closed. **Evidence:** the new test builds a `*url.Error` whose URL contains a token and requires the result to keep `connection refused` while **not** containing the token, plus passthrough/nil/truncation. Verified along the way: the notification-route list **already redacts** secrets, and route writes require `settings.write` (admin by default).
- **The automation webhook never looked at the response it got.** `executeWebhook` closed `resp.Body` without inspecting `resp.StatusCode`, so a receiver answering **500 or 404 was treated exactly like a 200**, and its body — where a receiver puts "signature mismatch" or "unknown event" — was never read. A rule pointing at an endpoint that had moved or started rejecting was **silently dead** (the third variant of the same silent-failure shape in automation). Now `logWebhookResponse` records the **host** (never the query string: a webhook URL can carry a token), the event type and the status — `Warn` with the first 512 bytes of the body for a non-2xx, `Debug` for a success, with the body bounded before it is read and again before it is logged. **Evidence:** the new test drives a 500 (requires `webhook rejected` + status + body), a 200 (requires a debug line) and a 5 KB body (requires the log line under 800 bytes), and asserts the URL's query string never leaks. **A first version of the test passed even when `executeWebhook` stopped calling the helper** — so it now also reads `automation.go` and fails if the call site disappears (verified: `executeWebhook no longer inspects the webhook response`). Already sound in the same pass: `webhookTargetValidator` + `ssrfSafeClient` guard the target.
- **Two automation actions silently did nothing when their preconditions were unmet.** `ActionCreateTask` guarded with `if targetAgent != "" && params.Type != ""` and `ActionSetSleep` with `if targetAgent != "" && params.Interval > 0` — so a rule with no explicit `agent_id` firing on an agent-less event, or a `set_sleep` with interval 0, behaved **exactly like a rule that never matched**: no log, no audit entry, no task. That is the branch an operator is most likely to hit while wiring a rule ("it just doesn't do anything"). Both now log a **Warn** with the event, the agent id and the offending field before returning. **Evidence:** `automation_script_log_test.go` gained a `create_task` with no target and a `set_sleep` with interval 0, requiring `create_task skipped` / `set_sleep skipped`; renaming the first message makes the test fail **with the log line quoted**, so the assertion is load-bearing.
- **Automation-triggered scripts threw their result away, so a script that failed on every event left no trace.** `ActionRunScript` called `engine.Execute(...)` / `engine.ExecuteCode(...)` and discarded the returned `ExecutionResult`: a typo, a permission the automation role lacks, a timeout or a budget cap produced **no log, no audit entry, no symptom** — the rule "ran", the event was consumed, and the only sign was whatever the script was meant to do not happening. The result is now captured and logged with the **script id, event type, agent, tenant and duration** — failures at `Warn` with the truncated error, successes at `Debug` so routine automation stays quiet. **Evidence:** the new `automation_script_log_test.go` installs a `slog` handler, dispatches a rule whose code throws, and requires a line containing `script failed`, `boom` and the agent id; it then requires a successful run to log at debug.
- **The frontend's API paths are now checked against the routes the engine actually has.** `api-paths.ts` is the single list of what the console calls, and a path that no longer exists is a button that 404s — the pages' `.catch()` blocks then make it look like "no data" rather than an error, which is how a feature dies quietly. The new Go test builds the **real router** (`New` + `SetupRoutes`), takes its route set from **gin** (`s.router.Routes()`) and compares every `/api/...` literal in `api-paths.ts`, treating `:id` and `${…}` as one wildcard segment. **Evidence:** renaming `/api/lateral/history/all` to `/every` in the paths file fails with `1 frontend path(s) have no registered route: /api/lateral/history/every`; unchanged it passes. Three things the round taught, each now encoded in the test: a **text parser of `routes*.go` is not enough** (routes sit behind groups — `auth.Group("/api/plugins")` + `GET("/import")` — and `registerAPIRoutes(v1Group)` hides the whole `/api/v1` surface; my regex version reported **90** false positives before I deleted it); `/api/lateral/history/all` **looks** like a call with a literal id but `handleLateralHistory` special-cases `"all"`, so pattern matching alone would call it a bug — it is allowlisted with that reason; and backticked paths in **comments** (`/agents/:id/*`) are not calls, so comment lines are skipped.
- **A dead nav link would only be found by clicking it, so it is now a test.** The router builds every page path from `ROUTE_PAGE_IMPORTS` (`/<id>`) plus a handful of explicit redirects, and the sidebar keeps its own list of hrefs — two tables that can drift. The new `route-imports.test.ts` reads `src/router.tsx` (tests run in Node), collects the paths it declares, and asserts **every** sidebar href resolves to either a page id or a declared path, plus that no href appears twice across sections. **Evidence:** changing `/dashboard` to `/dashboard-typo` in the nav fails with `nav entries with no route: /dashboard-typo (nav.dashboard)`; with the real value all four tests pass. It also pins the two properties the router silently depends on: ids are usable as URL segments (lowercase `kebab-case`, since the router concatenates them verbatim) and every id's loader returns a promise with a `default` export (what `lazy()` needs, and what prefetching assumes). Worth recording: `/chat` in the nav is **not** a dead link — the router redirects it to `/ai`, which the cross-check sees.
- **A data URL without `;base64` was decoded as if it were base64, so percent-encoded payloads became garbage.** `dataUrlToBlob` ran `atob()` on every payload; RFC 2397 says a data URL is base64 **only** when its media type carries the `;base64` marker, so `data:text/plain,test` produced three meaningless bytes instead of `test`. Screenshots arrive as `data:image/png;base64,…` and were never affected, but the helper is a general "data URL → Blob" utility and **silently corrupted** every other form. It now branches on the marker and percent-decodes otherwise, with the byte mask (`& 0xff`) kept so multi-byte characters cannot wrap. **Evidence:** the new test asserts the two decodings produce `[0xde,0xad,0xbe,0xef]` and `"test"`, and forcing `isBase64 = true` makes the text case fail while the other 11 pass. Also added `download.test.ts`, which pins that the download anchor is removed from the document, that the object URL is released **only after the 60 s grace period** (revoking immediately breaks downloads in some browsers), that `Content-Disposition` quoted and unquoted names are both honoured, and records as a **known limitation** that an RFC 5987 `filename*` header falls back to the generic name rather than being silently mis-parsed.
- **`loadShortcuts` handed out the shared default objects on one path and copies on the other.** The custom branch copied each entry (`{ ...DEFAULT_SHORTCUTS[key], ...custom[key] }`), but the no-custom-storage branch returned `{ ...DEFAULT_SHORTCUTS }` — a **shallow** copy, so its values were the **module-level objects**. A caller editing a returned shortcut (`loaded.save.key = "k"` while building a settings form) therefore edited the defaults for the rest of the session, and the keybinding help then showed a binding that no longer matches what fires. Nothing mutates today, so it was latent, but the function was **internally inconsistent about what it hands out**. Both branches now go through `cloneShortcuts`. Found by the new `shortcuts.test.ts` (16 tests) — the assertion `first.save.key = "mutated"; expect(DEFAULT_SHORTCUTS.save.key).toBe("s")` is the one that failed, and it still fails when the shallow copy is put back. The other 15 pin `matchShortcut` (case-insensitivity for single characters, exactness for named keys, ⌘ treated as Ctrl, missing **and** extra modifiers rejected), `formatShortcut` (platform spelling, modifier order) and the storage merge (unknown actions ignored, corrupt JSON and a throwing `localStorage` fall back, untouched fields survive a partial override).
- **Tests for the dock preferences, and a measurement that proved last round's floor was safe.** `interact-storage.ts` rehydrates the interact dock on every mount (height, tab, pinned agent) and had no tests: 13 now pin the clamping (`NaN`/`Infinity`/out-of-range → default or bound), the unknown-tab and empty-agent fallbacks, that only a **literal `true`** counts as pinned, that a corrupt or **throwing** `sessionStorage` falls back instead of breaking the console, that an unpinned dock **drops** the agent id on write, and that a full quota does not throw. **Evidence the tests bite:** changing `parsed.pinned === true` to `Boolean(parsed.pinned)` makes one fail with `expected true to be false`. **The measurement matters more than the tests:** round 208 raised the frontend floor to 33.5 from a **34.84%** reading taken in the working tree — which also contains another agent's 11 in-flight frontend files. Re-measuring in a `git worktree` of **HEAD** (node_modules junctioned in) gives **36.33% (8783/24175)** committed, so the floor is safe and the in-flight work was not inflating it. The extra ~2.3 points of headroom that leaves is deliberate while a second agent is adding frontend code in this clone.
- **A defect I expected to find, and the debug print that said otherwise.** The listener create path sets `l.Status = "running"` in memory and, when the bind fails, marks the row stopped with `s.db.Model(&l).Update("status", "stopped")`. I expected the response to keep the stale in-memory `"running"` — gorm's `Update` writes the row, and I believed it left the Go struct alone — which would tell an operator a dead listener was live. It does **not**: the struct is updated too, and printing the unmodified handler's response showed `"status":"stopped"` with `enabled:true`. I reverted the change I had written for it rather than ship a no-op dressed as a fix. What the round does keep is a **guard**: a test that forces the bind to fail by letting the handler itself bind the port first, then asserts both the **response** and the **row** say stopped — a property nothing pinned before, and one that would matter if the update were ever moved off that line. Two earlier attempts at the same test were **vacuously green** (an unknown scheme is rejected before the row exists; a port occupied from the test side did not stop Windows from binding), which is why the failure is now injected through the same code path.
- **A script could `sleep` longer than the execution timeout, and loop `query` without limit.** `sleep` accepted up to **60 seconds** while the execution timeout is **30** — and a Go-side sleep cannot be interrupted, so `sleep(60000)` did not merely waste time: the caller was told "script execution timeout (30s)" while the VM goroutine kept running for another half minute. **Proof:** with the cap removed, the test's `sleep(60000)` script never completes at all — it dies on the 30-second timeout; with the 5-second cap it finishes in ~5 s. The `query` bridge had no bound either, so a tight loop pinned the **single writer connection** and allocated a result set per iteration. The budget is now split: **500** mutating calls (`sendTask`) and **5000** read calls (`query` **and** `sleep`, so a sleep loop terminates with an error instead of running to the timeout). Tests cover the clamp, the sleep loop, and a 20 000-iteration query loop.
- **A script could flood the database with tasks in a tight loop.** Every `sendTask` bridge call inserts a task row **and** an audit row, and the database has a **single writer**; scripts can also be **bound to events** and re-run on every matching event. Nothing bounded the number of calls per execution, so `for (i=0;i<2000;i++) sendTask(...)` created 2000 tasks plus 2000 audit rows before the 30-second guard fired — a write-amplification primitive on the hottest write path. Execution now carries a per-run budget of **500 mutating bridge calls**; the 501st throws a JS error naming the limit. **Evidence:** with the budget check removed, the test's 2000-call loop reports `success` with output `2000` and the bridge sees all 2000 calls; with it, the script fails with "mutating bridge call limit" and the bridge sees exactly 500 — and a normal 3-call script still succeeds. Worth recording from the same audit: the scripting `httpRequest` bridge **already** has its SSRF guard (host resolution + no redirects), so that surface needed nothing.
- **A runaway-recursion script held a core for the full 30-second timeout.** goja ships `maxCallStackSize = math.MaxInt32` — **no depth limit** — so `function f(){return f()}f()` ran until the execution interrupt fired, growing the stack and burning CPU the whole time; the engine's own 30-second guard was the only thing that stopped it. `executeScript` now sets a depth bound (4096), which turns the same script into an **immediate** JS error. **I expected this to be a fatal Go stack overflow and could not reproduce one** — Go grows goroutine stacks, so the measured behaviour was `script execution timeout (30s)` after exactly 30.0 s, and the change is documented as what it is: a latency/CPU bound, not a crash fix. The same pass caps the rendered **result** at 1 MiB, because a script can return an arbitrarily large string that travels straight into an HTTP response. Tests pin both, including that bounded recursion (depth 200) still returns the right value, so the limit is not so tight that normal scripts break.
- **Plugin quarantine ignored the configured data directory, and the whole cwd-relative family now has a gate.** The new `check:runtime-paths` immediately found a sixth member: `handlePluginDelete` moved a deleted plugin's source to `filepath.Join("data", "quarantine", "plugins", name)` — a path relative to the **process working directory**, not to `server.data_dir` — so quarantined sources landed outside the configured tree and any cleanup walking that tree never saw them. The path now resolves from `rawDataDir()` with the documented `data/` fallback used elsewhere. The gate itself is the point: this shape has now shipped **five** distinct symptoms (payloads, uploads, ACME private key, build output, OpenAPI spec) and every one was found by hand. It flags file operations in server code that start from a **relative literal**, exempts absolute host paths (the implant reading `/etc/hosts` or `/proc/<pid>/status` is the point of the product) and accepts a relative path only with a reason in its ALLOWLIST — four are listed, measured at **41** literal call sites. Proven against the OpenAPI bug: a synthetic `os.ReadFile("api/openapi.yaml")` fails with the file and path named.
- **The API docs endpoint was broken in every packaged release.** `resolveOpenAPIPath()` looked for `api/openapi.yaml` relative to the process working directory, then next to the executable — but the release archives ship the binary, `config.example.yaml` and a start script, **not** the `api/` directory, so `GET /api/docs/openapi.yaml` answered **500** anywhere except a checkout (and a service manager that starts the binary from another directory hits the same failure). The spec is now **embedded** from `api/openapi.yaml` (new `api/` package with `//go:embed`), so the docs work regardless of the working directory while the file stays the single source that `cmd/checkopenapi` and the generated frontend schema read. Tests cover the endpoint returning the embedded bytes with the right content type, the Swagger UI page still rendering, and the embedded copy being the real document rather than a stale snapshot — the handler previously had **0%** coverage.
- **`check:task-docs` was blind to 49 handlers, and one task had no reference row at all.** The gate parsed only `taskHandlers["x"] = …`, but the core command surface in `task_registry.go` is registered with a **map literal** (`"ls": handleLS,`) and four more come through `RegisterTask(protocol.MustSpec(…))`. Measuring the reminder was the point: knowing one idiom covered **169** registrations while the registry has **222**, so a fifth of it could never fail the gate. Now all three idioms are read, and the broader scan immediately found a real gap: **`abort`** — implemented in the agent, registered in the map, and selected by operators from the console — had no spec at all, so it never appeared in the generated command reference **or** the capability matrix. It now has a `TaskTypeAbort` constant, an entry in `AllTaskTypes`, and a spec with its `command:int*` parameter, which puts it in both generated documents (223 specs) and the README fact counters. Verified by a synthetic tree: a map-literal-registered, undocumented handler now fails the gate with its name.
- **Agent status history returned 500 for every tenant-scoped operator.** `handleAgentStatusHistory` applied `tenantScope` — which adds `WHERE tenant_id = ?` — to `db.AgentStatusEvent`, a table whose columns are only `(agent_id, status, timestamp)`. The query failed with `no such column: tenant_id`, so the whole feature was dead in any multi-tenant deployment, and the log line said so on every request. The scope now runs through the **agent** that owns the events (`getAgentOrFail`, which is tenant-scoped), the events query filters by agent id only, and the cross-tenant answer is **404** rather than a leak. The test was written first and captured the real error before the fix. I then audited every other `tenantScope`/`auditTenantScope` call site: the models involved are Implant, Task, AuditLog, CredentialEntry, Listener, CommandMacro, AlertRule, WebhookConfig, Alert, User, AutomationRule, Campaign and MacroRun, **all of which do carry a tenant column**, so this was the only instance of the pattern.
- **Logging stopped when stdout did.** `SetupLogRotation` returned `io.MultiWriter(os.Stdout, lumberjack)` — and `io.MultiWriter` **stops at the first failing writer**: once stdout is a closed console, a rotated redirect target or a full disk, the rotating log file received nothing more while the server kept running. For a C2 the log is the forensic record, so losing it because a terminal went away is the wrong trade; a small `fanoutWriter` now writes each sink independently and reports failure **only when no sink accepted the record**. The same pass fixed a second gap: the **stdlib `log` package was never redirected**, so panics and dependency output reached stderr only — the log file could be missing precisely the line that matters. `SetupLogRotation` now points `log` at the same fanout. Tests prove a failing sink first *or* last still lets the healthy one receive the record, that total failure is reported, and — end to end — that `logs/forgec2.log` really contains what the writer was given.
- **A defect I thought I had found, and the test that said otherwise.** `cleanupBuildJobs` is named like a one-shot sweep and launched from a bare `go func() { s.cleanupBuildJobs() }()` — I read that as "runs once at startup", concluded stager and one-liner artifacts were never reaped until restart, and wrote a replacement loop. The new test failed, which sent me back to the function: it is **already** a long-running loop with its own ticker, so the behaviour was correct and my change was noise. I reverted it (lifecycle, loop, test) rather than shipping a redundant ticker, and kept the part that was genuinely missing: the interval was hardcoded, so nothing could ever prove the reaping works. It is now `buildJobSweepInterval`, and two tests hold the loop to the claim in `registerTransientArtifact`'s comment — an expired artifact disappears **on a tick, without a restart**, and the loop exits when the server context is cancelled (otherwise `Shutdown`'s `wg.Wait()` would hang). Second self-correction of the round: my first shutdown test waited on the server's WaitGroup, which the loop does not touch — its caller owns `w`g.Done()` — so the test hung on its own helper, not on the product code.
- **Follow-through on the migration fix: a failed pragma no longer masquerades as a failed migration, and `foreign_keys` is no longer a discarded error.** Once migrations started failing closed, the eight startup `PRAGMA` calls that shared `execMigration` became wrong in two ways: their log line claimed "will NOT be recorded as applied" (meaningless for a pragma, which is never recorded), and a pragma label entered the migration-failure counter where the next reader would hunt for broken DDL that does not exist. They now go through `execPragma`, which says what actually happened — "the database is running with a less safe or slower setting" — and leaves the counter alone. The same pass fixed `PRAGMA foreign_keys = ON`, which was a **bare `Exec` with the error discarded**: that is the switch the schema's cascades and orphan rejection depend on, so it now reports its failure like the rest. Tests pin the split (a failed pragma is not counted as a failed migration, while a failed statement still is) and assert `foreign_keys = 1` on a freshly initialised database.
- **A failed schema migration was recorded as applied and never retried.** `execMigration` logged a **warning** for any non-ignorable error and carried on, and gormigrate marks a migration applied as soon as its function returns nil — so a failed `CREATE INDEX` or `ALTER` was recorded as done, the index never appeared, and the only trace was one warning line at startup. Failures are now collected per run, logged at **error** level, and returned, which leaves the migration **unapplied** so the next boot retries. Making it strict immediately exposed a real ordering case it had been hiding: schema migrations run **before** AutoMigrate, so on a legacy upgrade three `ALTER` statements target tables AutoMigrate has not created yet. Those are now told apart properly — a "no such table" error is skipped **only when the target table genuinely does not exist** (AutoMigrate creates it, columns included), while the same error against an existing table — the mis-ordered DDL the original comment worried about — is still a failure. Tests pin the table-name parser (`ON tasks(status)` must yield `tasks`), the failure counter's reporting and reset, and both branches of the skip rule.
- **The four places that resolve a directory from `server.data_dir` now agree.** After the payload and uploads fixes, the remaining inconsistency was the build output directory: `extractAgentsDir` turned an empty data dir into `<cwd>/agents` (absolute, so at least unambiguous, but still beside whatever directory the process started in), while `writeScreenshotFile` already defaulted to `"data"`. Build output now defaults to `data/agents` too, so the rule is uniform: a **write** target never depends on the caller's cwd — either the configured tree, the conventional `data/` fallback, or an explicit refusal. A single test pins the whole family — payloads and uploads refuse an empty data dir, agents falls back under `.../data/agents`, and all three honour a configured one — so the next person adding a write target has one place to see what the house rule is. (The `internal/server/data/` directory the suite leaves behind is `.gitignore`d residue from long-standing screenshot/anchor tests, not new pollution.)
- **Agent-exfiltrated files had the same cwd-relative hazard as hosted payloads.** `saveFileChunk` built its write target with `filepath.Join(s.rawDataDir(), "uploads")`, so a server without `server.data_dir` wrote **loot** into a relative `uploads/` directory next to the process working directory — the same class as the payload fix one round earlier, but the stakes are higher: these are **writes** of exfiltrated data, and a misconfigured deployment would have them scattered somewhere nobody backs up or cleans. `uploadsRootDir()` now refuses an empty data dir, and the chunk handler marks the task failed with a reason instead of completing silently. Tests pin both halves: the helper rejects an empty dir and joins the configured one, and a chunk arriving on a server with no data dir leaves **no `uploads/` directory** in the working tree while the task carries the refusal. The suite still leaves no stray directory behind.
- **Hosted payloads were written relative to the process working directory, and the Host check ran after the write.** Two problems in one handler, both visible as **372 empty directories inside `internal/server/`**: `rawDataDir()` returns `""` when `server.data_dir` is empty, and `filepath.Join("", "payloads")` is the *relative* path `payloads` — so payload hosting (and the sweep that expires it) landed next to whatever directory the process started in. On a deployment started from an arbitrary directory that means payloads go somewhere nobody looks while `cleanupOldPayloads` scans the wrong tree. A helper `hostedPayloadsDir()` now refuses an empty data dir instead of guessing, and both the writer and the sweep use it. Found while investigating the stray directory, a second defect: the handler **wrote the payload to disk before validating the Host header**, so every rejected spoofed-Host request still left a directory behind — validation now runs first, with a build-log entry, and the test server that reproduced the pollution now gets a temp data dir.
- **The string-splitting transform changed the value of a double-quoted string.** `splitStrings` ran a regex over the whole source, so single quotes nested inside a **double-quoted** string were rewritten too: `"say 'hi' now"` became `"say ('h'+'i') now"` — valid PowerShell whose value is different, which is a payload that does something else. It now scans the source with quote state, copying double-quoted runs whole (they may legally contain single quotes as text) and splitting only real single-quoted literals. The value property is now asserted rather than assumed: for six literals across 40 seeds, every rewritten form is reassembled from its fragments and compared to the original, and the test fails if a literal was **never** split (so it cannot pass by doing nothing). The original skip rules are pinned too — URLs, Windows paths, `{0}` and `%s` format strings, one-character literals and over-length ones all stay byte-identical. The superseded regex version was deleted; `internal/obfuscation` **94.4% → 94.9%**.
- **The trigger-scrubbing transform produced payloads that do not parse.** It was a global `strings.ReplaceAll` over each AMSI/EDR token, so the two most common shapes both broke: `Invoke-Mimikatz` became `Invoke-('mimi'+'katz')`, which is not a command name but a parse error, and `Write-Output 'mimikatz'` became `Write-Output '('mimi'+'katz')'`, an unterminated string. `standard` and `heavy` both enable this transform, so the presets could emit a payload that simply fails to run — the opposite of what obfuscation is for. The replacement (`scrubTriggersSafe`) reuses the quote-aware scanner from the previous round and applies the same `'a'+'b'` trick **only where it is safe**: never inside a single- or double-quoted string, and never where the token is adjacent to identifier characters, so a name like `Invoke-Mimikatz` stays intact. That last case is a deliberate trade — a payload that runs with one recognizable token beats a payload that does not run — and it is documented at the function. Tests cover the command name, three quoted shapes (including the `''` escape), a standalone token that is still split (with the concatenation reassembled to verify the value), and an already-split token not being wrapped twice. The superseded global version was **deleted** rather than left for someone to call again; `internal/obfuscation` **93.8% → 94.4%**. One existing test encoded the old behaviour ("Invoke-Mimikatz must be scrubbed") and was updated with the reasoning.
- **The variable-rename transform rewrote text inside single-quoted PowerShell strings.** `renameVariables` matched `$name` anywhere in the script, including inside `'single quotes'` — where PowerShell does **not** expand variables. A payload that printed `'costs $price'` came out printing `'costs $AbCdEf'`: still valid PowerShell, doing something different. The transform now scans the source linearly, copies single-quoted runs verbatim (honouring the `''` escape) and renames only outside them, while **double-quoted** strings are still renamed on purpose because PowerShell expands variables there — skipping those would break the payload the other way. One shared mapping keeps a variable's new name consistent across the segments the scanner produces. Tests: the literal survives byte for byte, the interpolation is renamed, escaped quotes don't derail the scan, the same variable gets one name in every segment, and `$true`/`$PSVersionTable`/`$env:PATH` are never touched. `renameVariablesOutsideSingleQuotes` **100%**, `internal/obfuscation` **93.5% → 93.8%**.
- **A transient local write failure locked the whole file upload.** The chunk handler commits the chain link inside `verifyAndCommitChain` **before** it opens, seeks and writes the file, so any local failure after that point — invalid offset, permission denied, disk full, short write — advanced the server's chain while the bytes never landed. The agent's next link then failed to match, and because round 147 made a mismatch **poison** the transfer, one disk hiccup permanently killed the upload. Every local failure path now rolls the link back to the value it replaced (`rollbackChainLink`, which also **clears the poison**, since a local failure is not tampering), so the agent's retry of that same chunk verifies again. Tests pin both halves: verify → "write fails" → rollback → the identical chunk retries successfully, and a rollback after a genuine mismatch re-opens a transfer that had been locked. `rollbackChainLink` **90%**, `chainLinkFor` **82%**.
- **An expired approval prompt could still be approved.** Approval-gated tasks carry `approval_expires_at` and a sweeper cancels them every five minutes — but `handleApproveTask` never checked the expiry, so between expiry and the next sweep an operator could confirm a prompt that had deliberately gone stale. That is exactly the window the field exists to close: the point of expiring a prompt is that the world changed since it was created, and a stale confirmation is a confused-deputy. The expiry is now part of the **atomic conditional update** (`approval_expires_at IS NULL OR > now`), so a concurrent sweep cannot be raced either; when the update matches nothing, the handler distinguishes the two refusals — an expired prompt is cancelled the same way the sweeper does it and answers **410** ("create a new task"), while a concurrently rejected/cancelled task still answers **409**. Tests cover the expired case end to end (410, row moved to `cancelled` with a reason so a retry cannot resurrect it), the still-valid case (200 and `approved_by` set), and that the two-man rule is untouched. `internal/server` **65.9% → 66.0%**.
- **Editing the plugin quotas on a running server changed nothing, while the reload reported "plugins applied".** The new `plugins` reload token had a hook that only called `SetTrust`; `max_concurrent`, `max_cpu_seconds` and `max_write_mb` were applied once at startup and never again. So an operator tightening the plugin ceiling during an incident saw a successful reload and kept the old limits — the same "reports success, nothing happened" shape as the last three rounds, this time in a resource-control policy. The hook now installs the whole policy (trust **and** the three quotas) and remains the single startup path. `plugin.Manager` gained four accessors (`MaxConcurrent`, `MaxCPUSeconds`, `MaxWriteMB`, `RequireSigned`) so both the tests and any status surface read what is **actually enforced** instead of assuming a setter took. Tests cover a reload that raises each quota, flipping `require_signed` on at runtime, an invalid `trusted_keys` list being **reported** rather than swallowed, and repeated application not drifting (a config that leaves a quota at 0 keeps the manager's build-time default, which the first version of the test got wrong).
- **`crypto.require_release_signature` could not be reloaded — the change was recorded as a failure with a programming-error reason.** `diffConfig` reports the token, but the reload table had no matching entry, so the apply loop hit its "unknown token (diffConfig/table drift)" branch: the operator edits the control, the reload is logged as failed, and the reason is a string meant for developers. The value happens to be read live by `performHotUpdate`, so the fix is the token itself (`reloadHot`, no hook, with the reason recorded next to it). Two new tests make the whole pairing impossible to drift again: **every token `diffConfig` can report must exist in the reload table, and every table token must be producible**, and no token may be appended twice. Both read the two source files, because a static comparison is the only place that can see both lists at once — the same technique the Node gates use. The first run of the parity test failed on this token, which is exactly the drift it was written to catch.
- **Four config sections were invisible to hot reload, and one of them was the plugin trust policy.** `CopyFrom` carried a comment warning that every top-level section must be listed or a reload keeps stale values — and the list was missing `Plugins`, `Backup`, `WebRTC` and `Protocol`. `diffConfig` was missing the same four, so a reload that changed `plugins.require_signed` did not even report a difference: the file watcher logged "no values differ, skipping reload" and the live trust policy stayed as it was at startup. Both halves are fixed: `CopyFrom` copies the four sections, `diffConfig` compares them (`DeepEqual`, since they carry slices) and classifies `plugins`/`backup` as hot / `webrtc`/`protocol` as restart-only, and the reload table gained matching tokens — `plugins` with a real Apply hook, `applyPluginTrust`, which is now **also the startup path** so there is one place that installs the policy. A reflection test (`TestCopyFromCoversEverySection`) now fails for any future section nobody adds, and the diff test flips **each** section including the four and requires a single, non-duplicated entry. That same test caught a live copy-paste bug: the `crypto.require_release_signature` condition appeared **twice**, inflating the "N fields changed" count that reaches the log and the audit trail.
- **A hot reload silently dropped every environment override — including `FORGEC2_DATA_DIR` and the crypto keys.** `Load` applied **21** `FORGEC2_*` variables; the reload path rebuilt the config from the file alone (`DefaultConfig()` + `LoadFromData`). So the first config change after startup reverted them all. The damage, worst first: `data_dir`/`db_path` revert → the running server starts writing to **different paths**; `loot_key`/`extc2_key`/`backup_key`/`totp_key`/`csrf_key` revert → existing credentials and backups stop decrypting; `plugins.require_signed` reverts → **the plugin trust policy silently weakens**; host/port revert. All env handling now lives in one `ApplyEnvOverrides`, called by `Load` **and** by both reload paths before `Validate`, so "the file changed" can no longer mean "the environment stopped applying". Secret minting stays in `Load` only — a reload must never mint a new JWT secret — and the ordering is pinned because minting saves the file: the full override set runs **after** it, or the save would copy environment secrets onto disk (a test caught exactly that when I first got the order wrong). Tests cover every variable, the invalid-value cases, reload parity for data dir / loot key / trust policy / db path, and that a reload does not mint. `ApplyEnvOverrides` **65% → 98%**, `internal/config` **83.3% → 86.1%**.
- **Saving the config from the console no longer deletes options it does not understand.** The previous round stopped *loading* from dropping unknown keys; the save path had the same trap in a worse form: `Save` rewrote the file from the in-memory struct, so an option added by a **newer build** (or set by hand for one) disappeared the first time an operator changed the port — and after the upgrade it was silently back at its default. `Save` now carries the unknown subtrees over from the file on disk, **including nested maps**, while every key the struct knows is taken from the live config (so a value the operator cleared cannot come back from the old file — that direction is tested too). The first write, and an unreadable existing file, still save normally. `Save` **92%**, `unknownSubtree` **93%**, `internal/config` **83.3%**.
- **A misspelled config key is no longer dropped in silence.** `Load` used a plain `yaml.Unmarshal`, which ignores keys the struct does not have — so a typo in a security control (`crypto.require_release_signatur: true`) was **discarded**, and the operator believed the control was on. The loader now reports every unknown key as a dotted path on the first start (`crypto.require_release_signatur, server.prot, totally_unknown_section`), and deliberately **warns instead of failing**: a config written by a newer build carries keys this one does not know, and refusing it would turn a staged rollback into an outage. The detection is a bounded reflection walk (depth 8) over the decoded document, and the warning caps its list so a pathological file cannot flood the log. The user-facing half is tested too: **the shipped `config.example.yaml` has no unknown keys**, so an operator who copies it is never told they wrote something wrong. `UnknownConfigKeys` **86%**, `internal/config` **83.2% → 83.3%**.
- **The transform library now has property tests instead of two fixed samples.** The profile editor validates a whole `server_output` chain at save time, so a lossy transform surfaced as a confusing "your profile does not round-trip" error with no indication of which step was at fault. The new tests name it: nine transforms (`base64`, `base64url`, `netbios`, `netbiosu`, `mask`, `xor`, `urlencode`, `append`, `prepend`) round-trip **six** payload classes each — empty, ASCII, mixed case, **raw binary including `0x00` and `0xff`**, a padded tail, and marker-heavy text — and all of them pass. Multi-step chains are checked to decode in reverse order (`base64` + `append` must strip the suffix *before* decoding). The conditional ones are pinned by their real behaviour rather than an assumption: `uri_append` and `print` round-trip, `case` is lossy by design (uppercase→lowercase cannot restore case), and `strrep` round-trips only while the payload does **not** contain the replacement — the decode pass rewrites the payload's own copy, which is exactly the case that makes the editor warn. `Apply` **94%**, `applySingle` **96%**, `internal/malleable` **78.8% → 79.0%**.
- **A payload icon shorter than four bytes skipped validation entirely.** The icon upload checked `len(data) >= 4 && !(ico) && !(png)`, so a file of one to three bytes failed the `>= 4` guard and the magic test was **short-circuited away** — a 1-byte "icon" was accepted and embedded into the build. The check is now `validateIconBytes`, which rejects anything shorter than a magic number and then requires the ICO (`00 00 01 00`) or PNG (`89 50 4E 47`) prefix **at offset 0**, so a signature that merely appears later in the file does not pass either. The table test pins both directions: valid ICO and PNG accepted; empty, 0–3 byte, `GIF8`, `MZ\x90\x00` and an off-by-one signature rejected. `validateIconBytes` **100%**.
- **A DSN carrying a query string silently disabled foreign-key enforcement.** `InitDBWithDriver` appended its `_pragma=` set only when the path contained **no** `?`, so a DSN such as `file:data.db?cache=shared` (or any path with a query, which is legal on Linux) ran with the pragmas skipped — `foreign_keys` off means orphan rows are accepted and the schema's cascades stop working, with nothing in the log to say so. The pragmas are now appended with `&` when a query already exists, and left untouched only when the DSN **already names `_pragma=`** (where the operator was explicit). Tests pin all three: a query-bearing DSN still rejects an orphan row, a SQLite pool is always clamped to the single writer **even when the caller asks for 25** (the invariant behind the transaction rule), and an explicit `_pragma=foreign_keys(0)` is **overridden to 1** — the server requires enforcement, so a DSN alone must not be able to switch integrity off. `internal/db` **62.3% → 62.4%**.
- **The scan-target guard is now a named, self-contained helper with direct coverage.** The inline check from the previous commit became `validateScanTarget`, which also rejects an empty target, so a future caller cannot skip the guard by trusting its own call site. The table test pins both directions: IPv6 literals, `host:port`, space-separated lists, trailing whitespace and empty are refused; a bare IPv4, a comma-separated multi-target list and a hostname pass. An attempt to teach `check-command-separators` about `:`-separated commands was **reverted**: a static rule cannot tell a task command from the many display-only `"%s:%d"` strings without flagging a dozen innocent functions, so the guard stays a runtime check and the gate keeps its one honest rule. `validateScanTarget` **100%**, `internal/server` **65.8% → 65.9%**.
- **An IPv6 target made the port scanner scan the wrong host.** The scan command is `<target>:<ports>` and the implant parses it with `SplitN(command, ":", 2)`, so a target of `2001:db8::1` became target `2001` with `db8::1:80,443` as the "port list" — the scan ran against a different host than the operator named, silently. The handler now refuses a target containing `:`, whitespace or a newline with a 400 that explains the format (comma-separated multi-targets still work, and the test pins that shape so the guard cannot over-reach). Worth noting the same separator class as the `"|"` gate: this one is `:` and the parser is in the implant, so the only compatible fix was validation on the way in. `handleScanTask` **86%**, `parsePortRange` **91%**.
- **Stopping a SOCKS tunnel left its queued frames behind.** The per-agent control and rportfwd queues are keyed by agent id, and nothing but a drain ever touched the key, so every agent that ever used a tunnel kept map entries after `stopSession` — and a re-used agent id could inherit stale close frames from the previous session. `stopSession` now drops both queues (and the byte accounting) with the session; the teardown test asserts the session, its control frames and its rportfwd frames/bytes are all gone, and that stopping an unknown agent is a clean error rather than a panic. `stopSession` **88%**.
- **`AGENTS.md` now documents the registry-eviction rule.** The last four defects were all the same shape — a per-agent registry with a writer and no *reachable* eviction, where the damage was not memory but a stale entry still answering "active" to code that gates behaviour. The new section records the rules (evict on the read path; `cleanupStaleMapEntries` is only reached by a **24 h** ticker; a single-flight reservation must expire because a panicking handler skips its cleanup; payload-holding entries die with their session) and a table of every current timeout and where it lives, so the next reader does not rediscover it the hard way.
- **A leaked start/stop reservation could lock an agent out of screen monitoring forever.** The single-flight map that stops two start/stop requests from racing is cleared on the handlers' success and error paths — but a **panic** inside `issueAgentTask` unwinds the handler and skips the clear, and `screenMonitorOps` had **no sweep anywhere** (it is not one of the maps the periodic stale-map pass touches). That agent then answered `409 screen monitor start already in progress` for every future start *and* stop, permanently. Reservations now carry a timestamp and expire after two minutes (`reserveScreenMonitorOp`), so the guard still refuses a genuinely concurrent request while a leaked one clears itself. Both directions are pinned: a fresh reservation still returns 409 with the operation name, and an expired one lets the start through. `reserveScreenMonitorOp` **100%**, `handleStartScreenMonitor` **79%**.
- **The cached screen frame is reclaimed when its stream expires.** Each entry holds a whole base64 JPEG per agent, and its only reclamation was the same once-a-day periodic sweep — so a crashed stream left a large frame resident for up to 24 hours (and a day's worth of them after a busy session). The frame now goes with the expired monitor entry. A correction to the entry above: the stale-map sweep was not missing, it was only reachable from a 24-hour loop; the heartbeat timeout makes reclamation immediate, which is what actually mattered.
- **A dead screen stream silently stopped screenshots from being saved.** The monitor registry stores a heartbeat timestamp per agent, and the stale-map sweep that reclaims it is only reached from the periodic cleanup, which runs **once every 24 hours** (`PeriodicCleanupInterval`) — so when a stream ended without a stop (agent crashed, console tab closed), the entry stayed for up to a day and `IsScreenMonitoring` kept answering "true", which makes `saveScreenshot` refuse to persist that agent's frames: the operator just saw screenshots stop appearing, with no error anywhere. Stale entries also counted against `MaxScreenMonitors`, eventually refusing new streams. The heartbeat now has a 2-minute timeout: `IsScreenMonitoring` treats (and removes) an expired entry, and the start handler sweeps dead entries **before** the slot-limit check. Both directions are pinned — a live stream is still relayed-not-retained and a frame refreshes its heartbeat, while an expired one lets retention resume. `IsScreenMonitoring` **0 → 100%**, `sweepScreenMonitorsLocked` **100%**.
- **A relay that was never stopped stayed "active" forever.** `ntlmRelayStore` only removed a session when the operator pressed stop, so if the agent died or the console was closed mid-relay the entry stayed in memory **and kept being reported as a running relay** — a phantom that misleads an engagement ("we still have a relay on the DC") while leaking memory. Worse, starting a second relay on the same agent added a **second** session while the stop handler looked sessions up by agent, so the orphan was unreachable and could never be cleared. Both are fixed: `add` now replaces any unfinished session for that agent (and defaults a missing start time instead of letting it look infinitely old), and the status endpoint sweeps sessions older than 12 hours before listing — so what the console shows is what is actually running. `handleNTLMRelayStatus` **0 → 100%**, `newNTLMRelayStore` **100%**.
- **A crashed backup left its temp files behind forever.** `createBackup` writes a VACUUM snapshot (`.forgec2-snapshot-*.tmp`) and then an atomic `*.fbk.tmp` before renaming, and its `defer os.Remove` only runs on a **graceful** return. A crash — or a `Stop()` during shutdown, which does not wait for an in-flight backup — therefore abandoned those files, and `cleanupOldBackups` only ever looked at `*.fbk`, so they accumulated until an operator noticed the disk filling. The retention sweep now also removes stale snapshot/temp artefacts older than an hour; the age gate is what keeps a genuinely in-flight backup safe, so the test pins both sides (2-hour-old leftovers deleted, a fresh `.fbk.tmp` and a real `.fbk` kept). `cleanupOldBackups` **90%**, `removeStaleTempFiles` **79%**.
- **Two CSV exports shifted columns whenever a field contained a comma.** The timeline and scan exports hand-built their rows with `fmt.Sprintf` and only guarded against formula leaders (`csvSanitize`), so a comma in `Details` — or in a service version like `Apache 2.4.1, OpenSSL 1.1.1` — moved every later value one column left, and a newline broke the row outright. In a scan export that maps a banner to the **wrong port**; in the timeline it associates a timestamp with the **wrong agent**, silently, in a file an operator pastes into a client report. Both now write through `encoding/csv` (as the task and credential exports already did), keeping `csvSanitize` for the formula-injection guard and letting the writer handle quoting, embedded commas, quotes and newlines. The tests parse the export back with `encoding/csv` and assert every row has the header's column count and that a comma-bearing value stays intact in **its own** column. `handleExportScanResults` **94%**, `handleTimelineExport` **77%**.
- **Two more separator-joined commands took unvalidated input: persistence and UAC bypass.** `handlePersistence` builds `method|binary_path` and `handleUACBypass` builds `method|payload` straight from form fields, while the inject/spawn paths already validated theirs with `validateCommandArg`. An unvalidated `|` shifts the later field, so a **`persistence_remove` would delete a different persistence entry than the operator picked**, and a UAC bypass would run a different payload than the one chosen — both are actions taken on the target, not console-side errors, so they cannot be caught later. Both now use the same `validateCommandArg` guard (which also rejects NUL/CR/LF/tab and enforces the length limits), and the tests assert the refusal **creates no task row** while a clean request still queues exactly one. `handleUACBypass` **0 → 81%**, `handlePersistence` **48.8%**; `internal/server` **65.4% → 65.5%**.
- **A credential containing `|` could check the wrong account — and dodge the lockout fuse.** `cred_check` uses the same `user|domain|password|dc` wire format as the spray path, built from both the manual form and **stored vault entries** (credential reuse). A password containing a `|` — legal, and common in generated passwords — shifts every later field, so the implant validates a different account than the one the server recorded; worse, the lockout fuse keys on **the domain the server parsed**, so the shifted check counts against the wrong domain and the fuse protection no longer covers the account actually being probed. The manual path now refuses with a 400 that names the separator, and the reuse path **skips the entry visibly** (`skipped_separator` with an explanation) instead of queueing a task that means something else — a stored value cannot be rejected at input time, so it is surfaced per-entry. A shared `credentialCommandHasSeparator` helper backs both. `handleCredCheck` **0 → 89%**, `credentialCommandHasSeparator` **100%**; `internal/server` stays **65.4%**.
- **A password containing `|` silently re-targeted the spray at another domain.** The password-spray wire format is `password|domain|dc|delay_ms`, and the implant splits it with `SplitN(..., "|", 5)`. A password such as `pa|ss` shifts every later field, so the implant reads the domain as `ss` and sprays **the wrong domain** — enough to lock out accounts in it. Compatibility forbids changing the wire format (already-deployed implants parse it), so the server now **refuses** a `|` in `password`/`domain`/`dc`/`delay_ms` with a 400 that names the field and the separator, instead of building a task that silently means something else. The happy path is covered too: the command is assembled in the documented order, usernames travel as task data, and each required field is enforced. `handlePasswordSpray` **0 → 92%**; `internal/server` **65.3% → 65.4%**.
- **An allowlist entry typed with capitals never matched.** Both origin gates (`server.allowedOrigin` for the WebSocket upgrade, and `config.AllowedOrigin`) compared the browser's `Origin` host with an **exact, case-sensitive** string, but hostnames are case-insensitive (RFC 4343). An operator who added `MyHost.com` to `server.allowed_origins` — and browsers send the host lowercased — was **still refused**, with nothing explaining why. Both copies now normalise with `ToLower`/`TrimSpace` (a padded hand-edited YAML entry works too), keeping the deny-by-default behaviour: an empty list still admits only `localhost`/`127.0.0.1`/`::1`, a suffix lookalike (`example.com.evil.test`) is still refused, and a malformed `Origin` is refused rather than guessed. `allowedOrigin` **100%**, `config.AllowedOrigin` **0 → 100%**; `internal/server` **65.1% → 65.2%**, `internal/config` **81.7% → 83.2%**.
- **The most common legacy malleable profile could not be imported at all.** `MigrateProfileJSON` decided "this is already v2" from the presence of `server_output` / `client_metadata` / `parameter` — but those keys exist in **both** shapes: a string wire name in v1 (`"server_output":"base64"`) and an array of steps in v2. A v1 profile was therefore classified as v2, the unmarshal failed on the string, and the import aborted with `cannot unmarshal string into ... []malleable.StepV2`. Detection now parses as v2 and, when that fails **and** the document also carries v1-only keys (`beacon_uri`, `user_agent`, `method`, `sleep`, …), falls through to the v1 converter instead of erroring. Both directions are pinned: a v2 profile is never pushed through the legacy converter, a legacy `http_get`/`http_post` profile keeps its URIs, a v1 profile keeps its fields and has its wire-form transforms **parsed**, and a real v2 syntax error still fails loudly. `internal/malleable` **71.7% → 77.7%**.
- **Saving a script executed it — now it only parses.** `LoadScript` validated a script by `RunString`-ing it in the reparse VM, so "save" ran the whole body: a top-level `sendTask(...)` **fired a real task against an agent** just by saving the script, `while(true){}` wedged the save until the parse timeout, and any reference to a per-run binding (`agent_id`, `args`, …) failed with `ReferenceError: … is not defined` because those bindings only exist during a run — so most useful scripts could never be saved. Validation is now `goja.Compile` (parse only): syntax errors are still rejected, nothing executes, and the load path enables the same `console`/`require` surface the runtime installs (a bare VM previously rejected `console.log` at load while accepting it at run time). Covered by a load/unload/execute lifecycle test, the "load does not execute" cases (infinite loop, side-effecting script), `toInt64` coercion of `int`/`int64`/`float64`, runtime errors surfacing as failures, and per-run context bindings. `internal/scripting` **55.2% → 72.4%**.
- **The SSE tool frame could be invalid JSON, dropping a tool result from the transcript.** The `tool` event was assembled with `fmt.Sprintf(`{"id":"%s","name":"%s","result":%s}`, …)`: the tool name was embedded unescaped (a model-supplied name containing a quote breaks the frame) and the result was spliced in raw — but `compactToolResultJSON` deliberately returns **plain text** for a tool whose output is not JSON, so exactly those results produced an unparseable frame. The frame is now marshalled (`buildToolEvent`): a valid-JSON result is carried as an object, anything else as a JSON string, and the name is escaped. Tests cover a JSON result (stays an object), a text result with quotes/newlines/`</script>` (round-trips as a string), a quoted tool name, and an empty result. `buildToolEvent` **83%**.
- **Two bugs came out of covering the AI web-search tool.** (1) Its error paths built the JSON payload with `fmt.Sprintf(`{"error":"…%s"}`, err)`; a message containing a quote — every `Get "https://…"` failure does — produced **invalid JSON** for the tool-result parser. Errors are now marshalled (`aiToolErrorJSON`), and a round-trip test covers quotes, newlines and backslashes. (2) The search allowlist check is now covered in both directions: subdomains pass, lookalikes such as `evilwikipedia.org`, `wikipedia.org.evil.example` and `duckduckgo.com.evil.test` do not.
- **Two backups in the same second overwrote each other.** Found by a flake in the new backup test: `Start` fires an immediate backup while a manual run can arrive in the same second, so both computed the same `forgec2_backup_<timestamp>.fbk` — the second rename overwrote the first on Unix and would fail outright on Windows. Snapshot creation is now serialised (`runMu`, so two concurrent `VACUUM INTO` runs cannot fight over the writer) and the filename gains a `_2`, `_3` suffix when the timestamp is already taken. The lifecycle test now polls for the snapshot instead of assuming which run produced it.
- **A base64-shaped hostname was silently mangled, breaking hostname matching.** `maybeDecodeStoredIdentity` (the restore for implant identity fields stored in base64 wire form) decoded any value that happened to be a valid quartet. `DC01` — one of the most common Windows domain-controller names — decodes to `"\x0c-5"`, which `TrimSpace` reduces to `-5`; the guard accepted it because `-5` contains `-`. The row in the database was correct, but every read showed `-5`: the console displayed a garbage hostname and hostname-based auto-tag rules silently stopped matching that agent. Found while testing the auto-tag sweeper, which read `-5` back from a row that plainly stored `DC01`. The decoder now requires the decoded value to be a **plausible identity** (starts alphanumeric, two or more alphanumerics, only identity characters) and, when the original carries no base64 proof (`+`, `/`, `=`), to contain an address separator (`.`, `:`, `\`); otherwise the stored value is kept verbatim. `WEB1` → `X@u` and `DC01` → `-5` are now both rejected, while real wire form (`MTkyLjE2OC4xLjI0` → `192.168.1.24`, padded values, `CORP\alice`) still decodes. Covered in `internal/db` (the table includes the exact regression) and end to end.
- **Auto-tag matching is covered, and an unknown field no longer matches the fleet.** `autoTagMatch`/`matchOne` were at 73%/33%. A rule naming a field the evaluator does not know compared `""` against the value — so a typo (`hostnmae`) made `equals:""` true and tagged **every agent**. Unknown fields now fail closed. The tests cover every operator (contains/equals/not_equals/starts_with/regex), case-insensitivity, the public_ip alias, `+`-AND-ing of multiple conditions, fail-closed shapes (empty, malformed, unknown operator, invalid regex) and an end-to-end sweep that tags only matching agents, is idempotent on a second run, and does nothing while the rule is disabled. `autoTagMatch` and `matchOne` **100%**; `internal/server` **64.4% → 64.5%**, CI total **51.2%**.
- **The emergency panel no longer fabricates zeros.** `handleEmergencyStatus` logged a failed count and fell through for both figures, so a database error produced `online_agents: 0, pending_kills: 0` — on the one screen an operator reads while deciding whether the fleet is really down. Both counts now answer **500** instead. The test pins what the numbers mean: `online_agents` counts everything that is not `offline` (so `stale` still counts, because a quiet beacon is not a dead one), and `pending_kills` counts only `kill` tasks in `pending`/`running` — completed kills and unrelated task types are excluded.
- **Two more "log and report success" paths are fixed.** `handlePluginDelete` dereferenced `s.pluginManager` with no nil check whenever the plugin had a name — the same panic the toggle had, on the delete path; it now guards the manager and still removes the row. `handleAutoTagApply` answered **200** with `{"applied":0,"partial":true,"error":"batch insert failed"}` when the whole batch failed; nothing reads `partial`, so the failure surfaced as success. It now answers **500**. The plugin-delete guard is covered by a test (delete works and no panic without a manager); the autotag failure branch cannot be exercised without forcing a batch-insert failure, which is recorded rather than claimed.
- **The plugin toggle could panic on a nil manager and could report a state the runtime never adopted.** `handlePluginToggle` called `s.pluginManager.SetEnabled` whenever the plugin row had a name, with no nil check — a server built without a plugin manager (tests, or any build with plugins disabled) would panic on the dereference. It now guards the manager, and when the manager **rejects** the change it reverts the row and answers **409** instead of returning the row's optimistic value: the console would otherwise show a plugin as enabled while the runtime kept it disabled until the next restart. The test pins the nil-manager path (toggle persists, no panic), the no-body flip, and that an unknown plugin is a 404 rather than a silent success. `handlePluginToggle` **59%** (the manager-rejection branch needs a real manager, which is why it stays uncovered).
- **The listener detail page no longer shows a reassuring zero when the count fails.** `handleListenerDetail` logged a failed `COUNT(active agents)` and then **fell through**, responding `ActiveAgents: 0`; the detail page renders that number prominently, so a transient database error read as "this listener has no live beacons". It now answers 500 like the neighbouring agent query does, and the page shows its error state instead of a fabricated figure. The new test also pins what the number means: only agents seen within `ListenerActiveThreshold` count, and an agent from another tenant never appears in `Agents` at all. `handleListenerDetail` **70% → 78%**.
- **The "stale" agent band was 180 nanoseconds wide, so stale agents were reported as offline.** `staleThreshold()` computed `time.Duration(d) * StaleThresholdMultiplier` where `d` is a threshold in **seconds**: the result was a nanosecond value, so the band `(offline, stale]` collapsed and any agent older than the offline threshold landed in **offline**. The agents page, the dashboard counts and the REST v1 list all bucket through this helper, so a fleet that had been quiet for two minutes showed as offline rather than stale. The multiplication now includes `* time.Second`; a regression test asserts the stale threshold is `multiplier * offline` **and** strictly wider than the offline threshold, and the filter test pins an agent seen two minutes ago to the stale bucket. `implantListQuery` **63% → 95%**, `staleThreshold` **100%**.
- **The agent signaling socket is covered, and its error-frame cap was dead code.** `handleRDWebRTCAgent` was at 0%. The tests now pin the token guards (missing session/token → 400, unknown token → 401, a valid token presented for a different agent id → 401 with no agent attached), the loop (the operator is told `agent_ready`; `answer`, `ice` and `state` frames reach the operator; an empty answer is dropped; `stop` ends the session) and the single-use token (a second dial with the same token is refused as 401 — the handler's 409 "already has an agent" branch is therefore unreachable, which is recorded rather than silently deleted). Writing the test exposed a real defect: the `error` frame truncated `msg.Reason` to 512 bytes and then forwarded the **original** bytes, so an agent could relay an error reason as large as the whole read limit; the truncated frame is now re-marshalled and sent. `handleRDWebRTCAgent` **0 → 79%**; `internal/server` **62.3% → 62.5%**.
- **Remote-desktop signaling was dead whenever the browser connected before the agent.** `webrtcSession.attachOperator` returned *agent readiness*, but `handleRDWebRTCSignal` used the value as *attach success* and closed the socket on a false return. The operator dials as soon as it has the session while the agent attaches after a round trip — the common order — so the signaling socket was killed immediately, no frame ever reached the browser, and remote desktop could not work. It now reports attach success and readiness is queried with `agentAttached()` (the operator is told `waiting` until the agent arrives).
