Both now go through the same redaction, which keeps the host (so the line stays diagnosable) and replaces the path/query with `<redacted>`; the delivery-error text is redacted too, because a `*url.Error` stringifies the whole URL. The helper is now named `redactURLFromMessage` since it is no longer webhook-specific. Tests pin the callback log end to end (token absent, host present) and the provider-key shape. `executeTaskCallback` **70% → 78%**, `internal/server` **65.6% → 65.7%**.
- **Webhook delivery wrote the target URL — which is often the credential — into the log.** `fireWebhook` deliberately avoided logging the URL ("No URL in the log: webhook targets may embed secret tokens"), but then logged the raw `client.Do` error a few lines later, and a `*url.Error` stringifies the whole URL — so every failed delivery wrote the token to the log. A Slack incoming-webhook URL *is* the credential, so this leaked live secrets into `logs/`. Errors are now redacted before logging: the scheme and host survive (so the log stays diagnosable) and the path/query — where tokens live — are replaced with `<redacted>`. The retry backoff also became a bounded lookup (`webhookRetryDelay`) so raising `webhookMaxRetries` past the table can no longer index out of range. Tests cover the redaction helpers, the bounded backoff, and an end-to-end delivery failure that asserts the token is absent from the captured log **while the host is still present**. `redactWebhookTarget`/`redactURLForLog`/`webhookRetryDelay` **100%**, `fireWebhook` **7% → 40%**.
- **The webhook validator accepted hosts it could not resolve, and missed multicast/CGNAT.** `validateWebhookURL` carried its own copy of the address policy: it ignored a DNS resolution **failure** (`if err == nil`), so a hostname that did not resolve at save time was accepted and then dialled once it did — the classic DNS-rebinding shape — and it did not reject multicast or CGNAT targets. The policy now lives in one shared `nonPublicHostError` helper (netip + unmap + loopback/unspecified/private/link-local/multicast + `0.0.0.0/8` + CGNAT), with resolution failure treated as an error. Delivery re-validates the URL at fire time as well, so a target that later resolves to a private address is still refused. Tests pin both directions, including `172.15`/`172.32` and `100.63`/`100.128` just outside their ranges, and the existing webhook tests moved from `example.test` to a public literal so they no longer depend on DNS. `validateWebhookURL` **74% → 87%**.
- **The outbound-fetch guard could be walked past `0.0.0.0` and IPv4-mapped forms.** `isPrivateIP` — the check standing between a plugin's `http.request` and the server's own loopback services — matched **string prefixes** (`10.`, `192.168.`, `127.`, …). It returned false for `0.0.0.0`, which connects to the local host on Linux, for `::ffff:127.0.0.1`, for bracketed literals like `[::1]`, and for the `::` unspecified address. It now parses with `netip`, unmaps IPv4-mapped addresses and asks `IsLoopback`/`IsUnspecified`/`IsPrivate`/`IsLinkLocal*`/`IsMulticast`, adding CGNAT (`100.64.0.0/10`) which the standard predicates do not cover. The table pins both sides, including `172.15`/`172.32` and `100.63`/`100.128` just outside their ranges, and the bridge test proves private targets (`0.0.0.0`, `[::1]`, the metadata address, RFC1918) are refused before any request is made. `isPrivateIP` **81% → 100%**, `HTTPRequest` **75%**.
- **`LogConfigChange` wrote secret values into the audit log.** The details string paired the values with the keys `old`/`new`, and `sanitizeDetails` only redacts by the key it sees — so a change to a sensitive field (for example `jwt_secret`) recorded **both the old and the new secret** in a long-lived, admin-readable, chain-hashed table. The helper has no callers today, which makes this latent rather than exploitable, but it is exported and any future settings handler would have inherited the leak. Sensitive field names (`secret`, `password`, `key`, `token`, `credential`, `cookie`, `session`, matched case-insensitively and deliberately broadly) now record `values redacted (sensitive field)` while still naming the field that changed; a non-sensitive field keeps `old=… new=…` so tuning changes stay useful. The three operator-audit helpers are covered (**100%** each) including the nil-context `system` attribution and that every row carries its chain hash.
- **The unauthenticated payload download is pinned against traversal.** `handleServePayload` (the `/payloads/:id/:filename` route, which needs no login so a stager can be fetched) was at 0% and builds its path from two URL segments. The test drives five traversal shapes — `id=".."`, `../../../config.yaml`, Windows separators, `....//`, a dot-rooted path — against a decoy `config.yaml` planted above the payloads root, and asserts only the refusal bodies come back (`forbidden`, `Invalid path`, `not found`); no bypass was found, the containment check holds. The missing-payload branch now answers `not found` with a body instead of a bare `c.Status(404)`, which keeps the status observable on a recorder and gives clients something to log. `handleServePayload` **0 → 100%**.
- **The integrations view of the webhook table was not tenant-scoped, and its rows never fired.** Two handlers manage the same `WebhookConfig` table: the automation one was scoped correctly, the `/api/integrations` one was not. A probe showed an integration created by a tenant-1 operator was stored with `tenant_id = 0`, and because webhook delivery matches `tenant_id = event.TenantID`, that integration **silently never fired** for any tenant-scoped event. The same handlers listed every tenant's webhooks and let `findOrFail`/an unscoped delete act on another tenant's row — a tenant-1 admin could retarget or delete a tenant-2 webhook, redirecting that tenant's event payloads (hostnames, findings). Creation now stamps the caller's tenant, the list goes through `tenantScope`, update/toggle use `findTenantOrFail`, and delete is tenant-scoped with a 404 when nothing matched. The new test proves stamping, list isolation, and that a foreign row cannot be toggled, updated or deleted.
- **The generated payload URL trusted the Host header, and the Host ends up inside the generated commands.** `handleGenerateOneLiner` built the download URL as `scheme://<request Host>/payloads/...` and interpolated it, unescaped, into every one-liner it returns (PowerShell single-quoted strings, `certutil`, `curl`). A spoofed Host therefore either pointed the operator's download at another server or broke out of the quoting entirely — e.g. `evil.example'; Start-Process calc; '`. A new `sanitizeRequestHost` accepts only hostname[:port] and bracketed IPv6 and rejects quotes, spaces, `;`, `$`, backticks and out-of-range ports; the handler answers 400 for an invalid Host, and the WebRTC TURN URL builder uses the same validator instead of trusting `c.Request.Host` verbatim. Covered by a 21-case accept/reject matrix plus a handler test that posts an injected Host and asserts nothing reaches the response.
### Security

- **User management was not tenant-scoped, and two paths were exploitable.** A probe showed a tenant-1 admin's `/users` page payload contained tenant-2 accounts, `handleAddUser` created rows with `tenant_id = 0` (the legacy unscoped bucket every tenant can see), and `handleSetUserPassword` updated `password_hash` **by id with no scope** — so a tenant-1 admin could reset a tenant-2 user's password and sign in as them. The page now lists through `tenantScope`, creation stamps the caller's tenant, and every user lookup uses `findTenantOrFail` (a foreign id answers 404). A same-tenant reset still works and revokes the target's sessions.
- **The tenant lookup in `handleAddUser` deadlocked its own transaction.** The first version resolved the tenant inside `s.db.Transaction`: the nested query waits for a connection the transaction already holds (a single-connection SQLite pool), and the test hung for three minutes instead of failing. The tenant is resolved before the transaction opens.

- **Six 5xx responses echoed raw internal errors, and a gate now prevents it.** The generation handlers answered 500 with `err.Error()` for v3 registration-secret and implant-config failures, and the reverse-port-forward bind error included the raw socket message — all of which can name key material, absolute paths or local addresses. They now log the real error and answer with `sanitizeError`. `scripts/check-error-sanitization.mjs` (`check:error-sanitization` in `npm run check`) walks every `respondError(..., StatusInternalServerError, …)` call and fails when the message is a raw error rather than a literal or a sanitizer; verified in both directions with a probe file. It scanned 1,277 5xx call sites clean after the change.
- **`POST /api/update-check/refresh` was open to every authenticated role.** It forces an outbound GitHub API call and rewrites the update state, but was registered on the root auth group with no permission middleware, so a viewer could trigger unlimited egress; it now requires `settings.write` (the status endpoints stay readable). A router audit cross-checked all 374 mutating routes against their group middleware: the only remaining ungated ones are the two login endpoints and the self-service session extend/logout, which are intentional. A source-contract test keeps the permission middleware (and the admin requirement on hot-update) from being dropped in a refactor.

- **Two more role checks were blocklists.** `handleRerunTask` only refused the literal `viewer`, so a request with no `user_role` in context — or any unknown role — could clone a task and queue it as a new write; it now uses `requireOperator`. `userCanViewScreen` (the dashboard socket's live-frame fan-out) had the same shape and now goes through `roleGrantsOperator`, so an unknown role cannot receive screen frames even if a permission lookup were to drift. Both are pinned by regression tests covering the no-role, viewer and unknown-role cases.
- **Three password-confirmation handlers resolved the caller unsafely.** `handleEmergencyStop`, `handleKillSwitch` and `handleChangePassword` did `s.db.First(&user, c.Get("user_id"))`. When the context had no `user_id` the condition was a nil interface, and GORM then matched the **first row of the users table**, so the supplied password was verified against that arbitrary account. A probe test proved it: posting the bootstrap admin's password with no principal armed the fleet kill switch (HTTP 200, every online implant got a `kill_date`). All three now resolve the principal through `currentUserID` and answer 401 when it is absent; regression tests assert nothing is armed or rotated without it.
- **`requireOperator` failed open when the role was absent.** The gate was a blocklist — `if role == "viewer" || role == ""` — but `c.Get` returns a nil interface when the key is missing, and comparing that to a string is always false, so **any request whose context carried no `user_role` was treated as an operator**. It also let any unknown role string through. The check now requires a role that (a) is `admin`/`user`/the legacy `operator` alias, or (b) exists in `custom_roles`, and denies `viewer`, `guest`, the empty role and anything unverified (a DB error denies too). Twelve existing tests were passing *because of* the bypass — they never set a role — and now set one explicitly; the role matrix is pinned by a new table-driven test.

### CI
- **The first-paint bundle budget had 292 kB of slack, not the ~122 kB its comment promised.** `check-bundle.mjs` was tightened to 1090 kB when the critical path measured 968 kB; the path has since fallen to **798 kB**, so the guard had quietly loosened to the point where **a 200 kB route chunk could start leaking again and still pass** — which is precisely the regression the budget exists to catch. It is now **900 kB** (~100 kB of ordinary entry/vendor headroom). **Evidence:** the gate passes with `critical path 798kB (13 chunks, 0 route) under 900kB`; setting the limit to 700 kB makes it fail with `FAIL: first-paint critical path is 798kB — exceeds 700kB` and the list of pre-paint chunks, so the mechanism bites. The `AGENTS.md` ratchet section now carries the bundle row alongside the two coverage floors, plus the rule that generalises this and round 208: **when a budget's comment states a headroom, check that arithmetic against the current measurement.**
- **Both coverage floors had stopped ratcheting.** The frontend floor was **16.5%** against a measured **34.84%** — an 18-point gap, which meant **roughly half the frontend suite could be deleted without the check noticing**. The Go floor was 50 against a measured **52.8%** (it was set when coverage was 50.4%), so a 2.8-point regression would also have passed. The frontend floor is now **33.5** and the CI floor **51.5**, each keeping the ~1-point headroom the scripts already document, and both comments record today's measurement and the drift. **Evidence:** the frontend gate passes at 34.84%, and `COVERAGE_FLOOR=36` makes it fail with `FAIL: frontend statement coverage is 34.84% — below the 36% floor`, so the mechanism bites. A new `AGENTS.md` section tabulates both floors, where they live and the rule — raise in the same change that adds tests, lower only as part of it, never to make a regression pass.
- **The ATT&CK coverage page now has a guard instead of a comment promising honesty.** `handlers_mitre.go` carries a warning that earlier versions advertised "fantasy names (exec, schtasks_add, reg_add, steal_token, clear_logs, beacon, sleep …) which no agent handler implements", making techniques look covered by operations nobody could run — and nothing enforced it. Measured first: all **82** referenced task types resolve to a dispatchable spec today, so the claim holds; the new `check:mitre-coverage` keeps it that way by requiring every `TaskTypes` entry to exist in `pkg/protocol`'s spec list (the single source the console and the implant both read) and by rejecting a malformed technique id. It reports the honest split too — **32 techniques, 28 mapped, 4 explicitly uncovered**. Wired into both gate lists (22 tasks in `check`, 18 in `check:pre`), documented in `AGENTS.md`, and proven against a synthetic map naming `schtasks_add`: it fails and names the technique.
- **`check:context-keys` could not see a typed context read.** The gate matched `c.Get`, `c.GetString` and `c.MustGet` only, so `c.GetBool("auth_via_api_key")` — the read that decides whether an API key is treated as **scoped** or as a **legacy full-access key** — was invisible to exactly the guard that exists to catch a read without a writer. If that setter were renamed or dropped, `apiKeyScopes` would answer "not a key" for every scoped key and the scopes would silently stop applying; nothing would have failed. The gate now reads **every** context accessor (`GetBool`, `GetInt`, `GetUint`, `GetFloat64`, `GetTime`, `GetDuration`, the `GetStringMap*` family, `MustGet`, …) while leaving request accessors (`GetHeader`, `GetRawData`, `GetCookie`, `GetPostForm`, `GetQuery`) out, since they are not context keys. Eight keys are now verified against production writers — `auth_via_api_key` among them — and a synthetic file reading `c.GetBool("ghost_key")` fails with the key named.
- **`check:tx-queries` never saw a transaction whose handle was built by a call chain.** The gate matched `<receiver>.Transaction(` where every segment was a bare method name, so `s.db.WithContext(ctx).Transaction(func(tx *gorm.DB) error {…})` — the shape `audit.go` uses for the audit batch — matched **nothing**: not only were pool queries inside it unscanned, the block itself was never recognised as a transaction (27 blocks reported, 28 exist). The receiver pattern now allows an argument list per segment, and the **base handle** beneath the chain is flagged too, so `s.db.First(…)` inside a `WithContext(…).Transaction(…)` block fails the gate. Verified with a synthetic file: `internal\server\leak.go:6  s.db.First` fails, where the same file passed before the fix. No live violation exists — the audit block correctly uses `tx` — which is exactly the point: the guard now covers the shape that would have hidden one.
- **The `agent_status_events` 500 is now a gate, not a memory.** Last round's defect — `tenantScope` applied to a table with no tenant column — is invisible at review time and reads as a database error at runtime, so it gets its own static check: `check-tenant-scope` resolves the model behind every `tenantScope`/`auditTenantScope` call (from the scope line, its statement, the query's destination variable, or the function's only table) and fails when that model has no `TenantID`. Wire-up is the part that matters here: it is a task in **both** local lists, and adding it to one list without the other is exactly the label/task mismatch this repository shipped once before — the first wiring attempt put `scopes` in the label list while the task list still had twenty entries, and `concurrently` would have run the gates misaligned; the counts are now asserted equal (21/21 and 17/17). Resolution is deliberately conservative: **141** call sites resolve and are checked against **78** models, and the **4** that cannot be resolved (the generic wrappers and `multitenant.go` itself) are **printed** rather than guessed — guessing is how the original bug passed review. Proven in both directions: the tree passes, and a synthetic file carrying the original shape fails with `db.AgentStatusEvent has no TenantID column`.
- **The docs-freshness check ran in CI but not in the local gates.** `gen-command-reference.mjs --check` and `gen-capability-matrix.mjs --check` had a dedicated CI step and were absent from **both** `npm run check` and `check:pre` — so a stale generated doc passed every local run and only failed minutes later in CI. They are now one `check:docs` task wired into both lists (20 tasks / 16 in `check:pre`). Its real scope is documented next to it in `AGENTS.md`, because the name promises more than it does: it verifies the generated command reference and the **generated task-inventory block** plus the `as of vX.Y.Z` stamp inside `docs/CAPABILITY_MATRIX.md`; the **hand-written per-OS tables are not verified** — I proved that by flipping a `Yes` to `No` in one of them and watching the check pass. Failure was proven where it does apply: editing the inventory count inside the generated markers and the version stamp both exit 1 with "task inventory is stale", then the file was restored.
- **The plugin quota numbers in the docs were accurate but unguarded.** `docs/PLUGIN_TIERS.md` and `config.example.yaml` promise plugin authors how many runs proceed at once, how much CPU and disk one run may consume, how long it may take and how much memory the WASM tier gets. All five matched the constants when checked — and nothing held them there. A drift would be invisible: the docs keep reading as authoritative while the server enforces something else. `TestTierNumbersMatchTheCode` parses both files and compares each number against the constant that enforces it (`defaultMaxConcurrentPlugins`, `defaultPluginCPUQuota`, `defaultPluginWriteQuota`, `maxPluginTimeoutSecs`, `wasmMemoryPages × 64 KiB`), and **fails when a documented phrase no longer matches the expected shape**, so a rewording cannot silently turn the check into a no-op. Proven by drifting the CPU budget text to 90 s and watching it fail with `says 90, the code enforces 120`, then restoring the file.
- **Recorded the defect shapes this audit kept finding, and where each guard lives.** Fifteen rounds produced tests and gates, but the *pattern* behind them was only in the changelog, so the next person would have had to rediscover it. `AGENTS.md` now has a "Recurring defect shapes" table mapping each shape to the tests that enforce it — reload/environment drift, config silently dropped or deleted, a tenant filter plus a sort the index cannot serve, a comment that describes the opposite of the code, a guarantee that lived only in a comment, a failure path that un-did its own protection, a local failure treated as tampering, an id lookup with no visibility check, and a hot path re-doing work per request. It closes with the two habits that found them — **measure before claiming** (the handler benchmark was noise; the isolated query was the evidence) and **when a guard fails, first ask whether the guard is wrong** (three times the expectation was wrong, once the code was) — with the specific examples, so the advice is checkable rather than decorative.
- **The dispatch claim path now has a concurrency test — the one place where a bug means a command runs on the wrong host.** `fetchPendingTasks` claims rows inside a transaction with a conditional status update and returns exactly the rows it claimed; nothing tested that two agents beaconing in the same instant cannot both receive the same task. The new test seeds six tasks per agent, fires **eight concurrent fetches across two agents**, and asserts: no task is ever handed to more than one agent, no pending row is left stranded, and every claimed row's `claimed_by` equals its `agent_id`. It also pins the single-response dedupe (the reason the code returns what it claimed instead of re-querying "everything running") and the limit being honoured on a second fetch that re-carries an unacknowledged task. Redelivery to the **same** agent is deliberately allowed — lost-response recovery depends on it, and the agent dedupes by task ID — so the assertion is per pair of agents, not "each task appears once". Runs clean three times plus once under `-race`. `fetchPendingTasks` **86%**.
- **The stale-task sweep's rules are now tested, including the one that prevents double execution.** Three paths decide what happens to work an agent never finished, and none of them had a test: a task claimed but never acknowledged is requeued to `pending` with its attempt counter incremented **and `claimed_at` cleared** (a stale claim left in place would make the next claim look like a retry); a task already at the **3-attempt cap fails instead of cycling forever**; and a legacy `sent` row — which nothing creates or claims any more — is **failed, never requeued**, because resurrecting an ancient command would redeliver a stale instruction to a live agent. The important one is the guard the code comment describes: the sweep SELECTs, then UPDATEs, so a result landing in between used to flip a **completed** task back to `pending` and execute it twice; the test asserts the UPDATE's status guard matches zero rows and the completed row keeps its result. Also covered: `failStaleAcknowledgedTasks` on an acknowledged-but-silent task (it must end up either failed **with a reason** or renewed — never running with no deadline and no attention), and the long-sleep filter that gives slow agents `max(static, 3×interval)`. `requeueStaleTasks` and `failStaleAcknowledgedTasks` **100%**, `filterLongSleepTasks` **92%**.
- **New gate `check:task-docs`: a registered task handler must be documented.** `internal/payload/agent` registers 169 task types in `taskHandlers`, and nothing tied them to `docs/COMMAND_REFERENCE.md` — so the next handler added would be a feature nobody could find. The gate greps the registry files, greps the doc table, and fails on any registered type with no row; it passes today (169/169, 222 documented types). It is deliberately **one-directional**: 53 documented types are handled by the core dispatcher or the C implant rather than by that registry (`shell`, `ls`, `download`, `set_sleep`, …), so demanding a handler for every row would fail on legitimate entries — the reason is in the script header. Proven the way a gate must be: I dropped a temporary `taskHandlers["undocumented_probe_type"]` into the tree, watched it fail with the exact type named, removed it, and watched it pass — including **through `npm run check`**, where the first version broke because the path was cwd-relative and npm runs gates from `frontend/` (now resolved from the script location). Wired into **both** `check` (19 tasks) and `check:pre` (15) and documented in `AGENTS.md`.
- **Pinned the release-signature interop between the signing tool and the updater.** `release.yml` signs each `<bin>.sha256` file and the updater fetches `<bin>.sha256` plus `<bin>.sha256.sig`, verifying the signature over the **exact checksum bytes** with the signature read as hex text from an HTTP body. Both packages had their own round-trip tests and **neither would notice a drift between them** — the failure mode is the worst kind: releases publish fine, then every hot update is refused in the field. A cross-tool test now reproduces the updater's steps against a real `signFile` output: the fresh signature verifies, a **trailing newline** on the signature body (an HTTP artefact) still verifies because of the trim, a single changed byte of the checksum file does not, and an unrelated key does not. I also confirmed the two sides agree on **which file** is signed — the workflow signs `"$bin.sha256"` and the updater picks `binaryAsset.Name + ".sha256"`, not "whichever `.sha256` is in the release". `verifyFile` **93%**, `signFile` **80%**.
- **The `plugin-check` CLI contract is now tested instead of only documented.** `AGENTS.md` and the tool's own header both promise "exit code 0 means no error-level findings; warnings do not fail", and every agent or script calling it depends on that — but nothing verified it. The run body moved behind `runCLI(args, stdout, stderr) int` (a writer wall, no process spawn), and the tests pin the contract: warnings-only exits **0** with an `OK:` summary, an error-level finding exits **1** with the failing code named (`package.entry_missing`), a missing directory argument exits **2** with usage on stderr, a **flag typo** exits **2** rather than looking like a package failure, and `-require-signed` fails an unsigned package. `-json` is pinned too: a failing package still emits a parseable report with its stable diagnostic codes and no empty messages, which is what an agent loop consumes. `runCLI` **91%**, `cmd/plugin-check` **75.4%**.
- **Three servers starting at once against the same file leave a correct database.** The deploy scripts can produce a restart racing a start, so the migrations run concurrently on one SQLite file. The test starts **three** `InitDBWithDriver` calls together and asserts the outcome rather than the mechanics: the process does not panic, at least one start succeeds, the schema is fully applied (not half), and the admin is seeded **exactly once** — no duplicate row from two racers both deciding to seed. All three racers actually succeeded on this machine, which is the SQLite busy-timeout doing its job. The test uses `os.MkdirTemp` rather than `t.TempDir` on purpose: closing every pool is best effort on Windows, and failing the assertion because cleanup could not unlink a file would be the wrong failure. `internal/db` **62.4% → 63.1%**.
- **Two lifecycle contracts are now pinned instead of assumed.** (1) The PostgreSQL branch of `InitDBWithDriver` cannot be exercised without a real server, so the test pins its **failure contract**: a broken DSN returns an error rather than panicking, and the error **does not echo the DSN** — which carries the database password (it was already safe, because the driver reports host/port; now it cannot regress silently). (2) `Shutdown` must **drain tracked workers before closing the database**: a worker registered on `s.wg` is cancelled through `s.ctx` and waited for, `Shutdown` is idempotent, and a Server built without `Run` (nil context, no listeners) survives it instead of panicking. `Shutdown` **100%**, `internal/db` keeps its Postgres branch covered at the failure end.
- **Documented (and made diagnosable) a worktree trap that cost me a build.** I checked whether a local Windows bundle could be committed: `npm run build` succeeded, and `check:webdist` then failed with **383 changed files** — because the working tree carries another agent's uncommitted frontend sources, so `frontend/out` no longer represents the committed `internal/webdist/dist`. The bundle is gitignored, so nothing was at risk in git, but the failure reads as "dist is stale" when the truth is "out is not the committed bundle". The gate now prints exactly that distinction and the two commands that restore `out/` from `dist/` **without touching anyone's work**, and `AGENTS.md` records the rule (never commit a dist built from a tree with someone else's changes). Restored the local `out/` from the committed bundle; all 18 gates are green again.
- **Full verification pass, and a typo the pass caught.** All 11 Go packages are green under the CI profile (`-coverprofile`), total coverage **52.3%** (was 52.1%), and `npm run check` runs **18** concurrent gates with no failures. The pass also caught a typo I introduced while wiring the gates: one task list had `npm:check:i` instead of `npm:check:i18n`, so the i18n parity check was silently skipped by `check:pre` (the script that CI runs) while `check` itself still ran it. Both lists now name all 14 tasks, verified by parsing `package.json` rather than by trusting the concurrent output. The CI step comment, which still claimed "9 static checks", now states 14 and names the five Go gates.
- **The four Go structural gates were never actually run.** `check:route-auth`, `check:context-keys`, `check:error-sanitization` and `check:tx-queries` each exist, are documented as part of `npm run check`, and their labels were still listed in `concurrently -n` — but they had been dropped from the **task list** of both `check` and the `check:pre` script CI runs. They enforced nothing. All four are wired back in (plus the new gate below), so `npm run check` and CI now fail on a new unauthenticated route, an unset gin context key, a raw `err.Error()` in a 5xx, or a pool query inside a transaction.
- **New gate: `check:command-separators`.** A function that joins a task command with `"|"` must validate its fields first (`validateCommandArg` or `credentialCommandHasSeparator`). This class shipped **four times** (password_spray re-targeted the domain, cred_check probed the wrong account and bypassed the lockout fuse, persistence removed a different entry, uac_bypass ran a different payload), each fixed by hand; the gate now makes the fifth a build failure. It reports 15 pipe-joined builders — 7 validated, 8 allowlisted with a reason (they build in-memory keys, not wire commands) — so the allowlist doubles as a list of the places worth a second look. The password-spray handler was refactored onto the shared helper so the gate needs only one rule.
- **A backup test I added last week was flaky under coverage instrumentation.** `TestBackupManagerLifecycle` passed in isolation but failed in the CI-shaped run: `Stop` does not wait for a backup already in flight, so `t.TempDir`'s cleanup could race a file the background goroutine still held (Windows refuses to unlink an open file, and `-coverprofile` slows the goroutine down enough to expose it). The test now takes the manager's run mutex after `Stop`, which guarantees the in-flight backup finished before cleanup — no sleeping, no retry, and it holds under `-count=5` and in the coverage-instrumented suite. The CI floor is unchanged **52.1%** measured locally (floor 50).
- **The IOC list and export endpoints are covered, including the spreadsheet-injection guard.** `handleListIOCs` and `handleExportIOCs` were at 0% and `csvSafe` at 0%. The listing test extracts indicators from a task result (so the `AfterFind` decryption path is exercised), checks that the `type` filter **narrows and never widens** the list, and that a nonsense `days` value falls back to the default instead of failing. The export test pins both shapes: CSV gets its header, an `attachment` disposition and its values, while the STIX 2.1 export is a valid `bundle--` document containing both an `indicator--` object and the TLP marking definition. `csvSafe` is covered because opening an export in Excel executes any cell starting with `=`, `+`, `-` or `@` — the test asserts those are quoted (`=cmd|' /C calc'!A0` included) while ordinary values such as `1.1.1.1` and `a,b` pass through untouched. `handleListIOCs` **80%**, `handleExportIOCs` **79%**, `csvSafe` **100%**; `internal/server` **65.2% → 65.3%**.
- **Key erasure and the vault plaintext sweep are covered.** `Wipe` and `EncryptLootPlaintext` were at 0%. `Wipe` is best-effort key erasure, so a no-op would leave live key material on the heap: the test asserts every byte is actually zeroed (across lengths 0/1/7/16/31/64/129), that wiping a sub-slice does **not** touch its neighbours, and that a nil slice is a no-op rather than a panic. `EncryptLootPlaintext` is the vault sweep's migration step, and its contract is "only genuine plaintext is touched": a legacy value is sealed and reported `changed=true` (round-trips back), an already-sealed value passes through **unchanged and unre-written** (the sweep is idempotent), an empty value stays empty, and undecryptable ciphertext is an **error** — re-sealing it would destroy the only copy of the original plaintext. `DeriveSessionKey`'s missing branches are covered too: deterministic, 32 bytes, distinct per agent, never equal to the shared secret, nil for an empty secret. `internal/crypto` **69.9% → 72.2%**.
- **The crypto key derivations are covered, including what each MAC binds.** `DeriveRegistrationKey`/`DeriveRegistrationKeyFromHex`, `ComputeRegHMAC`, `DeriveFileChainKey`/`FileChunkMAC` and `DeriveKillSwitchKey`/`KillSwitchHMAC` were at 0%. These are the keys behind enrollment, chunked file transfer and the kill switch, and the tests pin the properties that matter rather than just executing them: derivation is deterministic and yields 32 bytes, **two agents never share a key**, the derived key never equals the master, and an empty master/key returns nil instead of a usable key (the fail-closed shape every caller relies on). The handshake MAC binds agent, identity key, timestamp **and sequence** — so a captured registration frame cannot be replayed with an inflated seq — and the file chain breaks on a changed chunk, a dropped previous link, a swapped order or another agent's key. The kill-switch tag is bound to both the per-implant key and the token, so one implant's tag never verifies for another. `internal/crypto` **61.0% → 69.9%**; the key-derivation functions are now **86–100%**.
- **CS profile diagnostics are covered on the operator-facing side.** `ErrorMessages`, `WarningMessages`, `HasErrors` and `ParseCSFull` were at 0%. The views are now pinned: an error carries `line:column` (so the editor can point at the mistake) while a warning carries just its message, a warning-only parse is **not** reported as having errors, and an empty result produces nothing. `ParseCSFull` returns the **partial profile together with the error** for a broken block — the editor needs the partial profile to render a live preview — and its message names the first problem. `internal/malleable` **77.7% → 78.8%**.
- **Database bootstrap and DSN redaction are covered.** `InitDB`, `withMigrationTx`, `migrationRowExists` and `redactDSN` were all at 0%. The bootstrap test runs the **real** migration path against a temp file: the core tables are created, the default admin is seeded **exactly once**, and running the bootstrap a second time (every server restart does) neither errors nor duplicates the seed. `withMigrationTx` is pinned to roll back a failed step completely — a half-applied migration without its ID row made the next startup replay the step and die on duplicate keys — and `migrationRowExists` gives data migrations their rerun idempotency. `redactDSN` is covered because a DSN carries a live password: `password=`/`pwd=`/`PASSWORD=` are masked while host/user/dbname survive, so the log line stays useful without leaking the credential. `internal/db` **60.6% → 62.3%**.
- **The plugin manager's operator switches are covered.** `SetMaxConcurrent`/`SetMaxCPUSeconds`/`SetMaxWriteMB`, `SetEnabled`, `Unregister` and `LoadFailures` were all at 0%. The quota setters are pinned to change **one** knob each and to ignore a non-positive value, so an operator typo (or a `0` meant as "unlimited") cannot silently disable a protection that is already in place. `SetEnabled` moves the database record and the in-memory flag together — a disabled plugin must be refused immediately, not after a restart — and an unknown name is an error rather than a silent no-op. `Unregister` must actually tear down: the plugin leaves memory, **its `Shutdown` is called**, its row is deleted, and its directory removed. `LoadFailures` returns a **copy** (a caller cannot mutate manager state) and a successful load clears the previous failure. `internal/plugin` **64.0% → 67.0%**.
- **Plugin resource quotas can no longer be silently reset.** `clone`, `withMaxConcurrent`, `withCPUQuota`, `withWriteQuota`, `acquire`/`release` were at 0%. These setters each change **one** knob, and the trap they guard against is a setter that quietly resets another budget (the CPU watchdog or the write cap) while looking like it only changed concurrency. The tests pin that: the clone shares the slot channel and keeps both budgets, `withMaxConcurrent` swaps the channel but keeps the budgets (and falls back to the default for a non-positive cap), `withCPUQuota`/`withWriteQuota` keep the cap **and** share the channel, a non-positive budget returns the same executor unchanged, and the nil-executor clone is safe. Backpressure is covered too: a saturated executor **honours context cancellation** instead of queueing plugin work without bound, and `release` frees exactly one slot. `internal/plugin` **62.9% → 64.0%**.
- **The kill switch's per-implant authentication tag is now asserted, not just executed.** The fleet kill switch attaches a token plus an HMAC derived from **that implant's** registration key, so an old or replayed broadcast cannot be used against another agent. The new test pins the whole chain: nothing is attached while disarmed; while armed the token and MAC are present; the MAC **equals** the one derived from this implant's key; a different implant derives a **different** MAC for the same token; and an armed switch with an empty token attaches nothing (nothing to authenticate). Kill-date behaviour was already covered by an earlier test, so the duplicate I wrote was removed rather than left to drift.
- **Beacon replay protection, invalid-id rejection and protocol refusal are covered — with a control that proves they reach their branch.** `processBeaconWithBudget` was at 53%. A replayed frame (fingerprint already in the dedupe cache) returns an **empty** response, so a replay cannot make the server hand out the same tasks again, and it creates no agent row; malformed agent ids (`""`, `not-a-uuid`, `../../etc/passwd`) are dropped before any DB or filesystem use; an agent speaking an unsupported protocol version gets `ProtocolRejected` with an explanation instead of a silent drop that would look "offline". My first version of these tests **passed for the wrong reason** — the placeholder UUIDs tripped the id guard, so nothing reached the branch under test; the fix was valid UUIDs plus a control case asserting that a valid beacon actually registers the agent. `processBeaconWithBudget` **53 → 62%**.
- **Beacon deduplication is covered, including what its fingerprint does *not* hash.** `beaconFingerprint` was at 52% and `isDuplicateBeacon` at 62%. The test mutates every field the fingerprint is meant to depend on (uuid, seq, result type/id/task/MAC/encoding/output/error/filename/size/offset/path, acks, SOCKS frames, relayed results) and requires the key to change each time — a field left out of the fingerprint means two different beacons collide and one is **dropped with its results and its tasks**. It also pins the documented limit: content past the 1 KiB prefix is **not** hashed, which is safe only because the result MAC is part of the key — both halves (same prefix+MAC collides by design; a different MAC or length does not) are asserted so the reliance is visible. The dedupe window, the new-seq exemption (a retransmission with a fresh seq must be served), per-agent separation, the nil-cache pass-through and the bounded cache are covered too. `beaconFingerprint` **52 → 85%**, `isDuplicateBeacon` **62 → 93%**; `internal/server` **65.0% → 65.1%**.
- **Enrollment refusals are covered: blocked implants and ghost registrations.** `processAgentRegistration` was at 64%. The tests pin the force-offline contract — a blocked implant is pinned `offline`, its check-in is audited with the blocked reason, it stays blocked (beaconing never un-blocks a row the way a soft delete is restored), and the caller receives no implant, so nothing is delivered to it — plus the tombstone rule (an agent deleted in the console that beacons again is restored instead of being locked out by its own tombstone) and the ghost guard (an enrollment with neither hostname nor IP creates no row). Writing them exposed a confusing contract that is now documented: the second return value means **"is new agent"**, not "accepted" — a refusal is signalled by an empty implant ID, and reading `false` as "rejected" would drop every returning agent's beacon. `processAgentRegistration` **64% → 71%**.
- **Build-job cleanup is covered, and its sweep is now callable without a 15-minute wait.** `abandonBuildJob` was at 0% and the cleanup pass only existed inside the ticker loop. The body was extracted to `sweepBuildJobsOnce()` (no behaviour change) and the tests pin: an old finished job is removed **with its output file**, a still-`building` job and a recent job stay, a job whose output is already on disk as missing does not abort the pass, and expired one-shot artifacts (stager/one-liner outputs that are not BuildJobs) are reaped. `abandonBuildJob` is covered for the queue-full case — a rejected job must not linger as a phantom failed build. `sweepBuildJobsOnce` **93%**, `abandonBuildJob` **100%**, `broadcastBulkAgentDeleteAlert` **100%**.
- **Scheduled backups are covered, including that a snapshot is encrypted and restorable.** `PerformBackup`/`Start`/`Stop` were at 0%/12%/0%. The tests pin the promise that matters at disaster time: a `.fbk` is written, it is **not** a plaintext SQLite file, it **decrypts back to a database that still contains the data**, and its sidecar names the key id so a restore can fail with an explicit key mismatch instead of a corrupt read. The constructor is fail-closed (no key, non-hex key or short key all refuse — an earlier random-key fallback produced backups that became permanently unrestorable after a restart), retention keeps exactly the newest N snapshots while leaving unrelated files alone, `Start` is idempotent, `PerformBackup` is a no-op while stopped, a manual run produces a snapshot, and `Stop` releases the ticker and is idempotent. `PerformBackup` **0 → 100%**, `Start` **12% → 92%**, `Stop` **0 → 100%**; `internal/server` **64.8% → 64.9%**.
- **Task callbacks are covered, and they now share the webhook SSRF gate.** `executeTaskCallback` was at 0%. A callback posts command output to an operator-supplied URL, so the tests pin both halves: the payload names the task and agent, carries the command/status/completion time, defaults the method to `POST`, and — the security-relevant part — a file-scan result is sent in its **redacted** form, with the test asserting the matched secret (`AKIA-…`) does not appear in the body. A callback aimed at a private address is refused and **nothing is delivered**. The callback path now validates through the same `webhookTargetValidator` seam the webhook delivery uses, so it cannot be the one outbound path that quietly skips the address policy (and the seam is what lets the delivery test reach a local receiver). `executeTaskCallback` **0 → 70%**; `internal/server` **64.7% → 64.8%**.
- **Screenshot persistence is covered, including the write path's containment.** `saveScreenshot` and `writeScreenshotFile` were at 0%. The tests pin the filename shape (`screenshot_<task>_<unix>.png` under `<dataDir>/screenshots/<agent>/`), the exact bytes written, owner-only mode, that an agent id which tries to escape the screenshots root writes **nothing**, that invalid base64 is a silent no-op rather than a corrupt file, and that a frame arriving while a screen stream is live is **relayed but not retained** (the documented live-monitoring rule). The mode assertion is skipped on Windows, where Go synthesises `0666` for any writable file and the check would only test the platform. The empty-`dataDir` fallback to `data/` is covered too. `saveScreenshot` **0 → 100%**, `writeScreenshotFile` **0 → 75%**; `internal/server` **64.6% → 64.7%**.
- **The ECDH session sweep is covered, including the long-sleep exemption.** `sweepECDHSessions` was at 0%. It evicts a session once the agent has been silent for `max(30m, 3×its sleep interval)`; the test pins all three outcomes — a chatty agent idle past the floor loses its session, an agent with no interval row falls back to the floor and also loses it, and a **long-sleep agent (1h interval) is kept** because it is still inside `3×` its interval. A second sweep with nothing idle is a no-op, and the early returns (no session manager, no database, empty session table) are silent. Reaching the eviction branch needs backdated sessions (the floor is 30 minutes), so `crypto.SessionManager` gained one narrow, documented `SetSessionLastUsedForTest` seam. `blockedReasonSuffix` is covered too: a blank reason produces no dangling `" ()"`. `sweepECDHSessions` **0 → 94%**, `blockedReasonSuffix` **100%**; `internal/server` **64.5% → 64.6%**.
- **The task-output vault contract is covered, including its fail-closed branch.** `encryptTaskFieldsOrBlank` was at 33% and `vaultNoteError` at 0%. The tests pin that with the vault available both `Result` and `Error` come back `FC2ENC:`-prefixed, and that a vault failure **drops the output** (`Result` emptied, a marker in `Error`, an error returned so the caller fails closed too) with the plaintext verified to be gone — storing command output as plaintext because encryption happened to be unavailable is the one outcome this path must never allow. `appendTaskResultTail` is covered too: it keeps the newest bytes and never exceeds its 64 KiB cap. Testing the failure branch needed a seam: `InitLootEncryption` deliberately never clears an existing key (stored loot must stay decryptable), so a test cannot make the real vault unavailable once the suite has initialised it — the single vault call is now a documented `var` that the test swaps and restores. `encryptTaskFieldsOrBlank` **33% → 100%**, `appendTaskResultTail` **100%**.
- **The outbound URL validator's port comments described a policy it did not implement.** `validateExternalURL` claimed "default deny for non-allowlisted custom ports" while the code allowed **every** port above 1024 — a reader would have believed in a protection that was not there. The comments now state the real policy (allowlist + a sensitive-port block list + privileged ports below 1024 refused; all other high ports allowed, because AI gateways legitimately listen on them), and a table test pins it exactly: the allowlisted ports, high ports such as `1234`/`65535` passing, and `22`/`25`/`3306`/`5432`/`6379`/`11211`/`808`/`1023`/`0`/`70000` refused, plus scheme and loopback checks. Tightening to a real allowlist was considered and rejected: it would break working AI-gateway configurations. `validateExternalURL` **95% → 97%**.
- **Profile validation is covered, including the CS auto-detection.** `looksLikeCS` was at 0% and `handleValidateProfile` at 55%. The detector decides which parser runs, so a false positive would push a JSON profile through the CS translator and a false negative the reverse: the test pins both directions (http-get/http-post/http-config, `set sleeptime`/`useragent`/`jitter`, and that JSON objects/arrays and plain notes are not CS). The handler tests cover the 400s (empty content, malformed JSON), a valid v2 profile coming back compiled, and — the contract that matters for the editor — **CS text is never a 400**: a minimal `http-get` block parses into a usable profile, while an unterminated block reports `valid:false` with diagnostics so the editor can render a partial preview. A missing semicolon is only a warning and keeps the profile valid, which the test records explicitly. `looksLikeCS` **100%**, `handleValidateProfile` **55% → 61%**.
- **Auto-tag rule CRUD is covered.** `handleAutoTagCreate`, `Update`, `Toggle` and `Delete` were all at 0%. The tests pin the create validation (name and tag required), that a rule without an id gets one and starts enabled, that update replaces name/priority while keeping the tag and 404s for an unknown id, that toggle flips `enabled`, and that delete removes the row with a second delete answering 404. `AutoTagRule` carries no `TenantID`, so unlike the integration webhooks this is a server-wide feature — verified rather than assumed. `handleAutoTagRules` **100%**, `Create` **65%**, `Update` **63%**, `Toggle` **67%**, `Delete` **78%**; `internal/server` **64.3% → 64.4%**.
- **Both relay accept loops are covered.** The SOCKS relay's `acceptLoop` was at 50% and the reverse-port-forward one at 47%. Each test binds a real loopback listener, drives a connection through it, and then stops the loop by closing the listener — requiring it to **return** rather than spin. The rportfwd loop is also covered for its guard case (a relay that never bound must return immediately, not panic on a nil listener). Both loops carry a documented failure mode worth protecting: a single transient accept error used to kill a relay permanently, and unbounded accepts are an FD-exhaustion primitive, so "the loop stops cleanly and keeps serving until told to stop" is the contract. SOCKS `acceptLoop` **50 → 58%**, rportfwd `acceptLoop` **47 → 63%**.
- **The SOCKS5 handshake refusals are covered.** `handleOperatorConn` was at 68%. The tests drive the real handshake over a loopback socket and pin the four refusals that happen before any relay exists: a non-SOCKS5 greeting gets **no reply** at all, an unsupported command (BIND) gets `0x07` (command not supported), an unknown address type gets `0x08`, and a session at `SocksMaxConns` gets `0x02` (connection not allowed) — the cap is exercised by pre-filling the connection map, so a session cannot silently exceed its limit. `stopSession` is also driven twice to prove the teardown is idempotent. `handleOperatorConn` **68% → 73%**. The test drives a real loopback socket rather than `net.Pipe`: a pipe has no buffering, and the first version deadlocked with the server writing its method reply while the client wrote the request.
- **The login-path lockout wrappers are covered.** `checkLoginLockout`, `recordLoginFailure`, `checkAccountLockout` and `recordAccountLoginFailure` sat between 55% and 84%. The tests pin the nil-tracker behaviour (a server without a tracker never reports a lock — refusing every login would be worse than no lockout), the account round-trip (record → check → `resetAccount`), the per-IP round-trip (lock → check → `clearLoginLockout`), and the **whitelist bypass**: a configured IP is never locked even after repeated failures, while a non-whitelisted one still locks. `checkAccountLockout` and `checkLoginLockout` **100%**, `recordLoginFailure` **88%**, `recordAccountLoginFailure` **84%**, `isAccountLocked` **89%**; `internal/server` **64.2% → 64.3%**.
- **Webhook delivery is covered, signature included.** `executeWebhook` was at 11%. The tests pin that the default method is `POST`, that custom headers and the `ForgeC2-Automation/1.0` user agent land on the request, that the body is the event JSON, and that a configured secret produces an HMAC-SHA256 **over the exact bytes the receiver got**, in `X-ForgeC2-Signature` — and that no signature header is sent when no secret is configured (an empty HMAC would be a lie the receiver cannot detect). A second test proves the SSRF gate still refuses a private-address target before any request leaves the server. Delivery needed a local receiver, so `webhookTargetValidator` is now a documented var seam (the same pattern as `releaseAssetsURLFmt`); production calls the unchanged `validateWebhookURL`. `executeWebhook` **11% → 78%**.
- **The account-lockout rule is covered, including its anti-DoS half.** `recordAccountFailure` was at 35%. The tests pin the two-source rule: repeated failures for one username from a **single** address never lock the account — that attacker is throttled by the per-IP lockout, and letting one source lock a username would be a trivial denial of service against any operator — while failures from **two** addresses do lock it, with `retryAfter` equal to the lockout window. Also covered: a locked account keeps reporting the lock with a positive retry, it becomes usable again once the window passes, failures older than the sliding window do not accumulate, and the account map is bounded by `maxLockoutEntries` (verified by filling it one past the cap). The per-IP path is covered too (lock, re-check, reset). `recordAccountFailure` **35% → 93%**; `internal/server` **64.1% → 64.2%**.
- **Result idempotency is covered, including the cache bound.** `isDuplicateResult` was at 39%. A re-sent result id is deduplicated within the window; a different result id, the same id from a *different* agent, and an empty id are all treated as new work — an empty id must never be deduplicated or legacy results would silently vanish. An entry past `BeaconDedupWindow` is accepted again, and the cache is proven bounded: past `MaxBeaconDedupEntries` it evicts, which is what stops a long engagement from leaking memory. `isDuplicateResult` **39% → 82%**; `internal/server` **64.0% → 64.1%**.
- **Kill-date enforcement is covered.** `enforceKillDate` was at 14%, which is a dangerous place for a low number: it is what stops an implant at the end of an engagement. The test pins all four behaviours — no kill date does nothing, a future kill date does nothing, a past kill date injects exactly one `kill` task **persisted as `running`** so the agent picks it up on its next check-in, and a second render that already carries the kill task does not duplicate it. Both failure directions matter: a missed kill leaves an implant running past the engagement, a spurious one destroys an agent mid-operation. `enforceKillDate` **14% → 71%**; the measured CI total is **50.9%** (floor stays 50 for cross-platform headroom).
- **Agent deletion is verified to purge every dependent table.** `deleteAgentRecordTx` deletes from thirteen tables and then the implant itself; nothing tested that the list is complete. The test seeds one row per table for two agents, deletes one, and requires every dependent row to be gone **while the other agent's rows survive** — tasks, credentials, tokens, SOCKS sessions, scan results, network hosts, tag/group assignments, notifications, locks, opsec history, BloodHound results and mesh peers, plus the mesh rows where the victim appears as the *peer*. The implant row must be hard-deleted (checked with `Unscoped`), not soft-deleted, or the reconcile path can resurrect it.
- **The intermittent `TestRDWebRTCSignalOperatorLoop` failure was a test-side race, not a product bug.** The handler writes the `waiting` frame **before** appending the offer to its pending buffer, so the test's immediate `len(pending)` read could observe 0. It now polls briefly for the buffer to fill. Two consecutive full runs after the change are clean; the previously-observed one-off failures in this suite are consistent with the same window.
- **Screenshot serving is pinned against path traversal.** `handleServeScreenshot` was at 0% and `handleListAgentScreenshots` at 62%. No vulnerability was found — the handler already combines an extension allowlist, `safeJoin` containment and a symlink-resolving re-check — but none of it was tested, and a regression here is an arbitrary file read. The test drives six traversal shapes (`../`, `..\..\`, absolute paths, nested `sub/../../`, encoded separators) and asserts none serves a file outside the screenshot root, that a non-image extension is refused even when contained, and that another tenant's agent answers 404 before any file handling. The listing test covers an empty directory, a page past the end (no slice panic) and the same tenant gate. `handleServeScreenshot` **0 → 86%**, `handleListAgentScreenshots` **62 → 81%**; `internal/server` **63.9% → 64.0%**.
- **The online → stale → offline transition is covered end to end.** `checkAgentAlerts` was at 59%. The test pins the three bands (an agent seen 5 seconds ago stays `online`, one quiet for two minutes becomes **`stale`**, one quiet for half an hour becomes `offline`), that a status event is written per transition and none for the healthy agent, and that the offline rule alerts on its **own** threshold — which is why a two-minute agent alerts while its status is only `stale`; the rule threshold and the status bucket are deliberately independent. A second tick is verified not to duplicate the active alert. This is the regression guard for the stale-threshold unit bug fixed last round: with a nanosecond stale band the middle transition never happened. `checkAgentAlerts` **59% → 67%**.
- **AI run cancellation and event streaming are covered.** `handleAIRunCancel`, `handleAIRunEvents` and `aiRunTerminal` were all at 0%. The cancel path marks the run `cancelled` **and** cancels its pending execution intents (recording `decided_by`), a second cancel on a terminal run is a no-op success, and another owner gets **404** — the run is invisible, not forbidden. The stream test drives the SSE replay of a finished run: events arrive in sequence order with `text/event-stream`, and a `Last-Event-ID` cursor skips what the client already had (a replayed event would duplicate the transcript). `handleAIRunCancel` **100%**, `aiRunTerminal` **100%**, `handleAIRunEvents` **64%**; `internal/server` **63.7% → 63.9%**.
- **Listener CRUD is covered, including the truthful-status and tenant contracts.** `handleCreateListener` was at 63% and `handleUpdateListener` at 47%. The tests pin the pre-bind refusals (a scheme the server never binds, an out-of-range port, an invalid host), that a DNS listener takes its domain from `Host` (otherwise it is born with an empty `DNSDomain`, keys as `dns://` and never binds), and the status contract: when the bind cannot happen — the port is already held — the row is still created but marked **`stopped`**, never `running`. Tenant isolation is pinned across all four verbs: creation stamps the caller's tenant even when the body tries to set `tenant_id`, the list hides foreign rows, and update/delete of a foreign id answer 404 with the row verified untouched. An update re-derives scheme/protocol/type. `handleCreateListener` **63% → 77%**, `handleUpdateListener` **47% → 57%**; `internal/server` **63.5% → 63.7%**.
- **A fifth ratchet now covers error bodies, and the listener delete path uses the envelope.** The success envelope has had a ratchet for a while; failures did not, so a handler could answer `c.JSON(4xx, gin.H{"error": …})` and hand the frontend a body shape the contract does not promise. `handlers_listeners.go` did exactly that in two places (the agent-count failure and the "N agents still using this listener" refusal) — both now answer the envelope, and the refusal keeps its `agent_count` field. `TestErrorEnvelope` counts raw error responses per file against `envelope_error_baseline.txt` (24 across 8 files, `extc2.go` carrying the documented protocol exception) and fails in **both** directions: a new raw error, or a file that improved without lowering its number. Verified with a probe handler that it fails as intended. `normalizeListenerProtocol` **52% → 75%**, `listenerKey` **57% → 100%**.
- **The hot-update rollback snapshot and the download step are covered.** `snapshotDatabaseForUpdate` was at 0% and `downloadUpdateFile` at 76%. The snapshot tests pin the no-op cases (a non-sqlite driver, an empty database path) and the real path: a sqlite database is copied with `VACUUM INTO`, and the copy is verified to be a **usable database containing the data** — a corrupt or empty backup would be worse than none during a rollback. The download tests pin that a 200 writes the exact bytes and reaches `downloading/100`, and that a non-200 fails **without leaving a partial file** behind. `snapshotDatabaseForUpdate` **0 → 76%**, `downloadUpdateFile` **76 → 85%**; `internal/server` 63.5%.
- **The AI approval gate is covered, so a dangerous tool cannot be approved across tenants or without its permission.** `findApprovableAIIntent`, `handleAIIntentApprove`, `handleAIIntentReject` and `handleAIIntentsList` were all at 0%. The tests pin that an intent belonging to another tenant answers **404 and stays pending**, an already-decided intent is a **409** rather than a re-execution, and an intent carrying a `RequiredPerm` the caller does not hold is refused — the row is verified still `pending` after each refusal. The reject path marks the intent decided (with `decided_by`) and refuses a second decision; the pending list returns only the caller's tenant. `findApprovableAIIntent` **87%**, `handleAIIntentReject` **73%**, `handleAIIntentsList` **74%**; `internal/server` **63.3% → 63.5%**.
- **Agent note/tag writes and agent deletion are pinned to the caller's tenant.** `handleUpdateNote` was at 50% and `deleteAgentRecordTx` at 55%. The tests prove a foreign tenant's agent answers **404** for both the note write and the delete, and the row is verified unchanged (notes/tags) or still present afterwards — the note handler's unscoped update was a fixed IDOR, so this is the regression guard. The owner path covers both the JSON and form bodies, the length cap on notes/tags, a no-op request, and that deleting an agent takes its **tasks and credentials** with it. `agentSortOrder` is also pinned, including that a hostile `sortDir` value cannot be injected (unknown directions degrade to `asc`). `handleUpdateNote` **72%**, `agentSortOrder` **100%**, `sortHostBuckets` **71%**.
- **Binary generation validates before it builds, and that is now pinned.** `parseBinaryForm` was at 52% and the four generate endpoints opened straight into a build. The tests cover each pre-build refusal — no listener/DNS/P2P config, an unknown listener id, mTLS material that cannot complete a handshake (cert with no key), an icon whose magic is neither ICO nor PNG, an oversized icon, and an unparsable failover URL — plus the accepting path (a PNG icon is re-encoded and round-trips byte for byte) and the beacon-key endpoint mirroring the configured value. All four entry points also reject a request with no form before touching the toolchain. `parseBinaryForm` **52% → 67%**, `profileAuditHash` and `handleGetBeaconKey` **100%**; `internal/server` **63.0% → 63.1%**.
- **Credential harvesting is covered, and the CI floor is ratcheted from 44 to 50.** `parseAndStoreCredentials` (the lsass/mimikatz text import) was at 57%. The tests pin tenant attribution (rows take the agent's tenant), the dedupe contract — an exact duplicate line inside one harvest is collapsed, a re-harvest of the same text adds nothing, and a domain-qualified `CORP\bob` is a **different** principal from `bob` and must be kept — plus the empty-input early return and the orphan-agent fallback in `agentTenantID`. `parseAndStoreCredentials` **57% → 74%**, `agentTenantID` **86%**; `internal/server` **62.9% → 63.0%**, and the measured CI total is **50.4%**, so the ratcheting floor moves **44 → 50** (the previous floor dated from 46.3%).
- **Every read-only AI tool is exercised once, with a tenant-leak assertion.** `executeToolSwitchCtx` dispatches all 31 tools from one switch and sat at 63%; the sweep calls the 20 read-only tools (agent detail, tasks, listeners, credentials, operators, task search, timeline, IOC, macros, attack surface, task detail, BloodHound, coverage gaps, pending tasks, screenshot/keylog summaries, situation, alerts, report) against a seeded store and requires a JSON result that contains **no** tenant-2 row — a sentinel agent is seeded for that check specifically. A second test pins the error envelope for an unknown tool and records that malformed arguments are **tolerated** (the tool runs with empty args) rather than failing the turn. `executeToolSwitchCtx` **63% → 72%**; `internal/server` **62.7% → 62.9%**.
- **AI chat sessions are covered, including their ownership contract.** `handleAISessionsList`/`Create`/`Update`/`Delete` were at 0%. The tests pin that a user only ever sees and mutates **their own** sessions — another user's id answers 404 for get, update and delete, and the row is verified unchanged and undeleted — that creation stamps owner, tenant and the `approval` default write policy, that a non-allowlisted write policy cannot escalate, that an over-long title is rejected, and that the owner can rename, pin and delete. `handleAISessionsCreate` **77%**, `handleAISessionsList` **56%**, `handleAISessionsDelete` **59%**, `handleAISessionsUpdate` **51%**; `internal/server` **62.5% → 62.7%**.
- **The remote-desktop signaling guards are covered.** `handleRDWebRTCSignal` was at 0%. The tests pin the two refusals that run **before** the WebSocket upgrade: a missing `session` parameter answers **400**, and a session owned by another operator answers **404** (session ownership is checked with the caller's user id, so a guessed session id is useless). The owner's dial is verified to upgrade. `handleRDWebRTCSignal` **22%**; `internal/server` **62.2% → 62.3%**.
- **Open gap: the signaling loop itself is still unasserted.** A test that drove ping/offer/stop frames end to end was written and then dropped: the handler consistently returned through `attachOperator`'s refusal branch (the recovered connection showed as an empty socket) and the cause was not identified before the round ended. The refusal coverage above is what landed; the frame loop remains a candidate for a future round rather than a claimed fix.
- **The external-C2 result path and its HMAC gate are covered.** `processMessage` was at 0% and `processExternalC2Result` at 70%. The tests pin the state machine (a result may only complete a **pending/running** task of the **claimed agent**; cancelled and finalised tasks are left untouched, as is another agent's task) and the security property that **channel membership is not authentication**: an unsigned or wrongly signed Discord result is dropped while the same frame with the correct `extc2` HMAC is applied. The result is verified encrypted at rest (`FC2ENC:` in the raw column, plaintext through the model) — and when loot encryption is unavailable the handler fails closed, dropping the output and recording an error instead of storing plaintext. `processMessage` **100%**, `processExternalC2Result` **71%**; `internal/server` **62.1% → 62.2%**.
- **The server boot path is covered end to end.** `Run` and `serve` were at 0%. A test now boots the real server on a free loopback port (TLS off, every optional listener off, update checks off), polls `/health` until it answers 200, proves a **second instance on the same port is refused**, then calls `Shutdown` and requires `Run` to return and the **port to be released** so a restart can bind immediately. That exercises the boot sequence — audit worker, vault re-encrypt loop, cleanup goroutines, pending-count reconciliation, ExtC2/domain-front restore, extra listeners from the DB, port pre-check, `markUpdateBootOK` — plus the graceful shutdown. `Run` 0 → **53.5%**, `serve` 0 → **100%**, `shutdown` 70%, `internal/server` **61.5% → 62.1%**.
- **The metrics endpoint and system alert evaluation are covered.** `GetLatestMetrics` and `handleGetSystemMetrics` were at 0% and `checkSystemAlerts` at 30%. The tests pin the NaN contract (a failed OS probe is reported as a **missing** `cpu`/`disk` key, never a fabricated 0%), the percentage arithmetic, the 503 when no collector exists, and the alert thresholds: cpu/memory/disk rules fire above their thresholds, a rule whose threshold is not crossed does not, a disabled rule does not, the alert carries the hostname and an `active` status, and a second tick does **not** duplicate the active alert (rule+source dedupe). `handleGetSystemMetrics` and `GetLatestMetrics` **100%**, `checkSystemAlerts` **95%**; `internal/server` **61.4% → 61.5%**.
- **The beacon WebSocket path is exercised for real.** `handleWebSocketBeacon`, `wsWritePump` and `wsReadPump` were at 0% because they need a live socket. An httptest listener now drives three cases: a malformed `agent_id` closes with a **protocol error** (never reaches the frame loop), a **garbage frame** is rejected by the envelope decoder while the connection stays registered and usable (the unauthenticated upgrade is safe only because every frame is authenticated), and a **duplicate connection** for the same agent is closed with a policy violation. `handleWebSocketBeacon` **88%**, `wsWritePump` **61%**, `wsReadPump` **42%**; `internal/server` **61.2% → 61.4%**.
- **`-shuffle=on` found no order dependence.** Two shuffled runs (on top of four plain and three coverage-instrumented runs from last round, nine clean in total) all passed, so the single unexplained server failure is rare rather than a deterministic ordering bug. It stays recorded as open instead of being closed without a reproduction.
- **The update-check failure path and the hot-update refusals are covered.** `checkForUpdate` was at 0% and only the happy path of `handleHotUpdate`'s guards was exercised. The tests record a failed check (a deliberately unparsable repository, so no network call) and assert `CheckedAt` is stamped while `CheckOK`/`Available` stay false and `Error` is populated, then pin all three pre-download refusals: a concurrent update answers 409 **without clearing the running update's in-flight flag**, while "no updates available" and "already up to date" both answer 400 and do release the flag so a retry is possible.
- **A fourth gate catches pool queries inside transactions, which hang on SQLite.** `scripts/check-tx-queries.mjs` (`check:tx-queries` in `npm run check`) walks every `<handle>.Transaction(func(tx *gorm.DB) …)` block and fails when the block queries through the pool handle instead of `tx`. SQLite is pinned to a single connection (`SQLiteMaxOpenConns = 1`, and the config sync refuses to raise it), so such a query waits forever for the connection the transaction is holding — the handler hangs rather than erroring. That is exactly the bug introduced (and caught by a three-minute test hang) while stamping a new user's tenant. Verified in both directions; the 27 existing transaction blocks are clean.
- **The session-management endpoints are covered, and their cross-user protection is pinned.** `handleListUserSessions` was at 0% and the two revoke handlers were partial. The tests prove a plain user can list and revoke only **their own** sessions — pointing the `:id` path at themselves while naming someone else's `:sessionId` is refused, and so is claiming the other user's id — while an admin may act on anyone. Revocation is verified end-to-end: the token is rejected by `isSessionRevoked` and the row disappears from the active list. The concurrency cap is pinned to evict the **oldest** sessions (an inverted order would log the operator out immediately on their fresh login), with `0` disabling it. `handleListUserSessions` 84%, `handleRevokeSession` 79%, `handleRevokeAllUserSessions` 75%; `internal/server` **60.7% → 60.8%**.

- **A second static gate covers gin context keys.** `scripts/check-context-keys.mjs` (`check:context-keys` in `npm run check`) collects every key written with `c.Set` in production code — resolving `const` key names such as `tenantIDContextKey` — and every key read with `c.Get`/`c.GetString`/`c.MustGet`, then fails when a read has no writer. That is exactly the class of bug behind the blank `c.GetString("username")` attribution: a string on both sides that no compiler check sees. Verified in both directions by temporarily reintroducing the bad read (it fails and names `"username"`) and removing it (it passes, 7 keys read).
- **The router audit is now a gate.** `scripts/check-route-auth.mjs` (wired as `check:route-auth` inside `npm run check`) cross-checks every mutating route registration — 388 today — against the permission/role middleware of its group and the handler-level gates, and fails when neither is present. The six intentional public exceptions (the two login endpoints, session extend/logout, the language preference setter and the rate-limited phishing landing) carry a reason in the script. It is the check that would have caught the open `update-check/refresh` route.

- **The automation scheduler is covered.** `dispatchScheduledRules`, `schedulerLoop`, `deleteAutomationRule` and most of `executeAction` were at 0%/28%. The tests pin all three scheduler branches — a rule with no `next_run` gets one computed and persisted, a future rule is untouched, a due rule fires — plus the **tenant gate** (a tenant-2 rule targeting a tenant-1 agent does not fire and queues nothing), the bad-schedule skip, and the counters (`run_count`, `last_run`, recomputed `next_run`). `executeAction` now covers `create_task` (with `{{hostname}}`/`{{agent_id}}` expansion and the `created_by=automation` stamp), `set_sleep` (OPSEC clamping), the **cross-tenant `run_macro` refusal**, `notify`, and the SSRF-rejected webhook. `dispatchScheduledRules` **78%**, `schedulerLoop` **83%**, `deleteAutomationRule` **100%**, `executeAction` 28% → **60%**; `internal/server` **60.3% → 60.5%**.
- **The automation rule matcher, the backup schedule parser and the REST v1 task endpoints are covered.** `matchCondition` and `parseCronSchedule` were at 0%: the table-driven cases pin contains/equals semantics (case-insensitive vs exact), numeric `evt.Data` fields, a missing field, the default-allow for an unknown operator, and every named schedule plus `time.ParseDuration` fallback and the invalid-schedule error. The REST handlers `apiCreateTask`, `apiGetTask`, `apiListListeners` and `apiBulkResults` are covered too, including the strict numeric id, the **cross-tenant 404**, the unknown-task-type refusal, the documented `{success, results, total}` bulk shape, and the viewer role being refused. `matchCondition` and `parseCronSchedule` **100%**, `apiGetTask` 88%, `apiBulkResults` 80%; `internal/server` **60.1% → 60.3%**.
- **`requireOperator` was a blocklist** (it refused `viewer` and the empty role, letting any other string through) until the security fix converted it to a role-existence allowlist.
- **The SOCKS relay endpoints are covered.** `handleStartSocksRelay` was at 0%, and status/stop/list were partial. The tests start a real relay on a freshly reserved port, assert the start/status/list/stop lifecycle (an inactive agent answers `active:false`; a second stop is a 400), reject invalid ports (`abc`, `0`, `70000`) and an unknown agent, and — the security property — prove the session list is **scoped through the owning agent** so a tenant-2 session never appears and the `agent_id` filter cannot widen the result. `handleStartSocksRelay` 0 → **78%**, `handleStopSocksRelay` **90%**, `handleSocksRelayStatus` **90%**, `handleGetSocksSessions` **85%**; `internal/server` **60.0% → 60.1%**.
- **The settings security surface is covered.** `handleDownloadConfig` now has a test that sets **all fifteen** secret fields (JWT, beacon key, SSH password, admin hash, AI key, the three Slack tokens, every crypto key including loot/TOTP/CSRF/ExtC2/backup, and the ExtC2 API token), writes them into a config file, and asserts none of them survives into the download while ordinary lines do; it also checks the non-admin 403 and the missing-file path. `handleDownloadConfig` 77% → **100%`. The webhook endpoints are covered too: the stored-target round trip, corrupt stored JSON degrading to an empty list instead of a 500, and the **SSRF guard** rejecting loopback, RFC1918, the cloud-metadata address and a non-HTTP scheme before any request is made. Plus `authConfigured`, the audit page and a real SQLite `VACUUM`. `handleGetSettingsWebhooks` and `authConfigured` are **100%**; `internal/server` **59.8% → 60.0%**.
- **The AI streaming conversation loop is covered.** `converse` was at 23% and `writeSSE` at 0%. A fake SSE provider now drives the real loop: a plain text answer (asserting the accumulated final text and the `done` event), a **tool round** (the model asks for `list_agents`, the dispatcher runs it, a second completion answers, and the `tool` event names the tool), a provider 500 surfacing as an `error` event, a pre-cancelled context returning without an error event, and the tolerant parse behaviour (an unparseable frame is skipped, `[DONE]` still terminates). `converse` 23% → **71%**, `writeSSE` **100%**.
- **`ensureDirWritable` and the update-poll lifecycle are covered.** The probe file is created and removed, a missing directory and a plain file path both report "not writable", and cancelling the server context lets the update poller exit instead of parking on its initial delay (`ensureDirWritable` **100%**).
- **The agent kill/unlinked/config endpoints are covered.** `handleKillAgent`, `handleListUnlinkedAgents`, `handleGetAgentConfig` and the agents page were at 0%. The tests assert that a kill actually creates a `kill` task for the right agent (and 404s for an unknown one), that the unlinked list contains only parentless tenant-1 agents in hostname order, and that the effective config prefers the agent's own interval/jitter over the configured defaults and surfaces a queued `config_push` task by id. `handleGetAgentConfig` **100%**, `handleKillAgent` **90%**, `handleListUnlinkedAgents` **71%**; `internal/server` **59.3% → 59.5%**.
- **The WebRTC start/stop handlers are covered.** `webRTCHub`, `handleWebRTCStart`, `handleWebRTCStop` and `parseWebRTCSignal` were at 0%. The tests pin the start refusals (offline agent, unknown agent, malformed body, out-of-range settings, the still-image-monitor conflict, WebRTC disabled, and a duplicate live session — all before TURN is consulted), the stop paths (no session answers a friendly 200, **another operator's session is refused with 403 and stays alive**, a stale `session_id` is a no-op, and the owner's stop unregisters it), the hub singleton, and the signal size/parse guards. `webRTCHub` **100%**, `handleWebRTCStop` **83%**, `parseWebRTCSignal` **71%**, `handleWebRTCStart` **47%** (the success path needs a live TURN relay); `internal/server` **59.1% → 59.3%**.
- **The phishing sender is covered, and a failed launch used to wedge the campaign.** `sendPhishingMail` and `runPhishingCampaign` were at 0%. An in-process fake SMTP server drives the real `net/smtp` path: the tests assert the header-injection guard (a CRLF in the subject cannot start a new `Bcc:` header), the envelope, the per-recipient landing link, per-target `sent` events with tokens, `sent_count`/`failed_count` bookkeeping, the `send_failed` payload when SMTP is unreachable, and that a stopped campaign sends nothing. `sendPhishingMail` 0 → **95%**, `runPhishingCampaign` 0 → **84%**.
- **A phishing campaign that failed validation stayed "running" forever.** The launch handler claimed the campaign (status → `running`) *before* checking the template, targets, `smtp_host` and `from_email`, so any rejection left it wedged and the next attempt answered "campaign is already running".
