Validation now runs before the atomic claim, which still prevents a double send; the existing test that asserted the wedged behaviour was rewritten to check claim semantics on a campaign with a valid target list.
- **The report generator is covered end to end.** `handleGenerateReport`, `generateHTMLReport` and `handleReportPage` were at 0%/31%/0%. The tests build a tenant-1/tenant-2 fixture and assert the whole contract: every section is tenant-scoped (a foreign agent, task, credential and audit row stay out), the summary counts match, `success_rate` is the real 66.7% rather than a fabricated 100%, the report is **not** flagged `partial` on a healthy database, the HTML body carries the title and a hostname, `format` is whitelisted, a reversed date range and a >365-day range are rejected, and an empty body falls back to the default (last month, HTML). `handleGenerateReport` 85%, `generateHTMLReport` 72%, `handleReportPage` 88%; `internal/server` **58.6% → 59.0%**.
- **The "missing API" handlers are covered, and `/chat/channels` was returning the wrong key casing.** Templates/info, the privesc panel, the merged timeline, chat history/channels/send, the chain topology endpoints, agent-chain set/clear, the settings/statistics panel, the mesh topology and the translation stats were all at 0%. The tests pin the two decryption paths that the handlers document (a sensitive command is verified to be `FC2ENC:` at rest and plaintext in the timeline; the privesc panel returns the stored result), the chain **cycle guard** (self-parent, ancestor cycle, unknown agent), chat channel filtering and aggregation, the message-size cap, the masked `database_path`/`data_dir` in the stats response, the outdated-protocol agent count, and the mesh node/edge build from `MeshPeer` rows. `handleAPIPackerTemplates`, `handleAPIPackerInfo`, `handleAPIPrivesc`, `handleAPIChatHistory`, `handleAPIChatChannels`, `buildChainEntries`, the chain endpoints, `handleAPIMeshTopology` and `handleAPITranslationsStats` are now **100%**; `internal/server` **58.4% → 58.6%**.
- **`/chat/channels` emitted PascalCase keys** (`Channel`, `MessageCount`, …) because its row struct had no JSON tags, while every other endpoint answers in snake_case and the OpenAPI entry defines no schema. The keys are now `channel` / `message_count` / `last_message` / `last_time`, matching the rest of the API.
- **The AI tools' write paths are covered, and they exposed a primary-key collision.** `execute_command` (approval gate, sensitive-command gate, queued path), `execute_command_bulk`, `run_macro`, `create_automation_rule`, `save_engagement_note` and the listener create/delete tools were all unexercised. The tests drive the allow_execute-off refusals, the approval vs queued outcomes, the per-agent result entries in a bulk dispatch (including the unknown-agent entry), macro lookup by id and by name, rule creation for both the command and macro action shapes, engagement-note append/replace/oversize, and the listener host/dns/agent-reference validations. `executeToolSwitchCtx` 42% → **63%**, `internal/server` **57.9% → 58.4%**.
- **Two automation-rule primary keys could collide.** Both rule-creation paths derived the id from `time.Now().UnixNano()`; the Windows clock is coarse enough that two creations in one tick produced `UNIQUE constraint failed: automation_rules.id` and a 500. The AI tool path now uses `ai-<uuid>` and the UI path `rule_<uuid>`, with a regression test that creates five rules in a row and asserts distinct ids.
- **Two more nil-map panics hardened.** `startExtraListener` wrote its placeholder into `s.extraListeners` without initializing it, and `trackPendingTaskReserved` did the same for `agentPendingTasks`; both now create the map under their existing mutex, so a `Server` built without them (tests, future constructors) cannot turn a request into a panic.
- **Playbook drafting and run-context assembly are covered, and the playbook had the same cap bug.** `handleAIGeneratePlaybook` was at 0% and `buildAIRunContext` at 36%. The tests drive the draft prompt (including the target profile reaching the model), the step trimming, the generated name, the unparseable and all-empty replies, and — for the context builder — the boundary warning, source citation, and the two isolation properties: an attachment from another session is never injected, and collections are not searched at all without a query. `handleAIGeneratePlaybook` 0 → **86%**, `buildAIRunContext` 36% → **100%**.
- **Playbook drafting capped before it filtered**, exactly like the suggestion list: a blank step consumed one of the twelve slots, so a model reply with 13 steps (one blank) produced eleven. The filter now runs first (`aiPlaybookMaxSteps`).
- **AI attachments and knowledge collections are covered.** Every handler in `handlers_ai_knowledge.go` for attachments (upload/list/delete), collections (list/create/delete), sources (list/delete), attachment promotion and knowledge search was at 0%. The tests upload real multipart bodies, assert the stored bytes never come back in a list response, check the per-request and per-session file caps, promote an attachment into a collection (chunked, hashed, duplicate-refused with 409), and search it — including the property that **search returns nothing unless the operator selected a collection**, and that a run scoped to a different collection cannot see it. `internal/server` went **57.3% → 57.5%**.
- **The one-shot AI helpers are tested, and one of them had a real bug.** `aiOneShot` (the shared completion helper behind suggest-next-steps and the natural-language query) was at 0%, as were `buildAttackSurfaceData`, `handleAISuggestNextSteps` and `handleAINLQuery`. A fake provider endpoint (loopback is allowed only through `ai.allow_private_endpoint`, which the tests set exactly as a local-model operator would) now exercises both wire formats, the provider-error/empty/malformed branches, the context aggregation, and the filter whitelist that keeps a model from reaching SQL. `aiOneShot` 0 → **87%**, `buildAttackSurfaceData` 0 → 70%, `handleAISuggestNextSteps` 0 → 70%, `handleAINLQuery` 6% → 76%, `decodeModelJSON` 40% → 90%.
- **Suggest-next-steps capped before it filtered.** The six-item cap was applied to the model's raw list and the empty-`action` entries were dropped afterwards, so a reply whose first six items included a blank one came back with five suggestions. The filter now runs first and the cap counts real items (`aiSuggestionMaxItems`).
- **The AI run endpoint is exercised end-to-end now.** `handleAIRunsCreate` (0%), `startAIBackgroundRun` (0%), `executeAIBackgroundRun` (0%), `resolveAIRunProfile` (0%) and `sessionAllowsLowRisk` (0%) were the whole create-a-run path. The tests drive the validation ladder (anonymous 403, missing session/messages, non-user roles, unknown and cross-tenant sessions, invisible context agent, over-long idempotency key), the accepted path and its idempotent replay (one row, `reused: true`), the background run against a refusing endpoint (so the failure path is real, not mocked), every profile-resolution branch (default, known provider defaulting its endpoint, `custom` refusing an empty endpoint, keyless profile, cross-tenant, disabled) and the low-risk policy lookup. `handleAIRunsCreate` is at **60%**, `resolveAIRunProfile` **91%**, `startAIBackgroundRun` **92%**, `executeAIBackgroundRun` **78%**; `internal/server` went **56.2% → 56.9%**.
- **The WebRTC session lifecycle now has tests.** Its methods take a `*websocket.Conn`, which is why `sendToOperator`, `attachOperator`, `attachAgent`, `peersReady` and `end` were all at 0%: an in-process websocket pair makes them reachable, so the tests drive attach ordering, the "already attached" refusals, buffered signaling flushed on agent attach, live sends, the bounded pending buffer (one frame past the cap tears the session down), teardown announcing `session_end` to both sides, idempotent `end`, and the hub's one-session-per-agent and max-sessions limits with TTL clamping. Those functions are now 93–100%.
- **`trackPendingTaskReserved` no longer panics on a nil counter.** A `Server` built without `agentPendingTasks` (tests, future constructors) turned a task insert into `assignment to entry in nil map`; the map is now created lazily under the existing mutex, with a regression test covering the lazy init, the cap, and the reserved-slot headroom. `internal/server` coverage went **56.0% → 56.2%** (CI total 47.0%).
- **The AI tool dispatcher and the plugin marketplace now have tests.** `ai_tools_exec.go` was the single largest uncovered file in the repo (666 uncovered statements, 19%): its one-switch dispatch means every tool was untested, including the tenant scoping of `list_agents`/`search_tasks`/`list_credentials` and the validation in `set_sleep`/`queue_collection`. One test file now drives 18 of its tools through a seeded tenant-1/tenant-2 fleet, asserting both the happy path and the cross-tenant denial; the file is at **42%**. The plugin marketplace handlers in `handlers_automation.go` were at **0%** across the board (list/create/get/toggle/delete, ratings, reviews, dependencies, update status, export, page): they are now 76–100%, and the file's remaining gaps are the two that need a real network release. `internal/server` coverage went **54.9% → 56.0%**.
- **The race job is two parallel legs instead of one 20-minute block.** The local measurement of the exact CI command is 822 s wall clock, of which the server package alone is ~780 s — 65% of the shared timeout, leaving little margin on a slower runner and no indication of which half regressed. `./internal/server/...` and `./internal/db/... ./internal/plugin/... ./internal/scripting/... ./internal/payload/agent/...` now run as a `fail-fast: false` matrix, each with the full 20 m.
- **`go test -race ./internal/payload/agent/...` aborts on Windows**, and that is now documented instead of being a mystery: the agent's Windows-only shellcode/PE/EDR code walks raw pointers, and checkptr (implied by `-race`) rejects the arithmetic with `fatal error: checkptr: pointer arithmetic result points to invalid allocation`. CI is unaffected (the leg runs on Linux, where those files do not compile). One genuine violation in `edr_windows.go`'s service enumeration was fixed with base-relative `unsafe.Add`; the remaining ~40 sites are a separate, risky batch, and the documented workaround is `-gcflags=all=-d=checkptr=0` (verified: the package passes under `-race` with it).
- `AGENTS.md` had drifted from the code: it said version 2.6.2 (2.7.1 now), `toolchain go1.26.9` (go1.26.10), and listed a 5-minute test command that no longer matched CI (8 minutes, more packages). All three are corrected, and the race legs and the Windows agent caveat are described there.
- **The coverage floor is a real ratchet now: 40% → 44%.** The comment claimed ~45.8% while the measured total was 46.3% across the CI package list, so the old floor allowed a 6-point slide before CI noticed. 44% keeps a little slack for a skipped platform-specific test without letting coverage fall another point.

### Changed

- **`check:i18n` no longer calls schema-driven keys dead.** The dead-key scan only understood `t("x.y")`-shaped calls, so a key held in a record and resolved later — `desc: "generate.format_desc_exe"`, then `t(FORMAT_META[key].desc)` — looked unused. That is why last week's prune needed a manual pass over all 79 candidates. A key now also counts as used when it is mentioned literally anywhere outside the dictionaries, which cannot hide a genuinely dead key (the mention *is* the use) and cannot make the gate fail; a synthetic probe confirmed both directions. The missing-key check is unchanged.
- **The English dictionary is off the first-paint path.** `t()` is synchronous, so the split is a bootstrap slice rather than lazy-everything: `src/lib/i18n/bootstrap.ts` (generated, 175 keys) carries the shell namespaces (`nav.`, `topbar.`, `section.`, `a11y.`, `common.`, `login.`, `error.`) synchronously, the provider merges the full `./en` in from a dynamic import, and the routed area shows `PageSpinner` until it arrives so no frame can render a raw key. The production build aliases `@i18n-sync` to an empty module (dev/test keep the real dictionary, which is why the 947 unit tests were untouched). **Critical path 1027 → 798 kB** (−229 kB, 27%), 292 kB of headroom against the budget. `check:i18n-bootstrap` fails when the generated slice drifts from `en.ts`. Verified with the full e2e matrix (mobile/tablet/laptop/desktop, visual baselines included).
- **79 unreferenced i18n keys are gone from both dictionaries.** `check:i18n` had been warning about them for a while; each was verified unreferenced before deletion — no literal occurrence outside the dictionaries, no `t(\`prefix${…}\`)` in that family, and no `label:`/`desc:` record holding the key (the schema-driven indirection that the gate cannot see). The prune takes 4.0 kB off each dictionary: `en`'s chunk from 242.7 to **238.6 kB**, the lazy `zh` chunk from 237.7 to **233.7 kB**, and the bundle from 4.54 to **4.53 MB** before the split below; `check:i18n` now reports zero unused keys, so the warning is a real signal again.
- `I18nProvider`'s "boots zh from localStorage" test asserted `document.documentElement.lang` synchronously after the text swap; `lang` is applied by a separate effect keyed on locale, so it flaked under load. The assertion now waits like the text one does.
- Dead exports are back to zero: `npm run audit:unused-exports` reported 9 files (`process-snapshot`, the profiles types module, `lib/errors`, and the `ui/sheet` · `ui/dialog` · `ui/field` · `ui/list-row` primitives plus `file-search` and `useWebRTCLive`). The four profiles helpers (`ActiveMalleableConfig`, `commonUAs`, `emptyProfile`, `emptyActiveConfig`) were genuinely dead and are deleted; the remaining nine symbols are used only inside their own module, so their `export` is dropped — per `docs/FRONTEND_CONVENTIONS.md`, a symbol used only in its own file is not an export.
- `docs/UIUX_AUDIT.md`'s backlog caught up with the code: U9.6 (density only affected the type ramp and table padding) and U9.7 (no layout gate) are marked landed with the verification used — the density tokens have real consumers outside `globals.css`, and `check:layout` enforces the layout contract. U9.5 (detail pages each growing their own chrome) stays open.
- `docs/FRONTEND_PERF.md` re-measured the budget on 2026-10-24: the critical path is 1030.8 kB / 1090 kB across 13 chunks and the `en` dictionary (242.7 kB, the largest chunk on the path) has moved from the entry into `utils-*.js`. The entry records **why** the dictionary split is not mechanical — `t()` is synchronous, so it needs a bootstrap dictionary plus an awaited full dictionary, with the flash-of-key-names failure mode verified against e2e and both baseline sets — so the next attempt starts from the design instead of rediscovering it. The same section records the race-suite runtime (670–855 s vs the CI job's 20 m timeout).

### Performance
- **GeoIP had no cache and no local budget, so a beacon fleet could trip `ip-api.com`'s 45/minute limit and blank geolocation for every host.** The call site only skips the database **write** when a location is unchanged, not the **request**, so every check-in from a public IP issued a lookup; ip-api answers **429 for the rest of the minute** once tripped. `lookupGeoIP` now keeps a **12-hour per-IP cache** (bounded at 4096 entries, cleared wholesale when full) and a **local budget of 40 lookups/minute**, below the upstream limit, so the 429 is never reached — a throttled lookup returns empty and logs the reason at debug instead of spending a request to learn it. A **failed** lookup is deliberately **not** cached, so a transient rejection does not blank an IP for 12 hours. **Evidence:** the new test counts requests through a stub transport — one lookup per IP (a repeat is served from the cache), the budget stops at 40 while 51 distinct IPs are asked for, private addresses never leave the process, and a `{"status":"fail"}` response is retried next time. Disabling the cache check makes it fail with `a repeat lookup for the same IP still went out (2 requests)`; disabling the budget makes it fail with `51 requests went out; the local budget is 40 per minute`.
- **The audit page and the notification bell both filtered on an index and then sorted in a TEMP B-TREE.** Same shape as the last two rounds: a filter index without the sort column. `audit_logs` had separate `action` and `tenant_id` indexes and a separate `created_at` one, and `notifications` indexed `read` alone — so each filtered page found its rows and then sorted them. They now carry `idx_audit_action_time (action, created_at)`, `idx_audit_tenant_time (tenant_id, created_at)` and `idx_notif_read_time (read, created_at)`; measured with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`, `audit_logs_by_action` now reads `SEARCH audit_logs USING INDEX idx_audit_action_time (action=?)` with no sort step. **One residual is recorded rather than fixed:** the audit page's tenant filter is written `tenant_id = 1 OR tenant_id = 0` (the legacy-visibility rule), and an **OR cannot use the composite for ordering**, so that query still sorts — `TestPagedListQueriesUseAnIndex` allows exactly that one and still fails if the plan degrades to a full `SCAN audit_logs`, which is the difference between sorting the tenant's own trail and reading every tenant's.
- **`one_shot_tasks` sorted every row of an agent in a TEMP B-TREE; `tasks` and `agent_status_events` already did not.** The scheduler and the one-shot list both read "this agent's shots, newest first" (`WHERE agent_id = ? ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50`). `tasks` has `idx_tasks_agent_created` and status history has `idx_status_agent_time`, so both serve the filter and the sort from one index — `one_shot_tasks` had only a single-column `agent_id` index, so the same query read **every row the agent ever produced** and sorted it: `before: SEARCH … USING INDEX idx_one_shot_tasks_agent_id | USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` → `after: SEARCH … USING INDEX idx_one_shot_agent_created`. Two tests now measure the plans rather than assume them: `TestHotAgentTaskQueriesUseAnIndex` (which **found** this one) covers the per-agent list shapes, and `TestAgentScopeQueriesUseAnIndex` covers the other tenant shape, `agent_id IN (SELECT id FROM implants WHERE tenant_id = ?)`, over four tables — all four already used an index, so that one is a **guard, not a fix**. Both fail on `SCAN <table>` and on `TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY`.
- **The four creator-scoped tables had no index on the column the tenant scoping filters.** Rounds 185-200 moved every creator-scoped list onto `WHERE created_by IN (SELECT username FROM users WHERE tenant_id = ?) ORDER BY created_at DESC`, and `scripts`, `workflows`, `phishing_templates` and `phishing_campaigns` had **no `created_by` index at all** — so each of those pages read the whole table and sorted it in a TEMP B-TREE, for every tenant-scoped operator, on every load. Each now carries a composite `(created_by, created_at)` index. **Evidence, measured with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`:** `before: SCAN scripts` → `after: SEARCH scripts USING INDEX idx_scripts_creator_time (created_by=?)`. The plan still ends in `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY` — with the `IN (subquery)` form SQLite uses the index for the equality only and then sorts — which is a bounded cost over the creator's own rows rather than the whole table, so the new `TestCreatorScopeQueriesUseAnIndex` **records that in a comment instead of asserting it away** (serving the sort needs the query rewritten as a join). The test fails on `SCAN <table>` and on a plan that does not use the expected index, so a future "just drop the index" change breaks loudly. All 26 `TenantID` fields were checked too: every one is indexed.
- **Every tenant-scoped request made its own `SELECT tenant_id FROM users`.** `lookupTenantID` resolved the caller's tenant by username out of the database, per request, on top of the user row the auth middleware had **already loaded** (and caches for five minutes on the session path). The middleware now hands the tenant over (`middleware.TenantIDContextKey`), and the lookup prefers it, falling back to the database only when the value is absent — so no existing call path changes behaviour. The safety property is preserved and tested: with the value absent and the pool **closed**, the resolution still fails **closed** (a failed lookup must never degrade to global visibility), which is why the test asserts against a closed database rather than a live one. A second test plants agents in two tenants and proves a context-scoped request still sees only its own. **Trade-off documented in code**: the session path's tenant now comes from the five-minute user cache, so moving a user between tenants takes up to that long unless `InvalidateUserCache` is called — a deliberate exchange for removing a query from every request.
- **The placement decode re-parsed the transform chain on every check-in.** `extractPlacedBody` calls `malleable.ParseWire(chain)` per candidate value, which splits and allocates a fresh `[]StepV2` each time for a string that only changes when an operator edits the profile. `ParseWireCached` memoises the result in a **bounded** map (256 entries, dropped wholesale at the cap, so request data cannot grow it into a leak) and the beacon path uses it. Measured: **249 ns → 23 ns per parse (10.8×)**. Framed honestly, this is a micro-step win, not a headline: the beacon's placed-body decode is dominated by the base64 transform itself, so the absolute saving per check-in is small — it is kept because it removes allocation churn from the hot path, and because the cache's boundedness and equality with the plain parser are both tested rather than assumed.
- **The beacon path re-read and re-parsed the profile JSON on every check-in.** `activePlacements` resolved the malleable placement set from scratch each call: `os.ReadFile` of `<data>/profiles/<name>.json` plus `MigrateProfileJSON`, or a `json.Unmarshal` of the inline copy — per beacon, per implant, forever, even though the answer changes only when an operator edits the profile. It is now cached behind a key of (inline JSON, profile name, file mtime, file size), so the answer is recomputed only when one of those actually changes. The first version of the cache still stat-ed the file on every hit, which measured **13.9µs** — the syscall dominated — so the stat is throttled to once per 200 ms; a hit now costs **48ns** against **31µs** for the uncached path, roughly **650×**. The trade is deliberate and bounded: an out-of-band edit becomes visible within 200 ms, far below what an operator would notice and documented in the test that pins it. Tests also prove the cache is actually used (hits/misses), that a config change (inline placements set, changed, cleared) invalidates it, and that a rewritten file is picked up after the window.
- **The two index fixes are now an executable guard instead of two memories.** `TestHotQueriesHaveNoUnindexedSortOrScan` walks `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` for ten queries the console and the beacon path run constantly — the task list page, the agent list page, the beacon claim, the stale sweep, the audit window, the task-type dropdown and the four `navStats` counts — and fails if any of them sorts in a **TEMP B-TREE** or scans a large table. It is the check that would have caught rounds 141 and 142 on its own, and it runs on every `go test` instead of waiting for the next audit. Two deliberate, documented exceptions: tiny tables may sort in memory (the index would cost more than the sort), and `ORDER BY id DESC LIMIT n` over a rowid table is allowed even though SQLite prints a plain `SCAN` — measured on **50 000** audit rows at **75 µs**, against **80 µs** for the equivalent `WHERE id > …` seek, so it is a backwards rowid walk and not a full scan. Writing that exception down mattered: the guard's first run failed on it, and the honest resolution was to measure rather than to weaken the rule.
- **The agent list had the same index mismatch as the task list.** `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` for `WHERE tenant_id = ? ORDER BY last_seen DESC LIMIT 100` read `SEARCH implants USING INDEX idx_implants_tenant_id` **plus `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY`**: the tenant index filters but cannot order, so SQLite walked **every agent of the tenant** and sorted it to return one page. A composite `(tenant_id, last_seen)` index removes the temp sort (the plan is now a single `SEARCH … USING INDEX idx_implants_tenant_seen`). Measured at 500 agents: **1.27 ms → 1.12 ms per query**, and the cost is now `O(limit)` instead of `O(tenant rows)` — the win grows with fleet size, which is exactly when it matters. The index-existence test from the previous round was generalised into `TestListPageIndexesExist`, which covers **both** list-page indexes with the reason each exists, so a future model edit cannot silently restore either scan.
- **The operator task list was sorting every row of the tenant on every page load.** Measured, not guessed: `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN` for `WHERE tenant_id = ? ORDER BY created_at DESC LIMIT 50` read `SEARCH tasks USING INDEX idx_tasks_tenant_id` **plus `USE TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY`** — the plain tenant index can filter but not order, so SQLite fetched **every row of the tenant** and sorted it to produce 50. A composite `(tenant_id, created_at)` index removes the temp sort entirely (plan is now a single `SEARCH … USING INDEX idx_tasks_tenant_created`). Isolated cost on 4000 tasks: **983µs → 555µs per query (−44%)**. Two supporting changes: `BenchmarkTaskList` covers the whole page for both a tenant-scoped and an unscoped caller so future regressions have a number, and a `PRAGMA index_list` test fails if a model edit ever drops the index silently. Note on method: the whole-handler benchmark is noisy (six statements, ~2.6ms) and did **not** show the isolated win, which is why the claim rests on the query-level measurement and the plan change rather than on that number.

- The beacon hot path no longer writes the log: at default `info` level a steady-state fleet produced **three INFO lines per check-in** ("Beacon processed", "Beacon fetching pending tasks", and — per returned result — "Processing task result"), which at the measured ~1,840 idle check-ins/s/core is ≈5.5k lines/s of pure noise that buries the events operators actually read and burns disk on long campaigns. Steady-state check-ins and zero-task dispatches now log at `Debug`; status transitions (offline→online, which already emit the WS broadcast, the event row and the connect hook), task deliveries (`pending_count > 0`) and task results keep their `Info` line. The idle check-in benchmark now emits zero INFO lines per iteration (measured), while the task-delivery benchmark still logs exactly one dispatch line per delivery.
- `offlineThreshold()` warned "OfflineThreshold is invalid, using default" on **every call** — the getter runs per agent list, per dashboard poll, per metrics scrape and per status recompute, so one misconfigured value drowned the server in its own diagnostic (measured: thousands of warns during a single benchmark run). The warning is now rate-limited to one per minute per server instance (CAS-guarded so concurrent beacons cannot stampede), pinned by `TestOfflineThresholdWarnIsRateLimited` (5,000 calls → exactly 1 warning, default still returned).

## [2.7.1] - 2026-10-23

### Upgrade note

- **2.7.0 cannot update itself.** Its update checker asked `forgec2/forgec2` for
  releases and read the 404 as "up to date", and even a release it did see could
  not be installed or restarted (wrong asset, lost `-config`, no rollback). Update
  a 2.7.0 installation by hand once: download the 2.7.1 binary for your platform
  (or the one-click archive), stop the server, replace `forgec2-server[.exe]`,
  start it again. From 2.7.1 on, the console's **update now** works, and every
  later release can be installed from the browser.
- The hot update needs the release to carry `forgec2-server-<os>-<arch>[.exe]`
  plus its `.sha256`; the release workflow builds exactly those names, and a
  release without a binary for the running platform is refused instead of
  guessed at.

### Fixed

- **The hot update could never succeed.** The release asset was chosen by a hard-coded `".exe"` suffix plus a loose name match (`windows` / `amd64` / `x64`), so a Linux or macOS host found no binary at all and an arm64 host could be handed the amd64 one. The checksum was taken from whichever `*.sha256` happened to be listed first — in a multi-platform release that is another platform's checksum, so the verification ended in `checksum file has no entry for …`. The selector now derives the exact asset name from the running platform (`forgec2-server-<os>-<arch>[.exe]`, with the usual architecture spellings) and derives the checksum from the selected binary; a release that carries no binary for this platform, or more than one, is refused instead of guessed. The release repository is stamped into the binary (`internal/buildinfo.Repo`, injected by the release workflow), so a fork checks its own releases.
- **The hot update never restarted the server.** The update worker was registered in the server's own waitgroup and then waited for that waitgroup, so it blocked forever, and only the context was cancelled — the HTTP server was never shut down. The console sat on "restarting" while the old build kept serving, and the restart script then copied over a running binary (Windows: the copy failed and the freshly downloaded binary was deleted). The process now shuts down properly and hands the swap over to a generated restarter.
- **The restart dropped the command line.** The new server was started without any arguments, so `-config` (and `-log-format` / `-log-file`) were lost and a server started with a custom configuration path came back on the default one. The restarter now starts the new build with the original arguments and working directory.
- **A broken release left the service down.** The old binary was overwritten in place with no backup, no health check and no rollback. The previous binary is now kept, the new build has to report a healthy boot (its listener is bound) within 60 seconds, and otherwise the backup — together with the SQLite snapshot taken before the swap — is restored and the old build is started again. The outcome (`success` / `failed` / `rolled_back` / `interrupted`) is audited and readable at `GET /api/update-progress`.
- **The update checker pointed at a repository that does not exist** (`forgec2/forgec2`), and a 404 from the releases endpoint was read as "no new release" — so the console always claimed to be up to date and no update was ever offered. A missing repository is now reported as an error, and `is_latest` is only true after a check that actually succeeded (`check_ok` / `check_state` were added to the payload).
- A second hot-update request while one is running is refused with `409` instead of starting a second download and a second restarter against the same binary; a cross-process lock in `.update/lock` protects an installation that is shared by two instances.
- The restart script no longer stays behind in the installation directory: the restarter lives in `<install dir>/.update/`, is written with restrictive permissions and deletes itself, and the staged download is removed on every failure path.
- Release notes can no longer be cut off by GitHub's 125,000-character body limit: the 2.7.0 changelog section alone is ~125k, so the published page ended mid-sentence. The generator keeps whole lines under a 60k budget and closes with a link to `CHANGELOG.md`, while the one-click instructions, the archive list and the raw binary list stay at the top, above the truncation point.
- The CycloneDX SBOM is really attached now: `forgec2-server-linux-amd64.sbom.json` was generated after `upload-artifact`, so it never reached the release although the notes announced it. The step runs before the upload (the artifact glob carries it), the one-click assembler filters `.sbom.json` out of its `forgec2-server-<os>-<arch>*` match, which could otherwise have packed the SBOM as the server binary, and the command passes `-json`: `cyclonedx-gomod` writes XML by default, so the asset named `.sbom.json` held an XML document; the tag is then stamped into `metadata.component.version`, which the tool leaves empty for a main module.
- The release notes' signature hint showed the signing command (`go run ./cmd/sign-release <file.sha256>`), which needs the private seed and fails for an operator; it now shows `go run ./cmd/sign-release -verify -key <pubkey> <file.sha256>` and points at the published key in `docs/DEPLOY.md`.
- `docs/CAPABILITY_MATRIX.md` follows `VERSION` again: the 2.7.0 bump left its stamp at v2.6.2, which failed the CI docs-freshness gate.

### Changed

- The update banner and the update dialog only offer the installation to an administrator (the endpoint is admin-only, so every other operator used to get a 403), and the dialog now waits for the server to come back after the restart and reloads the console instead of hanging on "restarting". The background check runs with a jitter so a fleet of servers does not poll `api.github.com` in lockstep.

## [2.7.0] - 2026-10-22

### Added
- **The engagement bundle gained `credentials.json` — metadata only, and it says so.** A handover bundle **leaves the console**: it gets emailed, put on a share, attached to a report. Shipping passwords and NTLM hashes in it would move live secrets **out of the system that audits their use**, so the member records what was found and where — id, agent, domain, username, type, source, tags, time — and carries a `note` explaining that the secrets are deliberately absent and must be retrieved in the console where the access is audited. The lookup is tenant-scoped and bounded to the engagement's hosts and its effective window, like every other member. **Evidence:** `TestEngagementBundle` seeds a credential whose password and hash are known strings and requires `credentials.json` to contain `svc-backup` and the `metadata only` note and to contain **neither** the password (`Sup3rSecret!`) **nor** the hash — an assertion, not a comment. The fixture also had to initialise loot encryption (`InitLootEncryption`), because the credential model refuses to store a plaintext secret without it — which is the product protecting itself in exactly the direction the bundle member now protects the operator.
- **The engagement bundle gained `timeline.md`: the exercise as a chronological story.** The bundle is handed to someone who **was not there** — an after-action reviewer, a customer, a lawyer — and a CSV plus an NDJSON dump makes them reconstruct the narrative themselves. The timeline interleaves the **audited actions** and the **task outcomes** by time (the two records that answer "what happened, in what order"), renders them as a table with the actor and host inside the detail, and **says when it capped** (`_N later entries omitted; the full record is in audit.ndjson and tasks.csv._`) rather than trailing off silently at 500 lines. The bundle now fetches the window's tasks and audit rows **once** and derives both views from the same set, so `tasks.csv`, `audit.ndjson` and `timeline.md` cannot disagree — a second query could have seen a different window. A pipe inside a detail cell is replaced with `¦` so one crafted action name cannot break the table. **Evidence:** `TestEngagementBundle` now also requires `timeline.md` to carry `# Timeline: DMZ exercise`, the table header, the in-window task's command and the in-window `task_create` action, **no** out-of-window record (`before-window` or the `login` row), and the **task line before the audit line** (the task is at −2 h, the audit at −90 m), which is what proves the ordering rather than trusting it. One expectation I had to correct: the task line shows the **command and outcome**, not the result text — the result belongs in `tasks.csv` where it is not flattened into a table cell.
- **New `GET /api/engagements/:id/bundle` — one zip that hands over the whole exercise.** Ending an engagement means **delivering** it, and collecting the evidence by hand is four downloads and a spreadsheet — with the audit part the step that gets skipped under pressure, which is exactly the part an after-action reviewer needs. The archive contains `summary.md` (the same document the summary export renders, via the same `writeEngagementMarkdown`), `tasks.csv` (the default export columns, the engagement's hosts, rows inside its window), `audit.ndjson` (**every hashed field** of the window, so it verifies offline), `agents.json` and `plugins.json` (the same index document the plugin endpoint returns, from the same `buildPluginIndex`). **Two properties are deliberate:** a bundle that would exceed the cap is **refused with 413 rather than truncated**, because a half-delivered bundle is a handover that silently lacks evidence; and the window is the **effective** one (earliest of `ends_at`, `closed_at`, now), the same merge the deadline enforcement and the summary use. **Evidence:** `TestEngagementBundle` seeds an engagement with one in-window task, one task before the window, and one in-window plus one out-of-window audit row, then requires `application/zip`, a `.zip` filename, all five members present, `summary.md` starting with `# Engagement: DMZ exercise`, `tasks.csv` with **header + exactly one** row containing `inside-window`, `audit.ndjson` with **one** row carrying both hashes, a parseable `plugins.json`, exactly **one** `engagement_bundle` audit row, and **404** when another tenant asks.
- **The output download now sends a strong `ETag`, which is what makes a *resumed* download safe.** Ranges let a client fetch half a result and ask for the rest; if the task was **re-run** in between, the two halves belong to different outputs and the spliced file is **silently wrong**. `If-Range` is the mechanism that prevents it, and it only works with a **strong** validator — so the endpoint now sets one covering the row id, the modification time and the body length, and `http.ServeContent` does the rest: a range whose tag still matches is served **206**, while a **stale** tag gets **200 with the whole document** instead of a partial splice. **Evidence:** `TestTaskOutputIfRange` requires the tag to be present, **not** `W/`-prefixed and quoted; `Range: bytes=5-9` **with the current tag** → 206 `56789`; the same range with a **stale** tag → 200 and the **full** body; and after the task's result is updated, the tag **changes** and an old tag **cannot** resume onto the new content (`NEW-RESULT-CONTENT` returned whole). The spec documents the header and why a resuming client should send it.
- **New `GET /api/exports/capabilities` — the download surface described in a form a client can read.** Both exports grew parameters across a series of rounds (time bounds, engagement, format, column selection, presets), and the only way to discover them was the OpenAPI document or the source; a UI building the download form, or a script validating its own arguments, now asks instead. **The point is that the values are derived, not written:** the task export's `fields` comes from the catalogue slice (so it is the default column order too), `max_rows` is `ExportTaskLimit`, the `minimal` preset is the same slice the parser uses, and `audit_export_bytes` is the variable the handler enforces — with a test pinning each, because **a capabilities document that drifts from the behaviour is worse than none, since clients trust it**. **Two constraints are stated rather than implied:** the audit export offers **no column projection** (offline verification needs every field the entry hash covers, so a projected export would look verifiable while being unverifiable) and any audit filter makes rows **a selection rather than a contiguous chain**. **Evidence:** `TestExportCapabilities` requires the field list to equal the catalogue **in order**, `max_rows == ExportTaskLimit`, the `minimal` preset to match `exportMinimalFields` element by element, `limits.audit_export_bytes == auditExportMaxBytes`, the audit entry to carry a `no column projection` note and **zero** selectable fields, the output download to advertise `supports_ranges`, and every entry to name its path.
- **The audit export can answer as one JSON document, and deliberately refuses to project columns.** `?format=json` returns `{count, truncated, filtered, format, rows:[…]}` for tools that do not stream, while `ndjson` stays the default; the format is part of the **ETag**, so a client that revalidated one cannot be told 304 for the other, and an unknown format is a **400**. **The decision worth recording is the one NOT implemented:** the task export lets callers choose columns, and it was tempting to mirror that here — but a verifier needs **every field the entry hash covers** plus both hashes, so a projected audit export would *look* verifiable while being **unverifiable**, which is worse than not offering it. The JSON rows therefore carry the same objects as the NDJSON, and the test asserts the presence of the hashed fields rather than trusting the comment. **Evidence:** `TestAuditExportJSONFormat` requires `application/json`, a `.json` filename, `count 3 / truncated false / filtered false / format "json"` with three rows, every verifier-relevant field non-empty (both hashes, user, action, resource, agent_id, details, created_at), `filtered: true` inside a filtered document (not only in a header), the default staying `application/x-ndjson` with three lines, **different ETags per format**, and **400** for `?format=xml`.
- **The task export gained three column presets: `?fields=minimal|default|full`.** The explicit list from the previous round is precise but verbal to type; the useful requests are few and nameable, so the common ones are now words: **`minimal`** (`task_id, agent, type, status` — the "what ran, did it work" set), **`default`** (exactly what an absent value produces, which matters because that set is **format-aware**: nine columns for CSV, plus `agent_id` for JSON) and **`full`** (every column in catalogue order, including `agent_id`). A preset **must be the whole value**: `minimal,time` has two plausible meanings — the preset plus a column, or a typo — so it is a **400 with the rule stated** rather than a guess, and an unknown name still names both the valid fields and the presets. Because the resolved selection goes into the ETag, `minimal` and `full` are different documents. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportFieldPresets` requires `minimal` → header `Task ID,Agent,Type,Status`, `default` to match an absent value in **both** formats (same CSV header, same JSON key count), `full` to include `Agent ID` and to have exactly as many columns as the catalogue, **400** with `cannot be combined` for `minimal,time`, **400** mentioning `presets:` for `bogus`, and different ETags for `minimal` and `full`.
- **New `?fields=` on the task export — choose the columns, in the order you want them.** Ten thousand rows carrying the result and error text of every one of them is often far more than the caller needs: "what ran, did it work" wants four columns. The selection is honoured by **both formats in the same order**, so a script and a spreadsheet cannot describe the same export differently, and an unknown name is a **400 that lists the valid ones** — a silently ignored column would look like a field that exists and was empty. **Two decisions worth stating:** the default set is **format-aware**, because the CSV has always shown nine columns including the hostname and adding `agent_id` would change a contract consumers already parse, while the JSON format (one round old) includes it since a script wants the id; and the selection is part of the **ETag**, because two column sets are two documents. The field catalogue is a table (`exportFields`) so the CSV header and the row values cannot drift apart — both now come from the same list. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportFieldSelection` requires `?fields=status,type` → header `Status,Type` and row `completed,shell`, the same two keys in JSON, an id-only selection (`?fields=task_id,agent_id`) whose header carries both, **400** for `fields=bogus` naming the bad field and the valid set, different ETags for `?fields=status` and `?fields=type`, an unchanged CSV default (no `Agent ID` column) and a JSON default that still carries `agent_id`. **The gate was right about the shape:** `check:command-separators` flagged the `"|"` I first used to join the format and the field list into the tag — it is an ETag component, not a task command, and switching it to **NUL** both silences the false positive and removes the theoretical collision.
- **The task export speaks JSON too: `?format=json`.** The endpoint only produced CSV, so a script had to parse a spreadsheet to read rows the server already had structured. The JSON variant returns one document (`{count, truncated, format, tasks:[…]}`) whose entries carry the **same values, the same redaction and the same column truncation** as the CSV — deliberately, so a script and a human reading the CSV can never disagree about what a cell held — plus `agent_id` as its own field and `task_id` as a **number**, which is the reason the format exists. The default stays CSV, the response says which it is in both `Content-Type` and the filename, and an unknown format is a **400** rather than a silent fallback. **The ETag now includes the format:** the CSV and the JSON of the same rows are different documents, so a client that revalidated the CSV must not be told **304** when it asks for the JSON. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportJSONFormat` requires `application/json`, a `.json` filename, `count 1 / truncated false / format "json"`, a row with the hostname, `agent_id`, type, status, the engagement name and a non-zero numeric `task_id`; unchanged `text/csv` for the default; **different** ETags for the two formats and the CSV's tag **not** satisfying a JSON request; and **400** for `?format=xlsx`.
- **The audit export gained `?until=` (and documented `?since=`), so an investigation can ask for "the night of the incident" instead of "everything after it".** Without an upper bound the only way to stop at an instant was to read the file and ignore the tail — the kind of manual step that gets skipped under pressure. Both bounds come from the **same parser as the task export** (relative `24h`/`7d`, or an RFC3339 instant), a window that ends before it starts is a **400** rather than an empty file that would read as "no audit rows that night", and `until` is part of the **ETag** so an until-only export cannot be served from a tag computed without it. Using either bound also flips the response into a **selection**: it keeps the `X-Audit-Export-Filtered` warning, because rows with gaps are not a contiguous chain. **Evidence:** `TestAuditExportUntilBounds` seeds three rows and requires no bound → 3, `until=48h` → only the three-day-old row, `since=4d&until=2d` → 1, `since=6h&until=30m` → the five-hour-old row, filtered header + warning on an until-only request, **400** for `until=whenever` and for a reversed window, and **different ETags** for the bounded and unbounded exports. **The bug this surfaced is about the fixture, and it is worth recording:** these columns are text carrying the writer's UTC offset, so seeding rows with `time.Now().UTC()` while the filter binds a **local** instant compared `15:39+00:00` against `23:39+08:00` — `since` matched nothing and `until` matched everything, in both directions. Production writes `time.Now()`, which is local; the fixture now matches the writer, with the reason in a comment.
- **The task CSV export is bounded by time: `?since=` and `?until=`.** The export could be limited and filtered by engagement but not by **time**, which is the first filter an operator reaches for ("the window of the exercise", "what ran last night"). Both spellings work — the dashboard's `24h`/`7d` (measured backwards from now) and an **RFC3339 instant** — and they come from **one shared parser** (`parseTimeBound`, `parseExportTimeBounds`) that the **audit export now uses too**, so "since 24h" cannot mean two different things depending on which export is open. `since` after `until` is a **400** rather than an empty file, an invalid bound is a **400**, and the validation runs **before the ETag** so a matching `If-None-Match` cannot serve a cached answer for a request that should have been rejected. The spec gained the parameter list, the 304 and the 400 for the endpoint, which previously documented only "CSV file". **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportTimeBounds` seeds tasks aged 1 minute, 5 hours, 3 days and 20 days and requires no bound → **4** rows, `since=24h` → **2**, an RFC3339 four-days-back cutoff → **3**, `until=48h` → **2** (the open-ended older half), `since=4d&until=2d` → exactly the three-day-old row, **400** for `since=forever`, `?until=whenever` and a reversed window, and **400 even with a matching ETag**. Two fixture mistakes I had to fix rather than the code: the export prints the agent **hostname**, so the first run compared empty strings, and a four-day cutoff legitimately includes a three-day-old row.
- **The task output download supports byte ranges, so a large result is resumable instead of all-or-nothing.** `GET /api/tasks/{taskId}/output` used to write the bytes directly: every request carried the whole result, and a client that already had the first half had no way to ask for the rest. It now goes through `http.ServeContent` with the body as a reader and the task's `UpdatedAt` as the modification time, which gives the standard behaviour for free — `Accept-Ranges: bytes` on a full response, **206 + `Content-Range`** for a range, open-ended ranges (`bytes=10-`), **416** for an unsatisfiable one, `If-Range` validation, and a **HEAD** that reports the size without a body. The content type is set explicitly beforehand so `ServeContent` does not sniff it, and the filename prefix stays what the download already promised. **Evidence:** `TestTaskOutputSupportsRange` fetches a 20-byte result and requires the full body with `Accept-Ranges: bytes`, `Range: bytes=5-9` → **206** with body `56789` and `Content-Range: bytes 5-9/20`, `bytes=10-` → the trailing half, `bytes=9999-10000` → **416**, and a HEAD with **zero** body bytes but `Content-Length: 20`. The spec gained the `Accept-Ranges` header and the 206/416 responses.
- **The diagnose endpoint now gives advice, not just verdicts.** The composed answer said what *would* happen; it now also reports what the verdict reveals about the **configuration**, three cases that all fail silently today: `template_variable_unfilled` (an action template using a variable this event cannot fill — `expandTemplate` substitutes by plain replacement, so `host {{hostname}} checked in` becomes `host  checked in` and nothing errors), `no_rule_listens` (an event no rule of the tenant handles, which otherwise looks like a successful diagnosis of nothing), and `no_route_would_deliver` / `no_notification_route` on the delivery half. The **operator trap it now explains**: `{{user}}` reads the event's own `user` field, **not** `data.user`, so a preview that supplies `data.user` still renders an empty `{{user}}` — the suggestion says so in as many words rather than leaving the distinction to be discovered in production. **Evidence:** the test builds a rule whose template needs `{{hostname}}` and `{{user}}` and drives four cases — `data.user` only (both variables reported, the `{{user}}` one carrying the `not from data.user` hint), `agent_host` + `user` (no template finding at all), an `info` notification against a `critical`-only route (`no_route_would_deliver`), and an event with no listener (`no_rule_listens`). Two structural fixes came out of the same test run: the suggestions are a **set** (map iteration order is not stable, so the assertion had to stop depending on which finding came last) and the section can no longer return a **nil slice**, which was marshalling as `null` and breaking a client's `suggestions.length`.
- **New `POST /api/automation/diagnose` — the two previews composed into one answer.** "Which automation rules would fire for this event" and "would a notification reach anyone" already existed as separate endpoints, which left the operator to run both and correlate them by hand while debugging a set. One call now returns both verdicts for the same event: the automation batch (firing rules, blocked-by-cooldown, blocked-by-conditions, disabled) and the notification delivery count for a candidate severity, with the `notification` block optional (defaults to `info`). It **runs nothing** — asserted: no notification persisted, no task created — and the successful call is audited as `automation_diagnose` with both counts, while a malformed request is a **400** before either half runs. The two halves are now shared functions (`diagnoseAutomation`, `diagnoseNotificationRoutes`) rather than duplicated logic, so the composed answer and the individual previews cannot drift. **Evidence:** the test seeds one firing rule, one cooling rule, one healthy route and one disabled route, and requires `automation.summary.would_run 1 / blocked_by_cooldown 1`, `notifications.would_deliver 1 / total_routes 2`, **400** for a missing `event_type` and for an unknown severity, **zero** notifications and **zero** tasks created, and exactly **one** audit row.
- **New `POST /api/notification-routes/preview` — "would this notification reach anyone".** Notifications are persisted first and fanned out in goroutines, so a route filtered out by severity, a disabled route and a route with an **unusable target** all look identical from the console: a notification that exists and reached **nobody**. The preview applies the **sender's own rules** — enabled, `min_severity`, and the same target validation the create/update path uses — to a candidate `{title, severity, …}` and reports each route's `would_deliver` with the precise reason it would not (`route is disabled`, `severity "warning" is below the route's minimum "critical"`, `telegram route has no bot token`, `invalid target: …`), plus how many of the configured routes would deliver **including zero**, which is the case worth knowing before a real beacon fails. Nothing is persisted or delivered — asserted — and only **successful** previews are audited, because a 400 is a malformed request rather than a delivery answer. **Evidence:** the test seeds five routes (healthy Discord, disabled, critical-only, Telegram without a token, webhook with an invalid URL) and requires one delivery for a `warning` with each of the four failures naming its own reason, **two** for a `critical` (the critical-only route joins), **400** for an unknown severity and for a missing title, **zero** notifications persisted and exactly **two** `notification_preview` audit rows.
- **New `GET /api/automation/health` — the static checks on a rule set that no per-event preview can reach.** The previews answer "what happens for this event"; this answers the questions about the **set**: `unconditional_mutating_action` (a rule with **no conditions** whose action changes state — it fires on **every** event of its type; a notify-only rule of the same shape is deliberately **not** reported, because it is noisy rather than dangerous), `missing_agent`/`missing_script`/`missing_macro` (an action targeting a resource that no longer exists would log a failure on every firing, forever, and nothing in the rule list says so), and `duplicate_rule` (same event, conditions and actions → the actions run twice). Errors come first, then by rule name; every finding carries a **stable `code`** for tooling. Read-only: no rule is modified and no action runs. **Two things the tests forced:** target lookups now include **tenant-0 rows** (legacy and globally-shared macros/scripts are real targets, and a health check that cries wolf is one that gets ignored — the first run reported an existing macro as missing), and the duplicate-grouping key uses a **NUL separator** rather than `"|"`, both because JSON may contain any printable separator (two different rules could collide) and because `check:command-separators` rightly flags an unvalidated `"|"` join — the gate was correct about the shape even though this is not a task command. **Evidence:** the test seeds an unconditional command rule, a notify-only rule of the same shape, a dangling agent target, a healthy rule with an existing macro, two identical rules and another tenant's rule, then requires `unconditional_mutating_action` **1**, `missing_agent` **1**, `duplicate_rule` **2**, **nothing** for the healthy and notify-only rules, `errors 2 / warnings 2 / rules 6`, another tenant's rule absent, and the first finding an error.
- **New `POST /api/automation/preview` — the batch direction: "which rules would fire for this event".** The per-rule preview (round 33) answers "why did *this* rule not fire"; while designing an automation **set** the question is the other way round, and the interaction between rules (two rules on one event, one cooling down) is invisible when you validate them one at a time. The endpoint evaluates every rule of the caller's tenant that listens to the given event and reports `would_run`, `matched`, `cooldown_open`/`cooldown_remaining` and the **first** reason, with a summary split into `would_run` / `blocked_by_conditions` / `blocked_by_cooldown` / `disabled`; firing rules come first, then by name, so the answer opens with what actually happens. Two deliberate exclusions keep the report readable: rules for **other event types** are omitted rather than listed as "not firing", and **other tenants'** rules never appear. It shares `previewRuleVerdict` with the single-rule endpoint and uses the same cached rule snapshot the live path uses, so a preview and a real evaluation cannot disagree; a body without `event_type` is a **400**, because judging every rule against an empty event is meaningless. **Evidence:** the test seeds five rules (firing, cooling, disabled, other-event, other-tenant), invalidates the cache, and requires exactly **3** rules back with `total 3 / would_run 1 / blocked_by_cooldown 1 / disabled 1 / blocked_by_conditions 0`, `a-fires` first, the cooling rule **matched but blocked**, a **400** without `event_type`, tenant 2 seeing only its own rule, **zero** notifications created and exactly **2** `automation_preview_batch` audit rows.
- **New `POST /api/automation/rules/:id/preview` — dry-run a rule and say why it would not fire.** The most common automation complaint is a rule that silently does nothing: a condition on a field the event does not carry, an open cooldown, a disabled rule, a mismatched event type. None of that is visible from the rule list, and the alternative is waiting for a real beacon — which costs an engagement. The endpoint evaluates **one** rule against a synthetic event and reports `would_run`, `matched`, `cooldown_open`/`cooldown_remaining`, a **reason for each failed condition with the value actually resolved**, and each action with its **template expanded** (an unexpanded template hides a broken variable reference). It executes **nothing** — proven by asserting that a preview creates no notifications — and it is tenant-scoped, so another tenant's rule is a 404. **Three findings came out of building it, all caught by the tests:** (1) conditions address data as `data.user` while the body naturally nests `{"data":{"user":…}}`, so the preview namespaces the keys — without it the tool reported "did not match" for a rule that would fire, a false negative in the one thing built to explain false negatives; (2) action templates use `{{hostname}}`/`{{user}}`/… , not `{{agent.hostname}}`, so the body now carries `agent_ip`/`agent_os`/`user` and the preview resolves every documented variable; (3) **a real product bug** — GORM's `default:true` on `Enabled` meant a rule created **disabled** came back **enabled and started firing on its own**, because `Save()` inserts and INSERT applies column defaults; `saveAutomationRule` now writes the column explicitly, with `TestSaveAutomationRulePersistsDisabled` covering both directions. **Evidence:** the preview test requires a matching rule to report `would_run` with both conditions resolved and the template expanded to `host web-01 seen for alice`, a failing condition to name both sides, a disabled rule to be explained, an **open cooldown to block a rule whose conditions match** (the case operators mistake for "broken"), a wrong event type to be refused, exactly **5** `automation_preview` audit rows, **zero** notifications, and 404 for another tenant's rule and a missing one.
- **The task export can now be selected by engagement too, completing the trio.** Round 25 let the agent list be filtered by `?engagement=`, round 31 made the export *say* which engagement a row came from; this makes the export **selectable** by the same parameter, so an operator who exports "this exercise" receives this exercise rather than the whole tenant with a column to sort by. Semantics are identical to the list (`<id>` = that exercise, `none` = agents in no exercise, absent = whole tenant), implemented as a **tenant-scoped subquery** rather than a join so the export stays one statement. Two ordering details matter: the parameter is **validated before the ETag is computed**, because an invalid value must answer **400** rather than be served from a tag that ignores it; and the subquery carries `tenantScope`, so naming another tenant's engagement id selects nothing instead of leaking its tasks. **Evidence:** the test seeds two in-scope tasks, one unscoped task and one in another tenant, then requires `?engagement=<id>` → exactly the two `web-01` rows, `?engagement=none` → exactly `web-02`'s task, no filter → all three of the tenant's tasks (the parameter narrows, never replaces), `?engagement=not-a-number` → **400**, and another tenant naming this engagement id → **zero rows**.
- **The task CSV export now carries the engagement, so the file agrees with the filter that produced it.** Round 25 let the agent list be filtered by `?engagement=`, which left the export contradicting it: an operator could select one exercise's hosts, export their tasks, and receive a file that could not say which exercise it was. `Engagement` is appended as the **last** column (additive for anything reading the existing header), filled from one lookup of the agents' engagement names — **two queries total**, not one per row, because the export is capped but a per-row join would still scale with the limit. An agent that belongs to no exercise gets an **empty cell**, which is the honest value rather than a placeholder like "none" that a reader could mistake for a real engagement name. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportCarriesTheEngagement` seeds an in-scope agent and an unscoped one, exports both, and requires `Engagement` to be the last header, every row to have the same field count as the header, the in-scope row to carry the exercise name, and the unscoped row to carry an empty string; `TestTaskExportIsSentWhole` was updated to the new width (8 → 9 fields) and now also pins the empty cell for an unlinked agent. **A fixture mistake rather than a code one:** the first run returned only the header because the seeded tasks had no tenant — the export is tenant-scoped, so they were correctly excluded.
- **The cache's benefit is now a regression guard, not just a benchmark number.** Last round measured **30.5 ms/op → 12.3 µs/op**; a benchmark nobody runs on a schedule protects nothing, so `TestStatsHandlerAllocationBudget` turns the same measurement into an assertion using **allocation counts**, which are deterministic where a timing ratio would flake on a busy CI box. It runs the endpoint on a 5000-task window and requires the cached path to stay under **500 allocations per call** (measured **56**) and the uncached path to stay above **10,000** (measured **170,238**) — the second half matters because it proves the two variants really differ, so the first half cannot pass for the wrong reason. Thresholds carry ~10× headroom on purpose: the point is to catch a **lost cache** or a runaway decode, not to freeze today's exact numbers. **Evidence:** the probe is the point of the round — disabling the cache lookup (`get(...)` result ignored) makes the test fail with `cached path allocated 170247 objects per call ... the cache is not being hit`, and restoring the line makes it pass again; the passing run logs `allocations per call: cached 56, uncached 170238 (ratio 3040x)`.
- **The statistics cache now has a handler-level number instead of an isolated-query one.** `BenchmarkTaskDurationStatsHandler` runs the whole endpoint — query, aggregation, JSON envelope — with and without the cache, on the same 5000-task window: **uncached 30.5 ms/op** (36.9 MB and **170,270 allocations** per call) versus **cached 12.3 µs/op** (9,983 B, 56 allocations). That is roughly **2,480×** for a polling client, and it also exposes *why* the read was expensive: decoding five thousand rows allocates a hundred and seventy thousand objects, which the earlier isolated-query benchmark (27.97 ms) could not show. The architecture: the cache was added three rounds before this measurement, on the strength of the query number; the metrics added afterwards (`stats_cache_hits_total`/`misses`/`evictions`) made it observable; this benchmark closes the loop by measuring the path an operator actually exercises, and the two variants sit in one file so the comparison cannot drift (same data, same context setup, same assertion). **Evidence:** `-benchtime 20x` → `uncached-14 30535470 ns/op 36918720 B/op 170270 allocs/op`, `cached-14 12305 ns/op 9983 B/op 56 allocs/op`. **A note on the output:** the seeding step prints the whole batch INSERT (5000 rows), which floods the benchmark log — the numbers above are from the tail, and the full output was spilled to a temp file rather than summarised away.
- **New `GET /api/engagements/:id/summary/export` — the summary as a document a person can read.** The JSON endpoint answers a machine; a report has to answer someone who **was not there**, and it usually lands in a document rather than an API client. The export renders Markdown by default (window with its state as a word, fleet covered, task outcomes, and the audited actions as a table) or the raw JSON with `format=json`, with the filename carrying the engagement id and a **timestamp** so two exports in a day do not collide. Two structural choices: the summary is **computed once** by `buildEngagementSummary` and shared by the JSON endpoint and the export, so the two can never describe the same exercise differently; and the body is **fully buffered before anything is written**, the rule the CSV and audit exports already follow — a truncated document that still opens is worse than an error. **Evidence:** the test requires `text/markdown`, a report containing the engagement name, its **notes**, the human phrasing `Fleet covered: 1 host(s)`, the outcome line and the `| task_create | 1 |` row, **no raw API field names** in the body, a `.md` filename containing the id, the JSON variant with `.json`, **400** for `format=pdf`, and **404** when another tenant asks. **One expectation I had to correct rather than the code:** my filename assertion trimmed the trailing quote and then looked for `.md"`; the header was right all along.
- **The engagement window itself now has the reminders the per-implant deadline got: "closes in a day" and "has closed, the summary is ready".** One host stopping is a task; a **whole fleet** stopping is the end of the exercise, and until now the only warning was per implant — so an operator could be told about twenty hosts individually and never be told the thing that actually happened. Two new notifications ride the existing scan (`engagement_ending`, `engagement_ended`), deduped by engagement **and its end date** so extending a window re-arms the reminder while a restart does not repeat it; the ended notice points at the summary endpoint added last round. Early-closed engagements produce **neither** notice — their window is over by decision, not by time. **Evidence:** the test seeds a window closing in 12 hours, one a month out, one already over and one closed early, and requires exactly **1 closing + 1 ended** notice with messages naming the engagement, **0** on a second pass, **1 more closing notice after the deadline is moved**, audit rows of **2 `engagement_ending` / 1 `engagement_ended`**, and — the assertion I had to rewrite — **no notice at all for the early-closed engagement**; my first version demanded zero notices in total and failed because the extended window legitimately expired by then.
- **New `GET /api/engagements/:id/summary` — "what did this exercise actually do".** Closing an engagement (or letting it end) leaves the question every report must answer, and until now it could only be assembled by hand from three pages. The endpoint is a read-only roll-up over the engagement's **own window**: the fleet it covered (`total`/`online`/`expired`), the tasks run and their outcomes, and the **highest-count audited actions** taken while it was open (capped at 25, already tamper-evident). The window runs from `starts_at` to the **earliest of `ends_at`, `closed_at` and now** — the same merge the deadline enforcement uses, because counting past the effective end would attribute the **next** engagement's work to this one. Tasks are counted only for agents linked to this engagement, and another tenant's engagement is a **404**. **Evidence:** the test seeds a closed engagement with two members (one online, one offline and expired), three tasks inside the window plus one before it and one belonging to an unlinked agent, and audit rows inside, before and in another tenant — then requires `agents = 2/1/1`, `tasks = 1 completed / 1 failed / 1 pending` (with the pre-window and foreign tasks excluded), `actions = [task_create x2, login x1]` in that order (outside-window and other-tenant rows excluded), `window.open = false`, and 404 for both another tenant's engagement and a non-existent id. **One expectation I had to fix rather than the code:** the fixture guessed "online" as a one-minute last-seen and failed with `Online: 0` — it is now derived from `s.offlineThreshold()`, because the definition of online belongs to the server, not to a test constant.
- **New: `GET /api/agents?engagement=<id>` — "who is in this exercise", and `engagement=none` for the inverse.** Round 23's roll-up says *how many* hosts an engagement covers; this says **which** ones, and the inverse is the more useful half in practice: an implant with **no engagement has no window and no deadline**, so "which implants will no deadline ever stop" is the question to ask before an engagement is declared over. The filter is additive (absent = whole fleet), accepts a numeric id or the literal `none`, and a non-numeric value answers **400** rather than silently returning everything — a filter that quietly does nothing is worse than an error. **One implementation (`applyEngagementFilter` + `validateEngagementQuery`) shared by the page handler and the JSON list**, because this codebase has already paid for drift between two copies more than once (the discarded-result and ETag guards exist for the same reason); the page handler responds with the error, the shared query builder stays tolerant and the JSON writer validates up front so its caller gets the 400. **Evidence:** the test seeds two imps in one engagement, one unlinked and one in another tenant, and requires `?engagement=<id>` to return exactly the two members, `?engagement=none` exactly the unlinked one, no filter to return all three of the tenant's agents, `?engagement=not-a-number` to answer **400**, and another tenant naming the same engagement id to see only its own agent (tenant scoping is not bypassed by the parameter).
- **The engagement window now bounds work at BOTH ends: a task before the window opens is refused, not just after it closes.** Round 22 enforced the end, which left the left bound unenforced — and that is the side where a premature action is most likely (a rehearsal run the day before, a test at the wrong site, a build that phones home early). The creation gate now loads the engagement **once** and answers both questions from the same row, refuses with **403** and a message naming **when the window opens**, and records a **distinct** audit action (`agent_not_started_block`) so "why was I blocked before the exercise" never reads as "expired". Closing a not-yet-started engagement does **not** make it startable: closing limits the end, it does not rewrite the beginning. The helpers were split so this costs one query, not two: `engagementFor` loads the row, `effectiveDeadlineFor` merges it, and the two predicates are pure. **Evidence:** the test seeds a not-yet-started engagement, a running one and an unlinked implant, and requires the first to be refused with `clientErrorStatus` mapping to **403** and the refusal to contain the start timestamp, the audit to show **one** `agent_not_started_block` and **zero** `agent_expired_block` (the two reasons must not be confused), the running and unlinked implants to be accepted, and closing the future engagement to leave it unstartable.
- **`GET /api/engagements/expiry` now rolls up per engagement.** Last round made an engagement's window govern the work, so the question an operator actually asks changed from "which host is about to expire" to "**how much time is left on this exercise and how many hosts does it cover**" — a per-host list cannot answer that. Each engagement in the response carries its derived `state`, `ends_in_seconds`, the number of implants linked to it and how many of those are already past their deadline. Tenant-scoped and ordered by when each engagement ends, so the next thing to lapse is first. **A projection mistake avoided by a comment with history:** the implant query had to add `engagement_id`, and this package has twice shipped a projection that omitted a column the aggregation read (round 247's `status`, round 245's `agent_id`) — the select carries a note pointing at both. **Evidence:** `TestEngagementExpiryRollsUpPerEngagement` seeds two tenant-1 engagements plus a tenant-2 one and five implants (two in one exercise — one already expired — one in the other, one unlinked, one in the other tenant), and requires **two** engagements back (tenant isolation), ordered soonest-ending first, `short exercise` = `agents 2 / agents_expired 1`, `long exercise` = `agents 1 / agents_expired 0`, a positive `ends_in_seconds` bounded by three days, `state: active`, and a fleet `total` of **4** that still counts the unlinked agent. The spec entry was replaced (not appended) so the documented schema matches the new shape, and the types were regenerated.
- **Engagements now actually gate work: an implant's effective deadline is the EARLIER of its own and the engagement window it belongs to.** Round 21 created the container and round 22 connected it: `RegSecret.EngagementID` is inherited into `Implant.EngagementID` at registration (one query returns both bounds), and `effectiveAgentDeadline` merges the two — a closed engagement ends at **`ClosedAt`** rather than its agreed `EndsAt` (closing early is a decision, and the earlier end is the one that applies), a missing engagement falls back to the implant deadline so an unreadable row can never block work, and the refusal message/audit now name the **effective** deadline so an operator knows whether the implant or the exercise stopped it. Installations without engagements pay nothing: the lookup only happens when an implant is linked to one. **A real bug the enforcement test caught in my own implementation:** the pre-check projected only `id, expires_at`, so `engagement_id` was always nil there and the merge **silently never applied** — the whole feature was inert on the real path while the unit-level merge looked right. Evidence: the test seeds a short engagement, a long one, one closed an hour ago, an implant with no engagement and one pointing at a missing engagement, and requires the earlier deadline to win in each case, the closed-engagement agent to **refuse a task despite having no implant deadline of its own**, the missing-engagement case to fall back rather than block, and `regSecretBounds` + `ensureBeaconImplantRow` to carry both bounds onto the implant row while a plain secret carries neither.
- **New: engagements — the time-boxed container above the tenant.** A tenant isolates **data** (whose fleet); an engagement bounds **when** work may happen, and gives the deadline that rounds 17–20 made enforceable a natural home so an operator records an exercise once instead of setting a deadline on every implant. `POST /api/engagements` creates one, `GET /api/engagements` lists the tenant's windows newest-first, and `POST /api/engagements/:id/close` ends one early. Three decisions came out of writing it: the **state is derived, not stored** (`scheduled`/`active`/`ended`/`closed` from the timestamps and `closed_at`, so it can never drift from the dates); **closing preserves the agreed window** — an early end is a decision, and rewriting `ends_at` would erase the terms the exercise was agreed under; and a new window that **overlaps an open engagement in the same tenant is refused with 409**, because two simultaneous exercises against one customer is the kind of mistake worth blocking at creation rather than discovering when two teams task the same host (closed engagements are history and never block). **Two real bugs the lifecycle test caught in my own first version:** `GET` and `close` were **not tenant-scoped**, so one tenant could list and even close another's engagement — both now go through `tenantScope`, and a second `_test` failure showed the handler-dispatch helper matching on path alone (the list call hit the create handler) — the helper now matches on method too. `Engagement` was also missing from `AutoMigrate` **and** from the test harness's migration list, which surfaced as a bare `500 Query failed`. The `spread of the new routes** was caught by the round-13 OpenAPI guard, and both paths plus an `Engagement` schema were added rather than allowlisted. **Evidence:** the test creates an active engagement, refuses an overlapping one with 409, allows a future non-overlapping one and the same dates in another tenant, requires the list to be tenant-scoped and newest-first, closes the first (state `closed`, `ends_at` unchanged, double-close 409), requires a replacement window to succeed after the close, demands 400 for each malformed body, and requires the create/close audit rows.
- **New `POST /api/agents/:id/expiry` — the deadline is now operable, not just build-time.** Round 17 made the deadline enforceable but settable only through the registration secret, which is the right default (it travels with the binary) and the wrong *only* option: engagements get extended, a host gets pulled out of scope, and an operator who cannot record that ends up keeping it in their head — exactly what the deadline exists to stop. The endpoint sets, extends or clears one implant's deadline, and reports the **previous value** back so the audit line reads as a decision rather than a toggle.
