A deadline **in the past is accepted on purpose** — it is how an engagement ends for a single host right now — and the response says `expired: true` instead of pretending it is scheduled; clearing removes the deadline and the implant accepts work again. **Evidence:** the test sets a future deadline and reloads the row, extends it and checks `previous`, sets a past deadline and requires the **task gate to refuse** the agent, clears and requires the gate to accept again, demands **400** for a missing field / an unparseable timestamp / a non-RFC3339 format, counts **4** `agent_expiry_set` audit rows, and requires **404** when the agent belongs to another tenant. **One expectation I had to correct rather than the code:** I first counted 3 audit rows and the test failed with 4 — the past-deadline step is its own recorded decision, and the comment now says so.
- **New: a warning a day before an engagement deadline stops work.** Round 17 made the deadline enforceable and round 18 made it visible, but both need someone to look — the failure they leave standing is concrete: a collection runs at 23:50 on the last night, the deadline passes at midnight, and every task afterwards is refused **correctly and as a surprise**. A background scan now announces each implant whose deadline falls inside the next **24 hours** through the existing notification pipeline (type `engagement_expiring`, severity `warning`, message naming the exact stop time) and records `engagement_expiring` in the audit trail, so "was anyone told" is answerable later. Three properties are enforced rather than hoped for: **one notification per implant per deadline** (the dedupe key includes the deadline, so extending an engagement re-arms the reminder for the new date while a restart does not repeat it); **the expired and the unbounded are not announced** (round 17 already refuses the first, the second has nothing to say); and the scan is **bounded** (500 rows per tick) so a fleet with thousands of deadlines cannot turn a timer tick into a long query. The worker is started next to the other periodic cleanup loops and carries a `recover`, in keeping with the goroutine rule for this package. **Evidence:** the test seeds an implant inside the window, one three days out, one already expired and one unbounded, and requires exactly **1** announcement, the notification's agent/severity/message shape, **0** on a second scan, **1 more** after the deadline is moved, and **2** audit rows total; a second test pins the window boundary (exactly 24 h in, one second beyond out).
- **New `GET /api/engagements/expiry` — the deadlines made enforceable last round, made visible.** A deadline an operator cannot see is still a surprise: the fleet stops accepting work one day and the first sign is a 403. The endpoint summarises how the caller's fleet ages out in the buckets people plan around — **expired**, **within 24h**, **within 7d**, **unbounded** — names the **earliest** deadline for a status line, and lists the bounded implants **ordered by deadline** so a UI can render "what is next". Tenant-scoped, `agents.read`, read-only on purpose: closing an engagement early is a write with consequences for running work and belongs with the engagement entity, not smuggled in as a flag on a status endpoint. **Evidence:** the test seeds one agent in each bucket plus an unbounded one and a second tenant's expired agent, and requires `total 5` (tenant isolation), `unbounded 1`, `expired 1`, `within_24h 1`, `within_7d 1`, `bounded 4`, `earliest` = the expired agent, a list ordered from `already-expired` to `expires-next-month`, tenant 2 seeing only its own, and an empty tenant reporting zeros **and `agents: []`** rather than nulls. **Two process notes worth keeping:** the route was first registered as `engagements/expiry` on a group whose prefix is `/`, which put it at a path no client would guess and **outside** the OpenAPI coverage guard's view (it only compares `/api` paths) — the guard caught it as soon as the path was corrected, and the spec entry was added rather than allowlisted.
- **Engagement deadline on implants: a registration secret can now carry an expiry, and every implant built from it refuses work after that date.** This is the borrow from the C2 survey I ranked highest, and the reason is not capability: a deadline that exists only on the operator's calendar is **not a control**. `RegSecret.ExpiresAt` is inherited into `Implant.ExpiresAt` at registration (`regSecretExpiry`, which treats a lookup failure as "unbounded" so it can never block enrolment), and the deadline is enforced in **`validateTaskCreationWithAgent`** — the single gate every creation path (handlers, bulk actions, automation, workflows, scripts) already goes through, so no path can disagree about it. An expired implant gets a **403-class refusal that names the deadline**, plus an **`agent_expired_block`** audit record, because "why did this stop working" must be answerable after the fact. `nil` means unbounded, so every existing installation behaves exactly as before. **Evidence:** the test seeds an expired, a live and an unbounded agent and requires the expired one to be refused with `clientErrorStatus` mapping to **403**, the other two to be accepted, and exactly **one** `agent_expired_block` audit row; a second test proves the deadline is inherited from the secret (`regSecretExpiry`) and that a missing secret or an empty id yields `nil` rather than an error.
- **New `POST /api/plugins/index/verify` — compare an installation against an index, without installing or fetching anything.** Last round's export answers "what is installed"; this answers the questions an index alone cannot: is this deployment still the set the team agreed on (`missing` / `extra`), did someone's package drift under the same name (`changed`, with both digests), and is the index even checkable (`unverifiable`). It needs **no trust decision** because nothing is downloaded or installed — that is exactly why it could ship before an install-from-index endpoint, whose "whose index, over what transport" question is a design of its own. The body is bounded (**4 MiB**, **2000 entries**) and every comparison is audited with its counts. **A logic bug the test caught in my own first version:** `in_sync` was computed as `missing==0 && extra==0 && changed==0`, so an entry with **no digest** was listed under `unverifiable` **and** the response said `in_sync: true` — a false all-clear, worse than no answer. The clause is now part of the expression, and the probe proves it is load-bearing: removing it reproduces exactly `an unverifiable entry was reported in sync`. The shared `buildPluginIndex` was extracted so the export and the comparison cannot compute digests differently. **Evidence:** the test requires a matching index to be in sync, a wrong digest to land in `changed`, a digest-less entry in `unverifiable` (and **not** in sync), an unknown name in `missing`, plus 400s for an empty and a malformed document; the spec entry and types were regenerated rather than allowlisted by the round-13 route guard.
- **New `GET /api/plugins/index` — the installed plugin set as a verifiable document.** This is the foundation a package registry needs (the role Sliver's Armory plays) and the answer to a concrete question: "what exactly is installed on that deployment?" Each entry carries name, version, type, interpreter, entry, **parameter names**, whether it is signed, whether the package is currently loaded, and the **package digest computed with `plugin.PackageDigest`** — the same function the trust policy uses, so the index is *verifiable* rather than descriptive: a receiver can recompute it. A record whose package directory is missing is reported with `loaded: false` and **no digest** instead of a fabricated one; the list is **sorted by name** so two deployments can be diffed, and two calls return identical bytes. Read-only on purpose: installing *from* an index needs a trust decision (whose index, over what transport) and deserves its own design. **Evidence:** the test creates a real plugin plus a record with no package, and requires two sorted entries, the ghost marked `loaded:false`/unsigned/no digest, the real one's digest to equal an independent `PackageDigest` recomputation, its interpreter/entry/params present, and two calls to produce byte-identical output. **The spec guard from round 13 caught the new route immediately** (`/api/plugins/index is registered but not in api/openapi.yaml`) — the entry was added to the spec and the types regenerated rather than allowlisted, which is exactly the behaviour that guard exists for.
- **The spec check now parses the document and covers *usability*, not just presence.** Round 13's check scanned `^  /path:` lines, which forced hand-rolled normalisation and produced 25 false "missing route" reports; `readSpec` now unmarshals `api/openapi.yaml` with `gopkg.in/yaml.v3` and inspects **operations**, so a path that exists but says nothing is caught: every documented operation must carry an **`operationId` and at least one response**, the minimum for a client generator or a reader to use it. Measured after the change: **600 documented paths, 1356 operations, all of them complete**, and **226 registered `/api` paths, none undocumented**. A number I had to correct in the same change: `operations` is keyed by both the plain and the `/api`-prefixed spelling, so reporting `len(operations)` as "documented paths" claimed **1186** where the document has **600** — the count now comes from the parsed `paths` map and the misleading figure is gone from the log.
- **New guard `TestDocumentedAPIRoutesAreInTheSpec`: every `/api` route the router registers must exist in `api/openapi.yaml`.** Round 12 documented three endpoints that had been code-only, and the reason they could stay invisible became a permanent check: `check:openapi-types` compares the **generated types** to the spec, so a route the spec never mentions is invisible to it. The new test builds the real router (`New` + `SetupRoutes`), reads the spec, and compares the two — the exact counterpart of `TestFrontendAPIPathsExist`, which checks the console's calls against the router. The allowlist is explicit and shrink-only: the beacon-facing endpoints (`/api/report`, `/api/task`, `/api/register`, `/api/download`, `/api/upload` and their `/api/v1` aliases), the WebSocket upgrades, and the two public landing pages. **The measurement mattered more than the assertion:** the first version reported 25+ missing paths, and every one was the test's own normalisation — the spec is inconsistent about the `/api` prefix (597 paths, most written without it) and spells parameters `{id}` where gin reports `:id`. Accepting both spellings changed the answer from "massive drift" to **226 distinct /api paths, 0 undocumented**, which is the claim worth making. A `len(seen) < 50` guard keeps the test from passing vacuously if the router or the parser breaks.
- **The three endpoints this session added are now in `api/openapi.yaml`, with types regenerated.** `/api/stats/tasks/duration`, `/api/tasks/{taskId}/output` and `/api/agents/{id}/tasks/rerun-failed` existed only in code: an API consumer reading the spec could not discover them, `npm run gen:openapi` produced no types for them, and the `check:openapi-types` gate was happy because it only compares the generated file to the spec — it cannot see a route the spec never mentions. All three are documented with their real parameters, the response **headers** that carry meaning (`X-Task-Status`, `X-Task-Final`), the `400` cases, and the properties a client must be able to rely on: `truncated` on the statistics (a percentile is never computed from a silent partial sample), the error appended to a failed task's download (never a file that omits why it failed), and `excluded`/`already queued` on the bulk rerun (control types and double clicks are reported, not dropped). **Evidence:** the three paths are present at lines 11699, 11779 and 11807; `npm run gen:openapi` regenerated `frontend/src/lib/api-schema.d.ts` (+209 lines, all three paths); `node scripts/check-openapi-types.mjs` answers **OK: src/lib/api-schema.d.ts matches api/openapi.yaml**; all 31 gates pass. **Known gap, stated rather than implied:** the spec has 597 paths while the server registers 646 route registrations, and nothing compares the two — a `check:openapi-coverage` gate is the right next step, but it needs a path-normalisation pass and an honest allowlist, so it is not smuggled in here.
- **The task CSV export is now correlatable, and two exports in one day no longer overwrite each other.** The export had every column except the one an analyst needs first: **which task is this row?** — so a suspicious result could not be matched back to the console without guessing from timestamp and command. `Task ID` is now the **last** column, deliberately: it is additive for anything that reads the existing header, and it survives `csvSafe` (a plain number). The filename also gained a time (`forgec2_tasks_2026-10-28_190613.csv` instead of `…2026-10-28.csv`), because two downloads on the same day previously collided in the browser's download folder and the second silently replaced the first. **Evidence:** `TestTaskExportIsSentWhole` now requires the `Task ID` header, **8 fields per row**, a numeric last column, and a `Content-Disposition` matching `forgec2_tasks_[0-9]{4}-[0-9]{2}-[0-9]{2}_[0-9]{6}\.csv` — the filename assertion failed first with the old date-only name and passes now.
- **One ETag *renderer* as well as one comparison: `weakETag` absorbed the second sanitiser, and the gate now covers both halves.** Round 5 consolidated the comparison after writing a guard for it; writing the tags themselves was still split, and `task_export_etag.go` had grown `sanitizeETagToken` purely to clean a timestamp before interpolating it into a raw `` `W/"…"` `` format string — so two places escaped tag values and **only one handled control characters**. The task export now builds a plain body and passes it through `weakETag`, `sanitizeETagToken` is gone, and `weakETag` replaces quotes, commas, spaces, backslashes **and** control characters (a newline in a header value breaks the response, not just the tag). `check:etag-impl` gained a second rule for the renderer: a `` `W/"` `` string literal outside `etag.go` is a violation. **Evidence:** the gate reports `0 duplicate reader(s)` on the tree and **exit 1 naming `zz_probe_render.go:6`** when a second renderer is introduced; `TestWeakETagEscapesHeaderBreakers` feeds a value containing a quote, comma, space, backslash, newline and tab and requires none of them to survive inside the tag, that the tag matches itself through `etagMatches`, and that two different bodies do not match.
- **The statistics cache key is now impossible to leave behind: the request's identity is a struct, and the key is that struct printed.** The cache added two rounds ago built its key from a hand-written argument list (`tenant, since, type, group_by, bucket`), which is the kind of thing that fails **silently**: add a parameter that shapes the query but not the key, and callers start receiving another query's answer. `durationStatsQuery` now holds the whole identity and `key()` renders it with `fmt.Sprintf("%+v")`, so **every field is in the key by construction** — the only way to add a parameter is to add a field, and then it is included. **Evidence:** `TestDurationStatsQueryKeyCoversEveryField` walks each of the five fields and requires that changing **only that field** changes the key (tenant, since, type, group_by, bucket), and that a disabled TTL yields no key at all so nothing is stored or read. Measured cost is unchanged: the cache still exists because the read it avoids has a measured 27.97 ms price.
- **Consolidated the ETag comparison — and the new gate found a *third* copy that predated the two I wrote.** Rounds 3 and 4 added conditional requests to the audit export and the task CSV export, and each grew its own "parse `If-None-Match`, compare tags" loop. Writing the guard (`check:etag-impl`, the 31st gate) for that duplicate immediately surfaced a **third** implementation: `etagMatches` in `static_assets.go`, which had been serving the static-asset middleware for far longer — so one comparison existed three times and only one of them handled weak validators on both sides. It is the same shape that produced `guardedClient` after rounds 232/233 fixed a zero-timeout hole in one of two copies of an HTTP client wrapper. There is now **one** canonical `etagMatches(header, etag)` in `etag.go` (weak validators on either side, `*`, comma lists trimmed but never morphed), with `ifNoneMatch(c, etag)` as the handler-side wrapper and the static middleware calling the raw form. The gate flags the duplicate **marker** — `strings.TrimPrefix(..., "W/")` — outside `etag.go`, so a file may still read the header and delegate but may not re-implement the compare; `TestIfNoneMatchUnit` covers exact, unquoted, wildcard and non-matching inputs. **Evidence:** the gate reports `0 duplicate reader(s)` on the tree and **exit 1 naming `zz_probe_etag.go:6`** when a comparison copy is reintroduced; wired into both task lists (31/31 for `npm run check`, 27/27 for `check:pre`, both exit 0) and documented in `AGENTS.md`.
- **The task CSV export can be revalidated too (`ETag` → 304), and the tag is keyed on the right column.** Same motivation as the audit export one round earlier: an operator re-runs the export after every batch, and downloading it again only to learn nothing changed wastes both sides. The interesting part is what the tag is *not* derived from: an **id-only** tag would be wrong for this export, because the columns that matter (`Result`, `Status`) change on tasks that already exist — a finishing task creates no new row. The tag therefore combines the newest **id** with the newest **updated_at** in the caller's scope, and the probe proves it is load-bearing: removing the `upd=` component makes the test fail with `after a task completed = 304, want 200`. `ETag` and `Cache-Control: private, no-cache` are set before the query, so a matching client costs neither side the read, and the 304 is written with `c.AbortWithStatus` (the same gin flush trap the audit export hit last round). **Evidence:** the test requires a first 200 with a tag and a non-empty CSV, a bodiless 304 on the same tag, a **changed tag when an existing task completes**, a changed tag when a new row appears, and tenant isolation in both directions (different tags, and tenant 1's tag does **not** satisfy tenant 2).
- **The audit export can now be revalidated instead of re-downloaded (`ETag` + `If-None-Match` → 304).** An investigator who keeps a copy usually wants to know "is there anything new", not another multi-megabyte NDJSON file. The export now returns `ETag: W/"v1-<tenant>-max=<newest id>-since=<since_id>-limit=<n>[-filtered=…]"` and `Cache-Control: private, no-cache`, and answers **304 with no body** when the client's tag still matches. The tag is derived from the **newest audit id**, which every append bumps — including the `audit_purge` marker a purge writes — so it changes whenever the export's content could have, and it covers the **tenant and every filter**, so two selections (or two tenants) can never share a tag; the test proves the cross-tenant case by sending tenant 1's tag to tenant 2 and requiring a full **200**. It is **weak on purpose**: two responses sharing a tag are equivalent for an investigator, not necessarily byte-identical. `If-None-Match: *` matches, as the spec says. **A real gotcha the test caught:** the first implementation wrote the status with `c.Status(304)`, and the recorder reported **200** even though the handler had matched and returned early — gin has to *flush* the header, which `c.AbortWithStatus(304)` does. The shipped code uses the flushing form, and the comparison helper has its own unit test (`TestAuditExportNotModifiedUnit`) covering exact, unquoted, wildcard and non-matching inputs, so a future regression points at the helper rather than at the handler.
- **The statistics cache is now observable: `forgec2_stats_cache_hits_total` / `_misses_total` / `_evictions_total`.** Last round added the cache on the strength of a 27.97 ms measurement, but a cache you cannot see is a guess: a hit rate near zero means the TTL is wrong for how the console polls, and a climbing eviction count means the key space outgrew the 256-entry cap — neither was visible anywhere before. The three counters sit on `MetricsCollector` alongside the existing ones (registered, not just declared, so `/metrics` actually exposes them) and are incremented on the real paths: a hit in the handler, a miss only when a cache key exists (a disabled TTL is not a miss), and an eviction when the cache empties itself at the cap. A collector attached *after* the cache was created still gets the counters (`setMetrics`), because a test — or a server path that installs metrics late — otherwise silently records nothing. **Evidence:** the new test installs a real `MetricsCollector` on the server, calls the endpoint three times with identical parameters, and requires **misses = 1, hits = 2, evictions = 0** read back through `promtestutil.ToFloat64`.
- **Measure first: the statistics endpoint now has a bounded, tenant-keyed cache because the read was expensive, not because caching is fashionable.** `BenchmarkDurationStatsWindow` (new) costs the actual query behind `GET /api/stats/tasks/duration` over a 5000-row window: **27.97 ms/op**, and the console polls the endpoint while an operator watches the page. A ten-second-old p95 is the same decision as a fresh one, so repeat calls inside the TTL are answered without touching the database. Three properties are enforced rather than assumed: the key includes the **tenant and every parameter**, so one operator's scoped view can never be served to another; only **successful** answers are stored (an error must never be remembered as the current state); and the map is capped at **256** entries, dropping everything when full rather than implementing an eviction policy that could be subtly wrong. The cache is created **lazily** (`statsCache()`), because servers built outside `New` — including every test harness — would otherwise silently run without one, which is exactly what the first test run showed (`sampled = 2` after seeding, i.e. no hit at all). **Evidence:** the test requires a repeat call inside the TTL to keep returning the stale count (1), a different `?since=` to recompute and see the new row (2), a different tenant to get its own entry (0, not the leaked payload), and a disabled TTL to bypass the cache entirely (2).
- **New guard: `TestDurationStatsQueryUsesAnIndex` (internal/db).** The endpoint's query and the bulk-rerun candidate query are checked with `EXPLAIN QUERY PLAN`: both must be served by `idx_tasks_tenant_created` with no `SCAN tasks` and no `TEMP B-TREE FOR ORDER BY`. Measured plans (recorded in the test log): all three shapes use `SEARCH tasks USING INDEX idx_tasks_tenant_created (tenant_id=? AND created_at>?)` — so the endpoints added in this batch need no new index, and the test now fails if a future query shape loses that.
- **New gate `check:test-stdio` (29 → 30 gates): a test must not close a handle it does not own.** Round 254's root cause became a permanent guard instead of a commit message. The gate flags a `for … range *.sinks` loop that calls `Close()` without skipping `os.Stdout`/`os.Stderr` **inside the loop body** — and that "inside the loop body" is the whole story of its first version: it checked whether the *file* mentioned `os.Stdout` anywhere, which passed a probe that had reintroduced the defect exactly as it shipped. The corrected rule catches a synthetic probe (`zz_probe_stdout_test.go:9 closes objects from a sink loop without skipping os.Stdout/os.Stderr`) and passes the real, fixed file. Wired into **both** task lists (30/30 labels and tasks for `npm run check`, 26/26 for `check:pre`, both exit 0), documented in `AGENTS.md` with the failure it prevents, and its scope is stated on purpose: it catches the construct that shipped, not every way to misuse a handle.
- **The plugin tools only work if the model knows them: the default system prompt now teaches the workflow, and a test keeps it there.** Four tools shipped (`create_plugin`, `run_plugin`, `update_plugin`, `delete_plugin`) but the default prompt's "useful starting tools" list never mentioned them, so an operator who had not written a custom prompt had a loop the model had no reason to enter. The prompt now carries a **"Writing plugins"** section: the four steps in order, the **JSON-on-stdout ABI** spelled out, "always run a plugin you wrote and fix it with `update_plugin` before telling the operator it works", "delete experiments you no longer need", and the ownership rule (a plugin the operator wrote is theirs — point at the Plugins page instead of rewriting it). **`TestDefaultPromptTeachesPluginAuthoring` pins it** and is the point of the round: the prompt *is* part of the feature, tools the model does not know about are tools it will not use, and dropping those lines would silently remove the capability while every unit test still passed — the test checks the four names, the ABI string, the stdin mention, the ownership line, and that **create appears before run**. `AGENTS.md` gained an **AI plugin authoring** section with the tool/permission table and the two defects this loop has already produced (writing files before `Unregister` deleted the package; refusing a duplicate *after* creating the directory, which then deleted the working plugin), so the next change starts from the failure modes instead of rediscovering them.
- **New: `delete_plugin` — the assistant's plugin lifecycle has an end.** `create_plugin` → `run_plugin` → `update_plugin` covered authoring, checking and iterating, but nothing let the assistant clean up after itself, so its experiments accumulated in the console until an operator removed them by hand. The tool unloads the plugin through `Manager.Unregister` (the same three steps the delete handler performs: unload, drop the record, remove the package directory), so there is one definition of "deleted" in the product. Two guards: it demands **`plugins.delete`**, a permission the console already separates from write and execute, and it refuses any package whose author is not `forgec2-ai` — the installed set is the operator's inventory, and a model cannot see what depends on an entry it did not create; the refusal leaves the record byte-identical. **A bug in a neighbouring tool surfaced while testing it:** `pluginNamesForAI` listed only the **in-memory** plugins, so an unknown-name error said "none registered yet" while the database (and the console) listed several — a record without a loaded package is a normal state after a failed load or a hand-removed directory, so the list now merges both sources, dedupes and caps at 30. **Evidence:** the test creates a plugin, deletes it and requires the plugin to be gone from memory, its record gone and its directory removed; a human-authored plugin is refused with its version untouched; an unknown name returns the error **plus the registered names**; and an empty name is a bad request.
- **New: `update_plugin` — the assistant can iterate on a plugin it wrote instead of deleting and recreating it.** `create_plugin` + `run_plugin` closed the loop only until the first bug: changing one line meant deleting the package, which loses the plugin's id, its reviews, its config and any operator edits to the manifest. The tool rewrites the entry file, bumps the **patch** version (a silent in-place change would leave "1.0.0" describing two different programs), and re-registers. One guard makes it safe: it only touches a package with `Author == "forgec2-ai"` — a model **never** overwrites code it did not write, because it cannot see the intent behind it, and that attempt is refused with a message pointing at the Plugins page. **The first implementation was wrong and the test said so:** `Unregister` is what unloads the old code, but it **also deletes the package directory and the plugin record**, so writing the new files first left `Register` loading a manifest whose files had just been removed — the run afterwards failed with `plugin failed`. The flow is now: read the old files into memory, `Unregister`, recreate the directory with the new entry + manifest, `Register`, and recreate the **record** (Unregister dropped it, so it cannot be an `Update`); any failure restores the backup files and re-registers the previous version. **Evidence:** the test creates a plugin, updates its code, requires `version 1.0.1` and the new description on the record, requires the entry file on disk to hold the new code, and requires **running it to return the new output and not the old one**; a hand-authored plugin (author `alice`) is refused with the record untouched; unknown names, empty and oversized code are refused; and a package whose manifest was deleted cannot be updated.
- **New: `run_plugin` — the assistant can check that the plugin it just wrote actually works.** `create_plugin` (round 249) writes and registers a package but deliberately never runs it, so "does it work" still meant leaving the conversation and finding the Plugins page; making that the operator's job is how a plugin ships broken. The new tool runs a **command** plugin through the same `Manager.ExecuteCommand` the page uses — inheriting its concurrency quota, per-plugin timeout and output caps rather than inventing new ones — and returns `ran`, `success`, `duration_ms`, the output (**capped at 8 KiB with an explicit `truncated` flag**, because a silently clipped result reads as a complete one), the error and the plugin's `data`. It is **not** a shortcut around the gate: running needs **`plugins.execute`**, a separate permission from `plugins.write`, and the call still goes through the approval intent. **Two real defects surfaced while building it.** First, my `create_plugin` tool definition had never made it into the tool list (a failed shell edit), so the model had no way to call the tool that already existed — both definitions are now present and the dispatcher finds them. Second, the run failed with `failed to parse plugin result: invalid character 'h'`: the plugin ABI expects **one JSON object on stdout**, while the tool description said "write your result to stdout", which invites plain text — the ABI is now stated in both tool descriptions, so the assistant writes `{"success":true,"output":"…"}` instead of needing a second round trip to discover it. **Evidence:** the test creates a plugin for the host's own shell, runs it and requires `ran:true`, `success:true` and the marker in the output; an unknown name returns an error **plus the list of registered plugins** so the model can correct itself in one turn; a plugin that exits non-zero is reported as a failure rather than as empty output.
- **New: the assistant can author a plugin and hand it to the product — `create_plugin`.** Until now the AI could only *suggest* plugin code for the operator to paste; it can now write the package (manifest + entry file), have it validated by the same code a manual install goes through, and register it so it appears on the Plugins page. Three boundaries make that safe to expose, all of them enforced in the handler rather than left to the prompt: it needs **`plugins.write` and a human approval intent** (policy: write risk, `GlobalOnly`, because plugins are installation-global); it **never executes** the plugin — the result says so explicitly and running it still needs `plugins.execute` from the page, because generated code is code and code is reviewed before it runs; and it writes through the same checks as an upload (name matches `^[a-z0-9][a-z0-9_-]{1,63}$`, entry cannot contain separators/`..`/be absolute, code is capped at **64 KiB**, `Manifest.Validate` runs **before** anything is written, `Register` rejects duplicates and unresolvable dependencies, and a failed call removes only the directory it created). **One real bug the test found in my own implementation:** the first version refused a duplicate name *after* creating the directory and then ran the cleanup, so a rejected duplicate **deleted the working plugin** — it now refuses before touching the disk. **Evidence:** the test writes a python plugin and re-reads both files, confirms it is registered, enabled and loadable **by a fresh manager from disk**, then requires refusals for `../escape`, an unknown interpreter, empty code, oversized code, a `../evil.py` entry and an invalid type — with `../escape` leaving no directory behind — and requires the original package to survive a duplicate attempt.
- **New: `?bucket=hour|day` on the task statistics — the per-type rows say WHAT is slow, the buckets say WHEN it got slow.** That is the question that follows a regression: someone notices `shell` is slow today and needs to know whether it degraded this morning or last week, and the answer was previously two hand-made exports and a spreadsheet. Buckets are derived from the rows the endpoint **already read**, so they cost no extra query, and each carries `count`, `p50_ms`, `p95_ms`, `max_ms`, `failed` and `success_rate` so "slow" and "failing" stay together. Two deliberate choices: **empty buckets are omitted** rather than emitted as zeros — a zero-width bucket reads as "everything was instant" instead of "nothing ran", and the `start` values make the gaps visible to a caller that plots the series — and the bucket boundary is computed in the **storage zone**, so a `day` bucket begins at local midnight and not at 08:00 (which is what a bare `Truncate(24h)` produces on a `+08:00` host). `?bucket=` is validated (`hour`/`day`, anything else is a 400) and omitted entirely unless asked for. **Evidence:** the test seeds two tasks in one hour (100 ms completed, 300 ms failed) and one in the previous hour (1000 ms), then requires **2 hourly buckets, oldest first**, the newer one with `count 2 / failed 1 / success_rate 0.5 / max 300`; `bucket=day` collapses all three into one bucket; no parameter means **no `buckets` field**; `bucket=week` is a 400.
- **The duration statistics now answer "is it failing" as well as "is it slow".** Each row of `GET /api/stats/tasks/duration` carries `completed`, `failed` and `success_rate` (0–1, three decimals), computed from the same rows the percentiles already read — no second query, no second endpoint. A type that got slower is interesting; a type that **started failing** is the thing an operator needs to see, and asking two endpoints to learn it invites looking at only one of them. Works for both `?group_by=type` and `?group_by=agent`. **The same trap as last round bit again, one round later:** the query projected `type, agent_id, created_at, updated_at`, so `status` arrived as `""` and **every row counted as failed** (`0 completed / 4 failed`) — the test caught it immediately, the projection now includes `status`, and the comment says why every column the aggregation reads has to be there.
- **New: `GET /api/tasks/:taskId/output` — one task's result as a plain text file.** The console renders a task's output, but getting it anywhere else meant copying out of the UI or scripting against the JSON; for the results that matter (a directory listing, a `wevtutil` dump) a file is what an operator actually wants. Served as `text/plain` with `Content-Disposition: task-<id>-<type>.txt`, plus `X-Task-Status` and `X-Task-Final` headers so a reader can tell a finished run from one that may still grow; also available agent-scoped at `/agents/:id/tasks/:taskId/output`. Three deliberate behaviours: a **failed** task still downloads and the file ends with `--- error ---` plus the message (a download that silently omits why it failed is worse than no file), **nothing to download is a 404 with a reason** rather than a zero-byte file, and the lookup reuses the detail route's legacy fallback (pre-tenant rows carry tenant 0) while re-gating on **agent visibility**, so a foreign task is a 404 either way. **Evidence:** the test seeds a completed task, a failed one, a pending one with no output and another tenant's, and requires `text/plain` plus the result body and the `task-<id>-shell.txt` filename for the first, the `--- error ---` block and `X-Task-Final: false` for the second, 404 for the empty, the foreign and the wrong-agent lookups, and 400 for a non-numeric id.
- **`GET /api/stats/tasks/duration` gained `?group_by=agent` — "which beacon is slow" as well as "which command is slow".** Round 242's endpoint only answered the second question; the same machinery answers the first, so the rows are now keyed by task type **or** by agent id, reported under `agents` (never `types`, so a caller cannot mistake one for the other), each row carrying the **hostname** from a single batched lookup so the answer is readable instead of a list of UUIDs. `?group_by=` accepts only `type` (default) and `agent`; anything else is a **400** rather than a silent fallback. **A bug the new test caught immediately:** the query selected `type, created_at, updated_at`, so every agent-keyed row had an empty key and all three seeded tasks collapsed into **one** group — `agent_id` is now selected too. **Evidence:** the test seeds two agents with `web-01`/`dc-02` hostnames and demands **two** rows, slowest first (`b-2`/`dc-02` at p95 2000), the second with `count 2` and its hostname, and `?group_by=tenant` rejected with 400.
- **New: the audit export can be filtered by actor, action, agent and time window — and says when it is.** The export already supported `limit` and `since_id`, but an investigation ("what did this operator do yesterday", "every action against this beacon") had to pull the whole table and grep it. `GET /api/audit/export` now takes `?user=`, `?action=`, `?agent_id=` and `?since=` (either the dashboard's `24h`/`7d` form or an RFC3339 timestamp), still tenant-scoped. **The honesty part matters more than the filters:** a filtered export is a **selection, not a contiguous hash chain** — row N+1's `prev_hash` points at a row the filter dropped — so the response declares it in headers (`X-Audit-Export-Filtered: true`, `X-Audit-Export-Rows`, a `Warning: 199` explaining that verification needs the unfiltered export, and a `-filtered` filename) rather than by injecting a metadata line into the NDJSON, because a verifier must be able to treat **every line as an audit row**. `since_id` stays unfiltered, since it is a contiguous continuation. **A trap this round found:** the timestamps are stored as text carrying the writer's offset (`2026-10-28T13:31:55+08:00`), so binding an absolute UTC instant compared `13:31+08:00` against `04:01+00:00` and returned **every** row — the filter looked applied and did nothing. The bound instant is now normalised to the storage zone (`comparableLocalTime`); duration-based windows were never affected because they derive from `time.Now()`. **Evidence:** the test seeds four audit rows across two actors and two tenants and requires the unfiltered export to be 3 rows with both hashes and **no** warning, `?action=` → 2 rows marked filtered with the discontinuity warning and the `-filtered` filename, `?user=` → 1, `?agent_id=` → 2, `?since=90m` → 1, an RFC3339 `?since=` → 2, `?since=forever` → 400, and `?since_id=1` **not** marked filtered; binding the raw UTC value makes the RFC3339 case fail with `returned 3 rows, want 2`.
- **New: `POST /api/agents/:id/tasks/rerun-failed` — recover from an outage in one action.** `handleRerunTask` covers a single task, but the situation that *produces* failures is the one that produces them by the dozen: a beacon goes offline or a listener dies for an hour and everything queued against it fails at once, so recovery meant clicking each task. The endpoint re-queues an agent's failed tasks newest-first (`?since=` default 24h, `?limit=` default 10 / max 25, `?type=` optional), tenant-scoped, behind the same operator gate as rerun. Three behaviours came out of writing the test, each of which is the difference between a feature and a hazard: **double-click protection** — an unkeyed original now gets a deterministic `rerun:<id>` idempotency key, so a second click returns the live row and the response reports it as `already queued` instead of queuing everything twice; **the limit counts candidates, not control tasks** — unrerunnable types are excluded **in the query**, because a burst of `kill_agent` failures otherwise filled the window and starved the tasks worth rerunning; and **excluded is reported** — a count of control-type failures comes back so the caller is not left wondering why fewer tasks ran than failed. The clone itself moved into `rerunTaskAsNew`, shared by both endpoints, so the no-rerun list and the key carry-over cannot drift. **Evidence:** the test seeds two failed shells, a failed screenshot, a completed task, a failed `kill_agent`, another tenant's failure and one outside the window; it requires **queued 3**, `excluded 1`, fresh `pending` rows carrying the original's command/shell, the originals still `failed`, a second call queuing **0**, and `limit=1` returning `queued 1, truncated true`.
- **New: `GET /api/stats/tasks/duration` — which task types are getting slow.** Nothing in the product answered that from the task history: the dashboard showed counts, the timeline showed events, and a type that had quietly gone from 2 s to 40 s looked identical to a healthy one. The endpoint returns, per task type, **count, mean, p50, p95 and max** over a window (`?since=1h|24h|7d|30d`, default 24h), plus `?type=` to narrow; types are ordered **slowest p95 first**, because the point is to point at the problem. Two deliberate choices are visible in the response: only tasks that reached a **final state** are counted (a pending row has no duration, and averaging it as zero would make every type look faster than it is), and the read is **capped at 5000 rows** with a `truncated` flag — this is a percentile input, not an export, and reading a busy deployment's whole history to answer "how slow is `shell` today" would be a self-inflicted load spike. Tenant-scoped, `tasks.read`, percentiles by **nearest rank** so a single sample reports itself rather than an interpolation. **Evidence:** the test seeds four `shell` samples (100/200/300/1000 ms) and requires p50 200, p95 1000, mean 400, max 1000; a `running` row and a tenant-2 row must be **excluded**; tenant 2 sees only its own 5000 ms sample; `?type=` narrows the answer; `?since=forever` is a 400 and `?since=7d` works.

- Protocol version negotiation with an explicit compatibility window. `pkg/protocol/version.go` now carries the version table (wire version, deprecation, server-compatibility, introducing release) and a `Negotiate(agentVersion, window)` decision instead of the hard `pv == CurrentProtocolVersion` equality: an agent whose version is older but still wire-compatible is served and flagged `deprecated`, an incompatible or too-old agent gets `426 Upgrade Required` on the HTTP beacon endpoint with a JSON reason (the secondary transports — TCP, SMB, WebSocket, DNS, extc2 — keep their framing but now count and audit the refusal instead of dropping the check-in in silence), and a newer agent gets the same 426 with an "upgrade the server" reason - never a silent `200` with an empty task list. `protocol.compat_window` (0-16, default 1) only widens the set of versions marked server-compatible; it can never resurrect a version whose envelope changed. Responses carry `pv_deprecated`, `/api/v1/agents` exposes `protocol_outdated` / `protocol_current`, the dashboard and Settings > About surface the negotiated version, the window and the outdated count, and the beacon list shows a `PVn` warning badge. Rejections are counted (`forgec2_beacon_protocol_rejected_total{reason}`, `forgec2_beacon_protocol_deprecated_total`) and audited once per agent per hour instead of every check-in. Both implants report the refusal once (the Go agent parses the reason, the C implant reads `WINHTTP_QUERY_STATUS_CODE` and keeps its queued results for the next successful check-in, which the server's result id dedup makes idempotent), so an old implant no longer spins silently.
- `scripts/deploy.ps1` (Windows) and `scripts/deploy.sh` (Linux/macOS): one command from a source checkout to a running, verified server. Preflight (toolchain, config, probe port from `server.port`, wildcard bind without `server.allowed_origins` called out) -> build through the canonical `scripts/build-embedded.ps1` -> keep the previous binary as `forgec2-server.prev` (or `forgec2-server.exe.prev` on Windows) -> stop the old instance and wait for the port -> start detached with `logs/stdout.log` / `logs/stderr.log` -> poll `/health` over HTTP and the self-signed HTTPS profile -> probe `/ready` and `/login`. A new instance that does not come up healthy is rolled back to the `.prev` binary and started again, so a bad build never leaves the box down; the log tail is printed either way. Flags: `-SkipChecks`/`--skip-checks`, `-SkipFrontend`/`--skip-frontend`, `-NoStart`/`--no-start`, `-NoRollback`/`--no-rollback`, `-Port`/`--port`, `-HealthTimeoutSeconds`/`--timeout`.
- One-click release archives next to the raw binaries: `forgec2-v<version>-windows-<arch>.zip` and `forgec2-v<version>-linux-<arch>.tar.gz` contain the single embedded-console binary renamed to `forgec2-server[.exe]`, `config.example.yaml`, `LICENSE`, `QUICKSTART.txt` and a launcher (`start.cmd`, CRLF-converted at packaging time; `start.sh`, executable in the tar). The launchers create `config.yaml` on first run, print the console URL and the first-boot credential hint, and (Windows) open the console once the listener is up. Both archives ship a `.sha256` and, when the release key is configured, a detached Ed25519 `.sig` over it, so the hot-update trust anchor covers the bundles too. Signing runs with the runner's host `GOOS`/`GOARCH`: the matrix exports the cross-compile target, and a `go run` that honours it produced a Windows PE that Linux then refused to execute (`exec format error`), which is why keyed tag builds had never succeeded. The release notes now lift the matching CHANGELOG section: they matched `## [v2.7.0]` and an exact line, while the file says `## [2.7.0] - <date>`, so the "Changelog" section had silently been empty; an empty section now fails the job instead of publishing a blank heading.
- `scripts/build-embedded.ps1` gained `-SkipChecks`, `-SkipFrontend` and `-NoRestart`, so the deploy script reuses the one canonical frontend -> `internal/webdist/dist` sync instead of growing a second copy, and backend-only redeploys no longer rebuild the bundle.

### Changed

- **The server binds `0.0.0.0` by default** (`internal/config` default, `scripts/setup-dev.sh`, `config.example.yaml` was already there), so a fresh instance is reachable from another host or a container network without editing the config first. The console stays TLS + authentication gated; `server.host: 127.0.0.1` keeps it local, and the SOCKS relay / remote port forwarding stay on loopback (`server.socks_listen_host`). Because a wildcard bind with an empty `server.allowed_origins` leaves remote browsers refused as cross-origin, the server now logs a startup warning for exactly that combination and `docs/DEPLOY.md` documents the fix.
- `docs/DEPLOY.md` leads with the one-command path (Option 0) and documents the bind default plus the CORS consequence; `AGENTS.md` points at the deploy scripts instead of the manual five-step sequence.

### Added

- Authenticode signing for generated Windows EXE/DLL payloads, exposed as `sign_mode` on the binary API and an **Authenticode Signing** section in the Generate panel (EN + ZH): `self` mints a fresh RSA-2048 code-signing certificate (default organization `Software Publisher`), while `pfx` accepts an operator PKCS#12 bundle as base64 with a 64 KiB cap and an in-request password; PEM certificate/key material is available API-side for automation. Signing runs on the final bytes — after PE finalization, optional UPX and any self-check patch — and validation re-verifies the embedded PKCS#7 signature, image digest, code-signing EKU and checksum. PFX bytes and passwords live only in request memory and are never persisted, logged or audited. `self_check` and signing can now be combined: the self-check hash uses the PE-aware digest (checksum and security directory omitted, certificate table excluded) and the image is padded before signing, so the certificate table does not invalidate the runtime integrity check. `self_check` on DLL output is still refused (os.Executable() resolves to the host process); self-signed builds are labelled as showing Unknown Publisher in Windows.
- Windows build status now classifies the embedded Authenticode signature as `valid`, `untrusted_root`, `invalid` or `unsigned` and returns `signature_signer`, `signature_issuer` and `signature_error` on the build status API, build snapshots and `build_update` WebSocket events. The Generate panel shows the classification next to completed EXE/DLL builds (EN + ZH); a self-signed certificate is shown as **Self-signed / untrusted**, never `valid`, and ZIP/LNK bundles and non-Windows targets are deliberately not inspected. The shared `pkg/peimage` digest also makes the Windows self-check agree between build time and runtime, including after signing, and the runtime fails closed on read, parse, duplicate-placeholder and digest errors.
- WebRTC live remote desktop (M2): the Remote Desktop page now offers **Snapshot** (the existing JPEG stream + `remote_input` tasks) or **Live (WebRTC)**. Live mode is a 1:1 Pion session: the browser sends a recvonly H.264 offer over the authenticated `/ws/rd/operator` socket, the agent answers over `/ws/rd/agent` using a single-use token embedded in the encrypted `screen_webrtc_start` task, and DTLS-SRTP media flows directly or through the embedded Pion TURN relay. The server embeds TURN (`webrtc.enabled`, off by default; `turn_addr`, `public_ip`, relay port range, realm, session TTL and session cap), mints per-session TURN credentials, caps sessions at one per agent and 32 globally, and expires credentials with the session. Media defaults to 720p30 at 2.5 Mbps and accepts 1080p30 at 4.5 Mbps (hard 1920×1080 ceiling, no 4K guarantee); encoders are `libx264` plus optional `h264_nvenc`/`h264_qsv`/`h264_amf`/`h264_videotoolbox`. The agent starts ffmpeg only after the PeerConnection is connected (so SPS/PPS/first IDR survive), captures via gdigrab / x11grab / avfoundation, forwards RTP into a Pion `TrackLocalStaticRTP`, and tears everything down on socket close, stop task, or session expiry. Live input reuses the server's `remoteInputRequest` validation and 40 events/s per operator+agent limiter over the same socket, keeping the authenticated-operator/tenant/audit gates without a task per mouse sample. The agent side lives in the optional `webrtc` module so Pion is excluded from builds that drop it; Win7-compatible builds always exclude it (Pion requires Go 1.24, the Win7 build pins Go 1.20). `docs/REMOTE_DESKTOP.md` carries the transport, TURN, capture-backend, limit and failure-mode contract
- File Intelligence M1a: metadata-only media search for the Beacon file browser. `POST /agents/:id/files/search` queues a new `file_search` core task with category/glob/size/mtime/depth/hidden filters (default 2,000 hits, hard 5,000; 20 s default, 60 s hard; result JSON under 900 KiB), and the agent walks the selected root without opening matching files, returning path/name/category/size/mtime hits newest-first. The Files page gains a **Media** view with category chips, filters, truncation/error counts and **Show in folder**; the operation is documented in `docs/FILE_INTELLIGENCE.md`, and M1c sensitive scans remain a separate batch
- File Intelligence M1c: text-only sensitive-data scan for the Beacon file browser. `POST /agents/:id/files/scan` queues a new `file_scan` core task with an explicit detector allowlist — email, phone and Luhn-validated bank card are enabled by default; US SSN, AWS access key, private-key marker, JWT and conservative password/secret assignments are opt-in. The Go agent walks the selected root under the existing skip/symlink rules, reads only bounded regular text files (default 1 MiB/file, 32 MiB total, 200 files, depth 8, 500 findings, 30 s; hard caps 8 MiB, 64 MiB, 1,000, 2,000, 60 s), skips known binary/container formats, decodes UTF-8 and BOM-marked UTF-16, deduplicates per file/rule and keeps the JSON result under 900 KiB. Raw matched values stay only in the encrypted operator task result; a fail-closed server helper rewrites every finding to `[redacted]` for AI tools/analysis, task callbacks, handover and CSV exports, skips `file_scan` in IOC extraction, and suppresses scan error text in logs; audit details carry only options. The Files page gains a **Sensitive** tab with detector opt-ins, limits, raw-value warning, summary/truncation/error states, copy and **Show in folder**. Contract in `docs/FILE_INTELLIGENCE.md`; M2 WebRTC screen streaming remains a separate batch
- File Intelligence M1b: bounded thumbnails and Range playback for the Media view. `POST /agents/:id/files/thumb` queues a new `file_thumb` core task for up to 12 image/video paths; the Go agent returns per-path metadata plus a JPEG (stdlib decoding for JPEG/PNG/GIF, optional target-side `ffmpeg` for video frames), with 320px default long edge (64-512px), 256 KiB default image budget (512 KiB hard), 64 MiB/40 MP source caps and a 900 KiB result budget. The server validates paths and JPEG magic, writes content-addressed files under `data/thumbs/<agent>/<sha256>.jpg` with a 7-day TTL, and rewrites the encrypted task result to `thumb_id` metadata so no base64 image is stored in task rows; `GET /agents/:id/files/thumb/:thumb` serves the cache through the existing operator/tenant gates. The Media view now defaults to a grid with a list toggle, lazily thumbnails the first 24 image/video hits in batches of 12, and stages audio/video up to 50 MiB through the existing chunked pull flow so playback uses HTTP Range from `/files/exfil`. The contract and limits are in `docs/FILE_INTELLIGENCE.md`; M1c sensitive scans and M2 WebRTC screen streaming remain separate batches
- The Go agent is now built from selectable modules instead of one monolith: the optional command surface is split into a 17-entry catalogue plus the always-on core (`internal/payload/agent_modules.go`), each module owning a `task_registry_<module>.go` and, where compile-safe, its implementation files. Build forms accept a comma-separated `modules` include list (empty = every module, `core` = minimal), the Generate page grows an *Agent modules* picker fed by `GET /api/generate/modules` (the server catalogue, so the UI cannot drift from the generator), and the generator resolves the selection once (`resolveAgentPlan`) into dropped files plus an injected `modules_excluded.go`. Excluded commands leave the build and are answered honestly if one still arrives — `task type "X" is not compiled into this build: agent module "Y" was excluded at generation time` — `help` marks them (`[not in this build: module "Y"]`) and counts them in its header, and `hostinfo` reports the compiled set. Measured (windows/amd64, UPX off): all-modules 31.9 MB, `core`-only 19.8 MB, slim 22.0 MB; the per-module table and the honest limits (shared files keep symbols reachable, so most modules shed command surface plus tens-to-hundreds of KB rather than megabytes) are in `docs/AGENT_MODULES.md`. The legacy slim/light build is now exactly the "`transports_advanced` off" preset instead of a parallel code path, with the two pinned equivalent by test
- A compile gate over every module-off combination: `TestAgentModuleExclusionBuilds` (opt-in via `FORGEC2_AGENT_MODULE_BUILD_TEST=1`, knobs for targets and cases) runs a real `go build` against a staged copy of the agent tree, so an `ExclusiveFiles` claim that breaks a kept file fails a test instead of shipping a broken payload; the catalogue is pinned to the files on disk and to the registries by `TestAgentModuleCatalogIntegrity` / `TestAgentModuleCatalogMatchesRegistry`, and `scripts/e2e-agent-local.ps1` gained `-Modules` (plus honest-exclusion assertions) so the flow is verified end to end, not only at unit level; CI runs a reduced case matrix (7 cases, and an unknown case name fails the test instead of silently running zero)
- AI-friendly plugin authoring, end to end: `cmd/plugin-init` writes a package that is valid by construction (manifest plus an entry template whose comments carry the full runtime contract, per type and interpreter; the WASM tier also gets a freestanding C skeleton and a `build.sh`), and `cmd/plugin-check` validates a package the way the server loads it — manifest, entry, the real digest/Ed25519 trust chain, the WASM ABI surface inspected with the server's own wazero runtime, and with `-run` one actual execution through the server's executor followed by the output-contract check for the plugin's type. Every finding carries a stable code (`wasm.bad_abi`, `trust.failed`, `result.bad_json`, …) and `-json` prints the whole report, so an agent can iterate generate → check → fix without a server round trip; `docs/PLUGIN_AUTHORING.md` and `docs/schemas/plugin-manifest.schema.json` carry the contract in prose and machine-readable form. The checker also probes interpreters instead of trusting `PATH`, which catches the WindowsApps `python.exe` alias (resolves, exits 9009) as `tier.interpreter_broken` instead of letting the plugin fail at run time
- A buildable WASM example plugin ships in `examples/wasm/hello-report`: freestanding C (no libc, one optional host import), manifest and build script, plus the committed `plugin.wasm`; `internal/plugin/wasm_example_test.go` loads that package's real manifest and runs the committed module through the executor on every test run, so the example doubles as an end-to-end ABI test instead of being a snippet that rots
- Live UI e2e in CI: a new `live-e2e` job builds the real server (embedded webdist), boots it on plain HTTP with a seeded admin, and runs `frontend/e2e/live.spec.ts` through `playwright.live.config.ts`, so login, session cookies, CSRF and the agents API are exercised end to end instead of only against the static export
- WASM plugin tier (`interpreter: wasm`, wazero): untrusted plugins run with no WASI (no files, sockets, env or clock), a 16 MiB linear-memory cap, the existing timeout/quota discipline, capped logs and result. ABI v1 is documented in `docs/PLUGIN_TIERS.md` (`run(ptr,len) -> i64` packing the reply pointer and length, plus an optional `forgec2.log`)
- Plugin package trust: manifests may carry a canonical `digest` (`sha256:` over every package file, plus any `includes` shared paths such as `lib/`) and an Ed25519 `signature` over that digest; the server verifies both on load and install, and `plugins.require_signed` turns "unsigned" into a refusal. Generate/verify with the new `cmd/sign-plugin` (`-gen`, `-root`, `-include`, `-verify -key <pubkey>`)
- The 52 bundled plugins are now signed with a project key whose private half was discarded at release, and `config.example.yaml` ships its public key with `require_signed: true` — so an out-of-the-box install refuses unsigned third-party plugins while the bundled set still loads
- Plugin execution quota: `plugins.max_concurrent` (default 4, env `FORGEC2_PLUGINS_MAX_CONCURRENT`) bounds simultaneous plugin processes, so event bursts cannot fan out interpreters
- Plugin CPU budget: `plugins.max_cpu_seconds` (default 120, env `FORGEC2_PLUGINS_MAX_CPU_SECONDS`) kills a native plugin's process tree once it burns that much CPU time, accounted per run across the whole tree (Windows Job Object accounting / Linux process-group `/proc` samples) and reported as an explicit CPU-budget error distinct from the 300s wall-clock timeout; platforms without CPU accounting log once and stay bounded by the timeout, and the WASM tier remains wall-clock bounded only
- Plugin write budget: `plugins.max_write_mb` (default 1024, env `FORGEC2_PLUGINS_MAX_WRITE_MB`) does for bytes what the CPU budget does for time — file, pipe and console writes are summed over the run's process tree (Windows Job Object I/O counters / Linux `wchar` in `/proc/<pid>/io`) and the tree is killed with an explicit write-budget error once the budget is spent, so a runaway writer cannot fill the volume for the rest of its wall-clock window
- The C implant now runs six more tasks with Go-agent output parity: `drives` (tab-separated `Drive`/`Type`/`Free`/`Total` table), `find` (base64 of `path\t size\t mtime` lines, which the server decodes before storing so operators read plain text), `portscan` (TCP `host:port open|closed` lines; `udp` fails closed and `host:port-range` comma lists parse like the Go agent), `clipboard_get`/`clipboard_set` (`CF_UNICODETEXT`; a session without an interactive desktop fails closed with `cannot open the clipboard` / `clipboard set failed`) and `get_sleep_mode` (reports the active interval/jitter, since the C build has no adaptive sleep modes); everything else keeps failing closed with `unsupported in C implant`
- Verification harnesses for the C implant: `cmd/mint-reg-secret` mints a v3 registration secret straight into a server's `reg_secrets` table (reuses `internal/crypto.RegSecretStore` and `internal/db.RegSecret`, prints the `set SECRET_ID=` / `set SECRET_B64=` pair, needs no restart, no operator session), and `scripts/e2e-c-implant-local.ps1` runs the whole loop against a throwaway server — builds it on `127.0.0.1:8100` with a fresh data dir, plain HTTP and generated beacon key/password, mints the secret, cross-builds the implant, runs it with `TEMP`/`TMP` redirected (a developer's `%TEMP%\fc2c.dat` identity is never reused) and drives `scripts/e2e-c-implant.bat` once per task with real result assertions; clipboard tasks pass when they either complete or fail closed, because a service session has no interactive desktop
- CI gains a `c-implant` job: the implant cross-builds with mingw-w64 under `-Wall -Wextra -Werror` (the vendored `sqlite3.c` is compiled separately, without `-Werror`, so its warnings cannot mask ours), each new task string is asserted present in the binary, the `C2_TRANSPORT_DNS` variant is syntax-checked, `x25519_selftest` runs as a host binary, and the shipped WASM example is built with clang and executed through `cmd/plugin-check`, so a compile break, a fresh compiler warning or a WASM regression fails CI instead of surfacing at deployment
- The primary (Go) implant now has a runtime E2E gate in CI: `scripts/e2e-agent-local.ps1` boots a throwaway server on plain HTTP with a fresh data dir, generates a real agent through the operator API (`POST /generate/{linux,exe}`), polls the build job, downloads the artifact and checks its executable magic, runs it with `TEMP`/`HOME`/`XDG_CACHE_HOME`/`LOCALAPPDATA` redirected, and asserts the whole loop — v3 registration, identity refresh (hostname/os/arch/pid/listener backfilled on the beacon after the registration frame) and a four-task matrix (`shell` marker, `read` file content decoded to plain text, `find` `name<TAB>size<TAB>date`, `get_sleep_mode`). `shell` is ops-profile-aware: a host the agent classifies as a sandbox refuses it by design, so the gate asserts that refusal (and that no output leaks) instead of the marker. A red run keeps the DB/config/binaries and tails the server log; a green one deletes them because they carry a server-minted registration secret. The new `agent-e2e` CI job runs the Linux ELF variant
- `docs/TRANSPORT_E2E.md` documents that gate plus the two operator-path traps it encodes: the server process must run with its CWD inside the checkout (the payload generator injects its module `replace` by walking up from the CWD) and artifact downloads must send `Accept: application/json`, or the SPA middleware answers with `index.html` and the "implant" is a 2 KB web page
- Extra `https`/`tls` listeners now carry their own TLS posture: `client_ca_file`, `require_client_cert` and `min_tls_version` on the listener record let one teamserver run a hardened client-certificate termination point next to a lab one instead of the all-or-nothing server-wide setting. The knobs only *strengthen* the server-wide posture — a listener can add a client-certificate requirement, verify against its own client CA, or raise the floor to TLS 1.3, and can never drop the server-wide requirement or go below TLS 1.2 — and the merge rule is pinned by real-handshake tests (valid certificate accepted, missing certificate refused, foreign-CA certificate refused, TLS 1.2 client refused by a 1.3 floor, same policy on a raw `tls://` listener). The listener dialogs expose the fields for https/tls listeners (EN + ZH), the listener detail page renders the resulting posture, and `docs/LISTENER_TLS.md` documents the contract, the fail-closed rules, the health-probe behaviour and the client-certificate path for implants
- Capacity work has a reproducible baseline instead of folklore: `internal/server/beacon_hotpath_bench_test.go` measures the beacon check-in in its three real shapes (idle, claiming a task, carrying a shell result) through the real handler and router, and `docs/CAPACITY.md` records the numbers for one core (≈1,840 idle check-ins/s; ≈1,010 with a result), the derivation from check-in rate to fleet size, and what the figures exclude (disk fsync, concurrency, network). Running the benchmarks also produced the profile that points at the remaining cost — roughly a quarter of the check-in is SQLite's SQL parser and about half of the handler is the per-beacon agent upsert transaction
- The profiling endpoints are now reachable on demand instead of only through a test binary: `server.pprof_addr` (empty by default) starts a loopback-only `/debug/pprof/` listener with its own mux, so the operator API, the SPA and the beacon routes are not reachable on that socket. A non-loopback address is refused at config load and again at bind time (the endpoints have no authentication), the setting is classified as static in the reload status matrix, a port conflict fails the boot synchronously, and `Shutdown` closes the listener
- The payload generator can now embed mTLS client credentials end to end: the binary form accepts `mtls_cert` / `mtls_key` / `mtls_ca` (PEM), the pair is validated at the API boundary, and the material travels as base64 inside the AES-GCM config blob — so the client private key never appears on the `go build` argv and is never written to the database, and a config-over-wire bootstrap build keeps the credentials it needs to reach the initial endpoint. The generate GUI shows the three fields when the mTLS transport is selected (EN + ZH, with the pair rule mirrored inline), and `docs/LISTENER_TLS.md` documents the contract plus the remaining limits (the token stager and the C implant still carry no client credentials)
- The token stager now carries the same transport posture as a direct build. `POST /generate/stager|stager_linux` accept `mtls_cert` / `mtls_key` / `mtls_ca` with the identical boundary validation, the stage-2 beacon receives them through the config blob, and the stage-1 stager compiles them in through a generated `mtls_inject.go` (the client private key never travels on the build argv, and a PEM block needs real newlines that `-X` cannot carry). `TestSmokeTokenStagerMTLSFetch` (CI smoke job) builds a real Linux stager and runs it against a TLS server that requires and verifies client certificates — accepted with the embedded certificate, refused when the server's client CA does not know it, with the stage-2 provably not executed in the refused case — and `docs/LISTENER_TLS.md` records the contract. The lazy token flow (`POST /stager/register`) refuses a client-certificate listener with `400` and the reason, because it persists nothing between register and fetch and a token URL for such a listener could never be served
- The remote-desktop/screen-streaming pipeline has a written contract: `docs/REMOTE_DESKTOP.md` maps both transports (the operator task path that the UI actually uses, and the legacy `/ws/remote-desktop` relay), the three gates every read passes (operator role, tenant scope, `agents.write`), the caps and rates introduced in this batch (8 viewers per agent, 64 globally, 100 monitor registrations, 40 input events/s per operator+agent, 1–60s stream interval, 16–16384px width), the audit events written on open/close/screenshot, and the known limits (input round-trips at beacon cadence, so it is an assist channel rather than a full RDP stack)
- The C implant's recon/delivery surface now covers four more types with Go-agent output parity: `usb_enum` (the Go agent's `=== volume enum (Windows GetDriveType) ===` table byte for byte — letter-mask volume order, lowercase type names, raw bytes, and the leading empty column its row format carries), `usb_drop` (`path` source / `command` destination with the Go agent's one-argument fallback and `hide=1` support, refusing to copy the running implant and refusing a non-removable destination, failing closed with `usb_drop: no removable volume mounted` when there is nothing to copy to), `session_recon` (console session id, foreground window, idle milliseconds, then the `query user`/`qwinsta`/`whoami /upn`/`whoami` sections in the Go agent's layout) and `token_whoami` (thread token first with process-token fallback, `{"whoami":"DOMAIN\\user"}`). The CI string gate asserts all four type names, and the local E2E matrix now runs 12 cases: the four new ones plus two `usb_drop` refusal cases (the implant binary as source, `C:` as destination) that hold on a machine with no removable media — the script sets `security.require_approval: false` in its throwaway config so a high-impact type reaches the implant instead of parking in `pending_approval`
- The generate panel's ghost mode option now explains itself on hover: an info icon opens a tooltip (EN + ZH) carrying the `ghost_protocol.go` contract — the trigger (a sandbox/analysis hit such as VM services, Procmon, Wireshark or a debugger, or an anti-debug trip), the behaviour (one final beacon, then no task polling with the sleep window stretched past 24 h while the in-memory config and session keys survive), how it ends (72 h auto-expiry, or the `ghost_mode_status` / `ghost_mode_exit` tasks) and why it stays opt-in. The trigger is a standalone button inside the row's label, so hover and keyboard focus both open it without toggling the checkbox
- Lint debt is now governed instead of tolerated: the mechanical linters (`unconvert`, `ineffassign`, `govet`, `misspell`) are at zero tree-wide and gated there on both analysis targets (windows — which covers the windows-only agent sources a native linux run would skip — and linux), and the remaining backlog is pinned by a checked-in ceiling (`scripts/lint-backlog.txt`, 968 windows / 634 linux) that `scripts/check-lint-debt.sh` enforces against full uncapped runs; the ceiling may only shrink, and its `--update` mode refuses to raise a number. `gosec`'s G702 is excluded: its taint findings flapped between identical runs on the same tree (5 of the 10 windows findings appeared or not), so they cannot be part of a pinned count — and both debt records are dumped to the CI log (one line per finding) so a count mismatch is diagnosable without a rerun
- Dead-code adjudication (2026-10 batch K) paid the `unused` linter to zero on both analysis targets, shrinking the pinned backlog to 846 windows / 569 linux: every write-only or never-read leftover is gone — the `enableVEHUnhook` and `chameleonEnabled` toggles (struct fields, env parsing and main()-side wiring included), the anti-debug score bookkeeping (the severity actions still fire their side effects), the egress-report cache, `profile_rotate`'s never-consumed `encoding` slot, `config_push`'s slept-through sleep/jitter slots, the Go Ekko sleep path (its `indirectNtDelayExecution` syscall lives on in `sleepmask_windows.go`; the C implant's Ekko is untouched), the whole 10-symbol `envdetect_windows.go`, ten dormant wrapper/stub/registry-orphan symbols from the census (kerb TGT monitor, NTLM relay target getter, SMB discovery, CLT PowerShell, PSS base64 runner, AMSI/ETW registry-bypass wrappers, BOF stubs, PEB `BlockDlls` wrapper and friends), the half-dead server side (`listAll`, `executeToolWithDefaultAgent`, `assignTenantToAgent`, `file_chain` prev/commit, the rportfwd relay's never-started event loop, the batch-pass-through `task` field) and mirror-simple test helpers. The six windows-only adaptive-evasion flags (`useIndirectSyscall`, `useStackSpoofing`, `pebBlockDLLs`, `patchAMSI`, `patchETW`, `enableSleepMask`) move into `edr_strategies_windows.go` behind a `applyAdaptiveEvasion()` seam so the shared `main()` no longer carries windows-only names, and the linux `ApplyStrategy`/EDR stubs stay honest no-ops. The one behavior change is deliberate: `deleteAgentRecord` now wires `cleanupLPortFwdForAgent` after the commit succeeds, so a deleted implant's listeners stop holding sockets until process exit. Trees walked blind before are now readable: service enumeration walks `ENUM_SERVICE_STATUSW` by the Win32 ABI stride instead of a mirror struct with eight fields nobody read, and the sleep-mask kit carries only what the shared build consumes
- Observability gaps wired (2026-10 batch O) and the OpenAPI gate tightened: the internal event bus's two formerly-silent shed paths (ingest queue full, handler workers saturated) now increment `forgec2_event_dropped_total` by reason alongside their warnings — the declared fuse existed, registered and exposed, but nothing ever incremented it, so a wedged event queue looked identical to a healthy one; `TestEventDropsCounted` proves both counters fire from a deliberately saturated bus. `cmd/checkopenapi`'s coverage gate is pinned at its achieved 100% (CI flag `0.95`→`1.00`, tool default `0.15`→`1.0`), and the tool grows a schema-coverage deep lint (`-deep-lint` reports + examples; `-deep-baseline scripts/openapi-debt.txt` enforces): operationId is already at 100% tree-wide, while the request/response-schema depth debt (275/507 operations) is pinned by an only-shrinking ceiling like the lint backlog; CI enforcement of the ceiling is deferred until the in-flight `api/openapi.yaml` work lands so that WIP cannot red the gate. Root-level dev clutter (`.gomodcache/`, `coverage*`, scratch patches, `nul`) is now gitignored instead of nagging `git status`
- Lint debt divide-and-conquer (2026-10 batch L) took the pinned backlog from 846/569 to 306/236 (windows/linux, the windows number keeping its +1 CI headroom): errcheck's mechanical tail is gone via config exclusions with recorded semantics (the `Set*Deadline` family — a deadline failure only means the socket is already dead and the next read/write surfaces it — and `(*syscall.LazyProc).Call`, whose error slot is the lazy-loading machinery, not the call outcome), and about seventy sites were adjudicated point by point — test seeds now fail the test (`t.Fatal`/`b.Fatal`), version-parse Sscanf sites keep their best-effort semantics with explicit `_, _` discards, `bytes.Buffer` writes carry an honest `_ =` (a buffer cannot fail), best-effort teardown paths (`PtraceDetach`/`CloseSend`/`CloseWithError`/`CloseWrite`/job-handle closes) discard with a stated reason, and genuinely-informative failures now log (`plugin` unregister shutdown, scripting API binding, ACME cache writes — whose cache writes also tighten to 0600) or propagate (`stripPEArtifacts` returns the write error; the DLL generator turns it into a build failure). The staticcheck ST100 campaign cleared every non-deprecated finding: ST1003 renames across the injection/BOF/token/TUN/PE-parser sources (~370 sites — the ALL_CAPS syscall/enum constants now read `processAllAccess`-style, `VM`/`ID`/`IP`/`TS`/`HTTP`/`DB` initialisms are canonical, and the two hostinfo wire structs keep their `json:"Id"` tags untouched), ST1005 error strings lower-cased and acronym-fixed, ST1016 receiver consistency, plus the S1000/S1008/S1017/S1031/S1039/SA1012/SA9003/SA4003/SA4006/SA4010/SA6003 small classes and dual-GOOS SA4023 dead-branch suppressions with stated rationale (the linux stub makes the branch statically dead on one side; it can genuinely fail on the other). The gosec taint family (G701/G703/G704) joins the already-recorded G702 exclusion: its sinks — task-named file paths in the agent, `-out` arguments in the CLI generators, operator-driven URL fetches in the cookie proxy — are the product's inputs by design, so ~48 rows of intentional-sink findings dropped off the backlog (the 3 G306 findings the CI ratchet surfaced on this batch's changed lines were fixed for real: ACME cache writes and generator output now 0600). The command reference three-way check (L-3) and the i18n dead-key sweep (L-5) both came back clean — `pkg/protocol` task constants, the win7shim registry and `docs/COMMAND_REFERENCE.md` agree with zero gaps (the +1 constant is the `shell_output` event type, correctly not a task), and all 4719 i18n keys are aligned in both languages — so no changes were needed there; both checks are recorded here as the baseline for future re-runs. Remaining debt is documented in the ceiling file: typed-wrapper errcheck sites (185/146), gosec G306 mode findings (5/5), and the deliberate SA1019 keeps (39/11 — `syscall.Syscall6` migration to `SyscallN` progressed to the loader's two non-placeholder call sites, `grpc.DialContext`, `http2.Transport`)
- RegSecret tenant attribution (2026-10 batch N) closes the landed-agent ownership gap: `db.RegSecret` gains an indexed `TenantID` (0 = legacy/unscoped), every mint path stamps it (`createRegSecret(tenantID)` / `ensureV3RegSecret(beaconKey, tenantID)` wired through the binary form, the stager form, the one-liners and the stage-2 lazy mint, which resolves the tenant from the token creator), and the three registration points scope a fresh agent to its secret tenant — v3 `register` prefers the secret tenant, `enroll` self-heals toward the secret tenant before the default, and a `relay` child inherits its parent row's tenant. `cmd/mint-reg-secret` grows an optional `-tenant <n>` (0 keeps the legacy single-tenant behavior), and `TestRegSecretTenantScopesRegisteredAgent` / `TestRelayChildInheritsParentTenant` pin the two scoping gates end to end over the TCP registration machinery
- Lint tail paid to the floor (2026-10 batch N): SA1019 is zeroed on both analysis targets — the whole `syscall.Syscall{,6,9,12,15}` family is now `syscall.SyscallN` with every argument slot preserved (the nargs literal is dropped, the slot padding stays, so the amd64/arm64/386 stack ABI is bit-identical), `syscall.StringToUTF16Ptr` becomes `windows.UTF16PtrFromString` with the error handled, `grpc.DialContext` becomes `grpc.NewClient` (the beacon gRPC connection now materializes lazily under the per-beacon 30s RPC deadline instead of the legacy 10s eager dial), the deprecated `http2.Transport{AllowHTTP,DialTLS}` pair is replaced by `http.Transport.Protocols` + `SetUnencryptedHTTP2` (net/http's first-class h2c since Go 1.24, in both the agent transport and the server-side h2c test client — the wire format and the listener round-trip tests are unchanged), and the four accept/read loops replace `net.Error.Temporary` with a shared `isTemporaryNetErr` helper (timeout via `errors.As` plus the same transient resource classes).
